NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. THE EDITOR of "The Examiner" invites corre- spondence on all subjects of public interest. LErreas should be brief and pointed. Their object must be to impart or seek information,

_ not abuse. LETTERS must in all cases be accompanied by the name and address of the writer. COMMUNICATIONS received early in the week— except urgent matters—can always be given the best attention.

LORD ROBERTS AND THE NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

Sir,—lt is generally agreed that a National Memorial should be raised to the great soldier -who has just passed away. Various suggestions have been made, amongst them the raising of a special Lord Roberts's Battaliorr of Lord Kitchener's Army. By all means raise this Battalion, but let us not forget that the best National Memorial would be the realisation of the idea to which Lord Roberts devoted the last years of his life. Two years ago, at a crowded meeting in Man- chester, it was my privilege to hear him say: "Arm and prepare to quit yourselves like men, hr the time of your ordeal is at hand." These words made a great impression upon me, as they must have done upon everyone else who heard them. He foresaw, as no other man foresaw, the great emergency which has now come upon us, and pointed out the steps that were necessary to ensure our safety and that of those nearest and dearest to us. The hour of danger has come and has found us without such an Army. It has still to be created—an Army not of menace or to attain conquests, but a Citizen Porce raised in aectlrd with the democratic conditions of the country, created for National Defence. • I venture to suggest that the raising of such an Army would be the finest Memorial that the nation could give to Lord Roberts, and would be more enduring than one either of stone or brass. ' HOLMAN KINGDON. Bank Quay, Warrington. • November 20th.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
SAVE DAYLIGHT.

Sir,—l believe Lord Kitchener has said that the war may last two or three years. If it lasts only another nine months, it is, I submit, most desirable that when the days lengthen, say, after March, we should not be compelled to waste the early morning light, and prolong the general inconvenience produced by darkened streets in the evening. Why should not the Government, by adminis- trative order, give effect to the proposition, for which so much public support has already been secured, that it is desirable that we should spend one hour more in daylight in the ordinary working day. The advantages to the health and happiness of the community have been amply demon- strated and admitted, for 26 out of the 28 Bor- ough Councils of London, and 719 other City Corporations and Town and District Councils, have passed resolutions confirming them. If, however, these powerful considerations should still be insufficient to move the Government, the financial aspect of the proposition should influ- ence them, for it is perfectly certain that the effect of the operation of the principle of a Day- light Saving Act would be to reduce the use of artificial light by every householder throughout the length and breadth of the land. The result would be a total saving to the nation of about £2,500,000 next year, and this saving would in- crease in succeeding years in proportion to the increase of population. Capitalising a minimum of £2,500,000 a year, it may be shown that the financial advantage alone to the country would be equivalent to a gift of about £80,000,000 sterling, and so the financial cost of the war up to the present time could be wiped out if this simple administra- tive Act became permanently adopted. W. WILLETT. Sloane-square, London, S.W., November 14th, 1914.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN SMETHURST AND THE

BRIDGE.

Sir,—" Grateful Citizen" struck a true chord when, in a recent issue of your paper, he at- tempted to draw back the thoughts of our fellow-townsmen to the duties and responsibili- ties that go to make up the comforts of our every-day life, at a time when the very nerve centres of the whole world tingle with the tragic events that are taking place on the battlefields of Europe, _ - . _

The bridge is a conception of the brain of a native of Warrington, who, alas! is gone. The town will know him no more, but he has left behind a landmark that will never be forgotten. This is the greatest municipal enterprise—l do not qualify the expression—that has ever been undertaken in the town, and the man that stands out pre-eminently in connection with the undertaking is Alderman Smethurst, the chair- man of the Bridges Committee. Every word of "Grateful Citizen" is to the point, lie voices the opinions of nine-tenths of the thoughtful people of Warrington when he sug- gests that the Council might mark its apprecia- tion of one of its members by bestowing upon him the Freedom of the Borough. Alderman Smethurst has devoted nearly a quarter of a century to the duties of the Council, and a much longer time on committee, and as president of that admirable institution, "The Old Men and Women's Christmas Treat." Alderman Sme- thurst fought with determined pertinacity against the. Cantilever and other inadequate schemes, and now we hive abridge that is not only pleasing to look at, but is the acme of ex- treme utility and stability. One stands on the bridge and looks up a street that is worthy of any town, and one has a suitable portal to it in this fine and permanent structure. The bronze effigy of that great man, Oliver Cromwell, stands as guardian of the bridge, a fitting emblem of such a masterpiece in engineering. Could he come back with his mail-clad Ironsides and view the change that has taken place, he would exclaim: "Truly this is a people of enterprise." Turn about and look for that death-trap leading into Latchford and you find it has van- ished into oblivion, and the two most charming suburbs of Warrington are safely opened out for the enjoyment of the people. These facts stand out without question, and the call is " Honour to whom honour is due." would fain subscribe myself as a "Grateful Citizen," but as this nuld be a plagiarism, I must be content with signing myself THE MAN IN THE STREET. Warrington, December 2nd, 1914.

MUCH ADO ABOUT LITTLE. Guardians and a Proposal to Spend

FAULTY FLOOR CAUSES ACCIDENTS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding over a good attend- ance of members.

MEMBER AND A "RIGID" COMMITTEE.

It was reported that at a meeting of the House Visiting Committee, the chaplains' report book and the Roman Catholic instructor's book were presented, and the reports were read and approved. It was recommended that the Clerk be requested to write to the Rev. H. Ogden, Non- conformist minister, calling his attention to the fact that no reports had been entered in his report book for two meetings, and that he be asked to present fortn:4lttly reports of his work in the future.

Mr. Mather moved and Mr. Hughes seconded the confirmation of the minutes.

Mr. Billinge : I should— imagine the com- mittee were "rigid" at that meeting. I was sorry not to be present. Miss Broadbent: You cannot be accused of being "rigid," Mr. Billinge. (Laughter.) The Chairman: Those who approve say "Aye," rigid or otherwise. The "Ayes" had it!

DANGEROUS TO THE CHILDREN?

At a meeting of the Padgate Homes Com- mittee, it was reported, the Superintendent was authorised to proceed with the re-flooring of the shed in the boys' playground, on the lines sug- gested by Mr. Brooks. Mr. Legh moved and Mr. Dale seconded the confirmation of the minutes. Mr. Bent moved that the matter be referred back. He had examined the floor of the shed, and found that it was not so bad as he thought. There was only one small portion that required re-laying, .and they had sufficient material by them to do that. The question should be fur- ther considered. Mr. Mather seconded Mr. Bent's motion, and said that with material from the yard at the Workhouse the job could be .done for a few shillings. If they followed out what the com- mittee's resolution intended to cover, the cost would be about £2O. Taking the schools and the state of the country into consideration, they should not go in for too much expense. Could the Clerk tell them what amount he had put in his estimate for repairs at the Cottage Homes for the next six months? The Clerk: I think about 4150.

Timber 50 per cent. Dearer. Mr. Mather: At the present time we are going on with a job to cost £l5O, and we have not got .22.00 or £3OO over and above the ordin- ary expenditure. The Chairman: ..t250. _

Mr. Mather: I think with the A:250 we have in hand it will take all the money which has been estimated. We were told that the cost of timber was 50 per cent. more than in ordinary times. I think we can stay our hands for a while. If we do one side, the question will crop up about the other side, and that will be more expensive. Thousands Spent without Demur. _ _ . _ _

Mr. Brooks said he was sorry to see the Guardians acting like they were over a matter of £2O. At a previous meeting the Guardians had decided that the work should be done, as floor was in such a dangerous condition. There had been all kinds of minor accidents in the shed through boards tripping up. Of course, it was a case of "Stay your hands, whilst the lives and limbs of the children are in danger." They talked a lot about £2O, but w,hen it was a case of buildings running into thousands of pounds nothing was said. At the last meeting of the House Committee, Mr. Mather was the first to propose that certain work should be done at the Workhouse. That involved expense, and if it were necessary at the Workhouse, the pres- ent expenditure was equally necessary at the Homes. There would be a £4 to £5 rebate on the £2O, so that the actual expense would only be about £l5.

Lady Member and Decent-Minded Ratepayers. Miss Broadbent said she could not under- stand anyone opposing the matter. There was not a decent-minded ratepayer who would begrudge the money. A little thing like that proposed would not make any difference to the rate. Mr. Billinge said there would be no harm done by referring the matter back for further con- sideration. Miss Broadbent: The danger is there! Mr. Billinge said he thought they might agree with the dispassionate appeal from the dis- tinguished member from Fearnhead, and make the floor last until the war was over. If the floor stood thg weight of the committee, surely it would support the young people. Mr. McKinley said there was always a lot of talk over small matters, but when it came to a big expenditure, it was passed nem con. In the opinion of the Homes Committee, the work should be done, and the £l5 would be money well spent. It would be a great pity to refer the matter back again.

Easy for the Boys to Break Their Legs. Mr. Dennett : Mr. Brooks referred to several minor accidents. What accidents have there been, and what number? Mr. Brooks: I have not tabulated them. Mr. Mather: I have not heard a report. Mr. Dale said he was surprised that the matter was raised again. It was evident to anyone that the floor was dangerous for the children. It was easy for a boy to break his leg because of the cavities. Mr. Bent: Only in one place! Mr. Dale: We are responsible for the lives and limbs of the children, and I think for a paltry £l5, we ought to do this. Mr. Furness said that in view of the fact that Mr. Bent's idea seemed to be that the work could be done more economically, he agreed that the matter should be referred back for further consideration. On a vote being taken the amendment was carried, and the minutes, as amended, were con- firmed.

• NEW ROMAN CATHOLIC INSTRUCTOR. On the proposition of Mr. Lee, seconded by Mr. Mather, it was decided that, subject to the consent of the Roman Catholic Bishop, the Rey. Father Miller be appointed Roman Catholic in- structor to the Padgate Cottage Homes and Schools at a salary of £lO per annum, together with pew rents to the amount of £lO.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 5, 1914.

NOT BOARDING-HOUSE KEEPERS.

Guardians Prefer Wounded Soldiers to Paupers from Other Unions.

"Would it be possible for you to receive boarders from other Unions?" was the question embodied in a letter from the Local Govern- ment Board Inspector, which was read at a meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday.

The Clerk explained that he had sent, in reply, the following information: "Capacity of the Warrington Workhouse, 624; maximum number expected in any one day, 559; surplus, 65"; adding that a new block, with a capacity of 160, would be ready about February. In his (the Clerk's) opinion it would be better to offer what room they had to the War Office for wounded soldiers. 'Some of the children could be removed and cared, for elsewhere. Mr. Mather: I think that is a very good idea. I suggest that the Chairman of the Board (Mr. A. J. Jolley), the Chairman of the House Com- mittee and the Vice-chairman, along with the officials, be appointed to see if the children could be removed, and what space we could allocitte.

The Chairman: We could give useful aid in that way. Mr. Brooks: The old and infirm might be re- moved as well. They, along with the children, could receive treatment in another part of the institution. Mr. Mather's proposition was carried.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
GUARDIANS' BUS RIDES

Declared illegal by the Auditor.

A MEMBER'S "AUDACITY."

"To 'bus or not to 'bus to the Padgate Homes?" That is the question over which the Warrington Guardians, at their meeting on Wednesday, had a lively discussion. Hitherto members of the committee have travelled to this branch of the local Poor Law institution in style. Orginally one horse was engaged, but exception was taken to this undue call upon the strength of a single animal, and a pair were substituted. So the 'bus has rumbled merrily on until now, when the Auditor has put his foot down and declared the charge on the expenses to be illegal. . _ _

Now the question is raised as to the advisa- bility or otherwise of holding the meetings of the committee at the Warrington Offices. A resolution in favour of this was defeated, so that members will have " to sneak in the Clerk's conveyance" (the charge for which is allowed), or get to the meetings at Padgate the best way they can.

MEETINGS IN WARRINGTON PROItIOSED. Mr. Brooks moved: "That in future the meetings of the Padgate Cottage Homes and Schools Committee be held at the Clerk's Offices, Bewsey Chambers, the dates and times of meet- ing to remain as before," and said that in view of a complaint made to him by a member of the Board, the Auditor decided not to allow any longer the payment of a 'bus to carry members from Warrington to Padgate, as such payment was illegal. The Guardians must, therefore, get to the committee meetings the best way they could, for the train service from Warrington was undoubtedly very awkward to fit in with the meetings at Padgate. It was a pity that the matter had not been looked straight in the face some time ago. Although the committees thSy had had were good, lie was of the opinion that if the resolution he proposed had been acted upon some time ago the committees might have been better, from a business point of view. The Clerk was entitled to a conveyance, but he did not think any member of the Board ought to shield himself behind the hospitality of the Clerk and travel in his conveyance now that the Guardians were not to be allowed one. It would be better to have the meetings at Warrington. Mr. Flanagan seconded the resolution. ARE THE CHILDREN INTERESTED IN MEMBERS' VISITS? .

Mr. Dennett said that the e";Wrise Of the 'bus ought not to be incurred, but he was of the .opinion that if the meetings were held in War- rington half of the members of the committee would not be able to turn up. Mr. Bent said that the gomes would suffer terribly if the committee did not visit them once a fortnight. Mr. Lee said he thought the children looked forward to the committee meetings at the Homes and took a great interest in the com- mittee when they went round. •The3r would feel something short if the committee did not assemble there.

Mr. Dale suggested that the meetings should be held alternately at Warrington and .Padgate. Mr. McKinley said he would support the resolution, although he would suffer the most. Mr. Mather: I cannot understand why: this resolution is proposed at all, unless it is to accommodate the Chairman and other busy men. lam not a busy man; it takes me. all My time to get my little bit ot bread and cheese. (Laughter.) Mr. Billinge : You are looking well on it. (Laughter.) MORE WAYS THAN ONE OF REACHING

===== 0002601_19141205_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
PAD GATE.

Mr. Mather: There are many ways of getting to Padgate. There are the cars and trains. It used to be said that members could get up there for the pure air, which would give them an appetite for tea. (Laughter.) For the sake of the schools, the officials and the children this resolution ought not to be passed. Mr. Billinge : The Board have not been in- formed about the Auditor's remarks on the payment for the 'bus. The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) : It has been mentioned.

Mr. Brooks:, At Padgate. The Clerk explained that the accounts for the past half-year, including the charge for the conveyance, had been agreed to. Mr. Dennett's objection to that item had been made since and the account would be attended to when the next half-yearly accounts came up. WOULD A CHANGE BE DISASTROUS?

..... _ Mr. Billinge characterised Mr. Dennett's action as "audacious." He should have men- tioned the matter to the Guardians or the Homes Committee before he approached the Auditor. That would have been one way of settling the matter. When the new offices were erected it would be more opportune to talk of having the meetings at Warrington. As the present system had been in operation since the schools were built it would be disastrous to make a change. The Chairman said he did not see any prac- tical difficulty in carrying out Mr. Brooks's suggestion. A Rotary Committee might be ap- pointed to keep in touch with the institution.

Mr. Brooks: Mr. Bent raised the question of keeping in touch with the children at the Homes, and it has been said that the children look forward to the visits of the committee. The new order advocates haphazard visits as better than regular ones. When it was known that a committee was "visiting" the children made preparation. Haphazard visits would be better appreciated. On a vote being taken eight declared for the resolution and ten against.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LEGAL POSITION.

The Clerk asked for instructions in regard to the contract for the 'bus. In all probability, if it were used it would be a surcharge against thew. He wanted to know the position from the Board.

The Chairman: The legal position is per- fectly clear. Seeing that a Guardian has ob- jected to the expenditure which is illegal, there is nothing to do but give it up. I move that the practice of sending the 'bus to Padgate be discontinued. Mr. Dennett seconded. Mr. Billinge : I understand the objection is only to the increased amount from ss. to 10s. The Chairman • No no; the whole expense.

Mr. Billinge : Why cannot it be cou,tinued at the old rate?

Mr. Lee: The Clerk has power to have one on his own at ss.

Mr. Billinge : No, I don't mean that. I desire to continue the arrangement as to the 'bus at the old fee.

The Chairman: The Board passed a resolution that in view of the cruelty to the one horse, two should be used. Do you suggest that we should have two horses for the price of one? _ _

Mr. Billinge : Could we not resort to one horse?

The Chairman: You can if you want to be cruel to it.

WHEN THE 'BUS WAS OVERCROWDED. Mr. Billinge : I have no desire to he cruel. The suggestion of cruelty probably came on those occasions when there were too many in the 'bus. .Some could go on the car and some on bicycles. I propose as an amendment that we resort to the old arrangement, with one horse. .

The Chairman: It is not legal. Mr. Billinge : It is best, though. Mr. Bent seconded the amendment and said they all knew it was illegal, but the thing had gone on for such a long time that it had become the custom. There was nothing said about it until his Reverence (the Rev. A. M. Mitchell) came on the committee and had to have two horses to carry him to Padgate. (Laughter.) Mr. Brooks: I don't think that is fair, seeing that the reverend gentleman is not here. Mr. Bent: I am sorry if I have said anything I should not have said about his Reverence, who is not here. The Chairman: You might leave him alone when he is absent. Mr. Bent: You cannot touch him then. COULD "SNEAK" IN THE CLERK'S CONVEYANCE.

Mr. Flanagan pointed out that the Auditor had said he would not allow any expenditure for the 'bus. Mr. Brooks: If it was illegal at 10s. the ille- gality is there at 58. The Clerk is allowed a conveyance. We could sneak in that and shield ourselves behind the Clerk, but that would not be a dignified position to take up. Subsequently Mr. Billinge withdrew his amendment, and the Chairman's resolution was carried.

£3O For Homeless Belgians.

The committee recommended that their action in granting £25 to the Belgian Relief Fund, and goods to the value of something over £5 nett, to the local Home for Belgian Refugees should be approved, and also asked. the author- _ _

ity of the meeting to grant £5 to the funds of the International Co-operative Alliance for helping the wives and children of aliens in this country. These grants were unanimously agreed to. Annual Trip Abandoned. _ _

_ The meeting decided, in view of the present condition of things, to abandon the annual trip. A Rejected Proposal.

A motion proposed by Mr. Sherburn to the effect that the committee be instructed to con- sider the method of the election of members of the Board, with a view to securing more repre- sentative basis of voting, and to report to the next quarterly meeting, was lost by ' a large majority. Generous Treatment of Soldier Servants. Under the heading of "Any other business," a member raised the question as to what is being done for employes of the society who have gone to the front? The President reported that married men were having their earnings made up to the average amount that they had received whilst in the society's service. As regards unmarried men, each case had been considered on its merits and dealt with according to circum- stances. In all cases where men returned to the society a position would be found for them and they would not be allowed to suffer any finan- cial loss through joining the colours. Ile could not promise, because of the business that had to be carried on, that any man's actual job would be kept open for him, but whatever posi- tion he was given his wages would be no less on his return than those he had been receiving prior to going out to fight. A resolution which had been moved regarding this matter was withdrawn after the President's explanation.

When Mark Twain was a young. and strug- gling newspaper writer in San Francisco, alady Of his acquaintance saw him one day with .a cigar boa under his arm, looking in a shop window.

"Mr. Clemens," she said, "I always see you with a cigar box under your arm. I am afraid you are smoking too much." 'lt isn't that," said Mark. "I'm moving again."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR CONTINENTAL FRIENDS AND THE ENGLISH RIVIERA.

Those of our, Continental friends who are acquainted with the French Riviera will be in- terested to visit its English prototype—the Cornish Riviera. Similarly, those of our visit- ors whose.desire is to know as much of England as possible shoald make certain of at least a peep at this piece of typically English England. The climate atothe Cornish resorts is of the kind usually associated with more southern retreats —remarkably equable and mild; the scenery is magnificent, and excursions are possible to de- lightful old-world villages that fortunately never seem to be able to shake off their atmosphere of romance. The air of social gaiety in the coast towns comes as a pleasant contrast. A visit to • the Cornishtßiviera is an essential item in a successful itinerary.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPLAIN AND GUARDIANS

Rev. H. F. S. Collier Sends His Resignation.

PEW RENTS QUESTION.

When there was an outbreak of scarlet fever at the Padgate Cottage Homes recently, the Chaplain, the Rev. H. F. S. Collier, who is now serving as a chaplain in the West Lancashire Division, deemed it advisable for the safety of his own family, and that of his parishioners, to temporarily discontinue his visits. The Warrington Gnardians have discussed the mat- ter at their meetings, and a letter was sent to the Chaplain from that body "expressing the surprise of the Guardians, and the hope that " no further cause for complaint would arise." The Rev. H. F. S. Collier ,has resigned. At a meeting of the Guardians on Wednesday morning it was reported that in compliance with a request from the Padgate Homes Com- mittee the Superintendent had drawn up a statement showing that from the time of his appointment to the date of his leaviqg to take up military duties the Rev. H. F. S. Collier had paid six visits to the Homes. During the same period the Roman Catholic Instructor had also paid six visits.

The Clerk then read the following letter from the Rev. H. F. S. Collier : „ . _

" After carefully thinking over the position of the chaplaincy at the Cottage Homes, I have come to the conclusion that under the circum- stances the only proper course for me to pursue is to tender my resignation of the position. Let this be done at the next meeting, and let my notice take effect from to-day" (November 24th).

Mr. Brooks moved that the resignation be accepted, and Mr. Bent seconded.

THE RESIGNATION REGRETTED.

. . _ Mr. Lee: Can we refuse to accept it? lam sorry this ever happened at all. The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) : The resig- nation has been sent in.

Mr. Bent: I do not think we have any other course open but to accept it. ile has resigned and we must accept it. The Chairman: However we may regret the letter or the circumstances, there is the accom- plished fact.

_ _ Mr. Mather, I take it we cannot get out of this, because if we pass a* resolution that we ask him ,to withdraw his resignation he can please himself. It is very regrettable indeed that all this has happened. If this thing is settled I will give notice of motion. The resignation was accepted. Mr. Mather: I give notice that the resolution Which was passed some time ago with reference to the payment of the chaplain be rescinded. It used to be £lO for the chaplain and £l5 for the use of the church. We should go back to the old system.

PROPOSAL TO DISPENSE WITH A CHAPLAIN PRO. TEM.

The Clerk: It hardly needs notice of motion. When the Rev. H. F. S. Collier's appointment is ended the Guardians are in a position to considei the conditions of the chaplaincy on any terms thought lit. Mr. Mather: That resignation has expired. The Chairman: Yes. _

_ Mr. Mather: My notice of motion will be that for the time being we have no chaplain; that the children go to the church and that the Pew rent be paid until the Board can come to some arrangement. -

Mr. Brooks: I understand that Padgate Church is free, and that we paid no pew rents. There was a resolution passed that the Chap- lain's stipend should be £25 per annum, and no pew rents.

The Chairman: But the Chaplain has re- signed. Mr. Brooks: But it is a free church.

_ The Chairman: This authority is entitled to contribute to the church and call it " a volun- tary contribution to the 'church in which the children worship." Mr. Mather: Anyone can see the notice out- side Padgate Church that pews are free, except those used by the 'children from ,the Cottage Homes.

CHAPLAIN "DID HIS BEST."

TIIE LONDON THEATRES.

Though London is just now shorn of its accus- tomed evening brilliance in the streets, it is by no means without compensations in theatrical attractions for those who care to venture out at night. Amongst the things well worth seeing, by those who enjoy the more serious kind of play, is "The Impossible Woman" at the Hay- market, in which Miss Lillah McCarthy and Mr. Godfrey Tearle figure conspicuously and with artistic talent of quite a new order. Founded on Mrs. Basil de Selincourt's novel, " Tante," it will be gathered, by readers of modern fiction, that there are in the play some strong situations, in which these well-known actors bear, with distinction, the brunt of its burden. At the Royalty Theatre "Milestones" still holds sway over large audiences, an almost unprecedently long run having failed to exhaust the drawing power of this epoch-marking drama. J. M. Barrie's "Little Minister," now being presented at The Duke of York's, affords fine scope for the display of Miss Lohr's splendid histrionic gifts, of which, in this bright comedy, she makes the most charming and .:eductive use in the part of "Lady Babbie," Lord Rintoul's lovely yet wayward daughter. For those who prefer a good hearty laugh in the portrayal of things purely farcical, the revival of" The Glad Eye" at the Strand Theatre will be unre- servedly welcomed. In witnessing it one is assured of the diversion of some two hours' fast and furious fooling, which cannot fail of its beneficial effects on overstrained and jangled nerves. All who are in a position to take ad- vantage of daylight conditions when in town have just now an unusual number of matinee performances of all kinds of pieces amongst which a choice can be made.

A SUNDAY MORNING SCENE.

Two Lads Committed for Trial on Serious Charges.

Frank and Thomas Hughes, the two youthful brothers from Walton Inferior, who stand charged with being concerned together in shooting Wallace Edgar Smith, a game- keeper, with intent to kill him, were, at an Occasional Court, held before Mr. T. Baxter

in Warrington on Tuesday morning, committed to takil their trial at the next Chester Assizes, which are due to open on January Ilth of next year. They had been on remand foi tight days, and made their appearance in the court room in

custody of two dieers. They were defended by Mr. T. S. Steel, whilst Supt. Owen had charge of the prosecution.

THE GAMEKEEPER'S STORY.

The first witness called was the gamekeeper. He was allowed to give his evidence sitting, and it was noticed that his left hand was heavily bandaged. He gave his age as 18, and said that he resided at the keeper's cottage, Walton Superior, and was a gamekeeper employed by Sir Gilbert Greenell, of Walton Hall. At about 7.10 on the morning of Sunday, November 22nd, he was in Walton Hall Wood, when he saw the Prisoners. The elder of them, Frank, who is also lame, was carrying a gun. Witness hid behind a bush, and as the prisoners were pass- ing his hiding place he came out and closed with Frank. He knocked the gun up three times, and Frank then asked him to stop, which he did. Frank then backed away some tlvelve or thir- teen yards, and the other prisoner called out, "Shoot him, and come on!" Frank immedi- ately put his gun to his shoulder.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
Shot in the Chest and Arm.

Fearing that he was going to fire, witness raised his left arm to protect himself. The trigger was pulled and witness received the Charge in his left breast and left hand. Witness ran across a field to try to intercept the pris- oners—who made off immediately after the shot Was fired—and when they saw him near the estate office they turned into a lane. It was light at the time and witness could see the men

quite plainly. The gun, which the police subse- quently took from the prisoners' house, was similar to the one Frank was carrying. After seeing the prisoners make off down the lane, wit- ness went home and made a statement to his

father, Who, after examining him, took him down to Dr. Howard's surgery. Witness had known the prisoners both by sight and by name for the last three years. He was still under Dr. lloward's care.

, Asked whether he wished to cross-examine, Mr. Steel said that he felt that there was noth- ing to be gained by entering into lengthy ques- tioning at that stage of the proceedings, but the fact that he refrained from cross-examining the Witnesses must not be taken as admitting the accuracy of the evidence of the prosecution.

WOUNDED LAD'S FATHER'S STORY. Predk. Oliver Smith, head gamekeeper to Sir Gilbert Greenall, said that at about eight o'clock Ott the Sunday morning in question his son, Wal- lace, came home, and in consequence of what he said, witness examined him aund found that he had been shot in the left side of the chest. Wit- hese lost no time in getting him to Dr. Howard's surgery. Subsequently witness examined his suh's clothing, and five small pellets dropped from his waistcoat. He handed them, together With his lad's clothing, to the police.

PASSED THE PRISONERS ON THE ROAD. William Probyn, a coachman, residing at Stockton Heath, deposed that at about 7.30 on the morning of Sunday, the 22nd ult., he was Roing to his work, along Whitefield-road, when he saw the prisoners, who were walking at a rather fast pace in the direction of Stockton Iteath. Frank bade him "Good morning." Answering Supt. Owen, witness said that he had known the prisoners for the last eight or ten Years, being on nodding terms with the elder of the twain. It was light at the time, and he had 40 doubt as to the identity of the youths he saw. harry Dunbobbin, a team-owner, said between 7t,and 7.30 on the morning of the 22nd ult. he met 4e prisoners hurrying to their homes, which as next door but one to his. Thomas said, Good morning, Harry !" and witness replied Good morning, Tom !"

===== 0002601_19141205_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
GUNSMITH'S EVIDENCE.

Albert io-M-D—ain-teth- a gunsmith, —.of Bridge. Street, Warrington, said that on Monday, Nov- eflTher 23rd, P.S. White handed him a gun and e number of shots. The shots were size No. 5 21' No. 6, and, the gun, in witness's opinion, had ",een fired recently and had not been cleaned. 4t was a 4.10 collector, and the shots were such 48 would be used in a gun of that make. Answering the Superintendent, witness said that the gun would kill small game at about tw.enty yards. He had tested a similar gun in 4 Similar case.

bOCTOR FINDS THIRTY SMALL WOUNDS. br. Howard, of Stockton Heath, stated that tit about 8.45 on the morning of November 12111 d, Smith was brought to him by his father. e examined the lad, and found him suffering fir.ein about thirty small wounds, probably in- eted by small shot. Witness attended to the 'Younds and sent the injured lad home. The following day witness located and removed a Nall shot from Smith's chest. upt. Owen: What about the lad's condition? as it serious at any time?—Witness: He did t.ot bleed a great deal, but all the wounds were heding slightly. The shots did not seem to ve hit him in a very vital place, but struck just near the shoulder, where one's cloth- is generally thick. As a matter of fact, the ;tett% was wearing thick clothing. Making an Excellent Recovery. Within what radius did you find these pellet (kudsF—Within a radius of about nine inches.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
PRISONERS MAKE NO REPLY WHEN CHARGED.

Inspector Breese, stationed at Stockton Heath, deposed to going to the prisoners' house at about 9,30 on the morning of November 22nd. He saw the lads in the kitchen, apparently dry- ing their trousers. Frank asked what was—the matter, and he told them, adding that they would have to go to the Police Station with him. Both 'said that they had not been out of the house that morning. He took them to the lock-up. Noticing that Frank had a scratched finger, witness questioned him about it, and he said he did it opening an apple barrel. His stockings were wet, as were also the bottoms of Thomas' trouserslegs. Thomas said they must have got wet.when he was carrying water to the horses. When charged with attempted murder neither of the youths replied. Witness took a gun from 'a rack in prisoners' house. Judging from the state of the barrel, it appeared to have been recently used.

Acting-Sergeant Henry White, also stationed at Stockton Heath, who was in company with the( previous witness when the arrests were made, said that shortly after he got into the kitchen of prisoners' cottage, their father came downstairs and asked what was the matter. On being told, he asked the prisoners whether they had been out of the house that morning, and they answered "No." Subsequently, however, Frank said that he had been out for some hay. Witness examined the gun and found the inside of the barrel dirty and smelling strongly of gunpowder.

"I CAN'T HELP IT! IT'S DONE NOW!"

P.C. James Crompton, of Stockton Ileath, deposed that whilst he was conveying the pris- oner Frank to Manchester he said, "Do you think I shall get off with a fine?" Witness re- plied that he could not say, and cautioned the lad that any statement he made might be used in evidence against him. The prisoner then said, " I can't help it! It's done now! I didn't know what to do, so I thought the best way out of it was to shoot him, when my brother said 'Let him have it,' and was running away, and leaving me to it."

This concluded the evidence for the prosecu- tion.

The magistrate then announced that prisoners would be sent for trial—Frank for shooting at the gamekeeper, and Thomas for procuring and commanding his brother to commit the felony— whereupon they both entered pleas of not guilty, adding that they reserved their defences.

BAIL ASKED FOR AND REFUSED. -

Mr. Steel applied for bail, pointing out that both the prisoners were really only boys. The youngest, Thomas, was 15, and the eldest but 18. Between now and the date of the Assizes came that period when everyone liked to gather round the family table, viz., Christmastide. It would be extremely nice if those two lads could be allowed to spend their Christmas at home. The eldest boy had established a little business and had a horse and cart of his own. In consequence of his having been in prison during the past eight days that business had suffered considerably, and it would be very helpful to his family if he could be allowed out on bail to attend to that business and to arrange as to its future. The younger boy had been in the employ of a Mr. Burrows, blacksmith. His master gave him a very good character as a worker, and was prepared to take him back again. Of course, the charge was a very serious one, but the Court had heard from the doctor that he did not anticipate any dangerous sequel to the alleged shooting, and that the injured man was making rapid recovery.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
YOUNGER PRISONER'S THREAT TO ESCAPE.

Supt. Owen said that, having regard to the fact that a deliberate attempt was made on the life of the young keeper, he objected to bail. During the remand the younger prisoner had been in the Tarvin Workhouse, and only yes- terday the Clerk to the Guardians of the (Tnion to which the workhouse belonged called at the Chief Constable's office to say that the Guard- ians could not accept any responsibility in regard to his detention. He had threatened to make his escape at the first opportunity, and that had necessitated the employment of a man to watch him day and night at a cost of 535. a week.

Mr. Steel: Here's a boy of fifteenr7who has never been away from home in his life, and what could be more natural than that he should want to get back. Surely the desire of a boy to get home again is no great offence. It is most natural that he should say If you won't let me go home I shall run away there the first chance I get." Surely your Worship will not say that there is anything serious in that! After consultation with the magistrates' clerk the magistrate said, "I have decided that I cannot allow these lads out on bail."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
Tragedy of the Fog.

_ In the thick fog on Saturday, November 21st, Sarah Ann Fast (70), of no fixed abode, was knocked down by a motor car driven by Mr. J. A. Ransome, of Hill Cliffe, near Messrs. Cros- field's Works.—Coroner Brighouse held the in- quest on Thursday, when Mr. Ransome, manag- ing director of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Company, said he was travelling slowly on account of the fog. A black object struck the right-hand side of the mudguard and there was a crash of breaking glass. He found that the car had collided with the deceased. An ambulance man from Crosfield's attended her, and she was subsequently taken to the Infir- mary, where she died on Monday.—" Accidental death" was the verdict returned, and Mr. Ran- some was exonerated from blame.

defence and commerce, and federated, in the fullest sense of the word, what would it matter about alliances? There would be no need to trouble abou`t ententes; in fact, they need not trouble themselves very seriously if all the countries of the world were against them. Thus federated, and by pursuing the path of justice, liberty and peace, the British Empire might by herself, off her own bat, practically secure throughout the world those principles of which they thought so much and in defence of which they were spilling the pest blood of their coun- try to-day.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR-AND AFTER !

A Federated Empire and One-Party

Government.

ALDERMAN BENNETT'S VISION.

"The War—and' After !" was the title of a thoughtful and striking address which Alder- man Bennett delivered before the members of the Philomathic Society, at their room in Trinity Chambers, on Wednesday evening, Mr. N. Nield presiding over a fair attendance, who had braved the elements.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR FROM THE GERMAN VIEW- POINT.

In the course of his remarks the ALDERMAN emphasised the fact that in considering the war they needed to get down to bed-rock and consider the causes which had led to a struggle that was unparalleled in the annals of the world. There ,were many things about Ger- many which had greatly impressed him, and in some respects he had felt that they had a great deal to learn from that country, and felt so still. Peeling like, that about Germany, one naturally tried to look at the question from the German point of view in order to ascertain whether there was any element of reason in her ideal and in the policy to which she had com- mitted 'herself. Adopting that view-point, he thought they must recognise that there was sa deracial SCOW of reasoning in Germany- s contention.v lore was a great nation that nad made. rapiu and tremendous strides; a nation that nad felt her growing strength and was proud of it, and, naturally, as she had grown stronger and greater her ambitions had ex- panded in the same latic. Aer population was enormous and prolific, and in many ways it had proved itself capable, enterprising and energetic. Granted all that, and looking at the matter from the German standpoint, it was not unnatural that having" done so much the nation should desire to do more; should, in fact, dream n of becoming one of the world's master Empires. She felt the need of elbow- room; felt that, by some method or other, she must endeavour to find opportunities for that expansion of which she felt herself capable. The ambition was not an unnatural one, and, in a sense, did the nation credit, for it must not be forgotten that the patriots of every land desired to see their country great and prosperous.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN AMBITION NOT IGNOBLE.

It was not an ignoble ambition on the part of a people to desire that their country should be a dominant power and should impress its civil- isation on other countries. But, from the first, there were difficulties in the way of realising that ambition. One sine qua non, if it was to be realised, was the possession of colonies, and, unfortunately for German ambition, all the best places in the world for colonies had already been bespoken. Another drawback was that she had a very limited seaboard on the North Sea, and one could readily understand her thinking how very nice and useful it would be if Denmark, Holland, Belgium, and part, at least, of France, with their extensive seaboards, were welded into her Empire. On sentimental grounds, too, one could understand her desire to own the mouth of the Rhine, the river which inspired her national song. Still, however natural one's desires and legitimate one's appetite might be, it was not permissible, in a world governed by something like a moral law, to gratify them by unfair and unreasonable methods. And it was in imagining that it was permissible that Ger- many had made her great mistake. A policy of peaceful permeation might have given her some kind of control over Denmark, Holland,

and Belgium; they might have been able to in- clude them in the German Federation by a method that was honest and honourable and had the sanction of the people concerned.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INFLUENCE OF A DAMNABLE PHILOSOPHY.

Of course, the people in England might have said that it was a danger to them and affected their interests, but from the German point of view, and from the broad, general view-point, assuming the nations concerned had not ob- jected, there could have been no insuperabla, objection, whilst, looking at it, again, through Teuton spectacles, the German Empire would have been naturally rounded off by the inclu- sion of the countries he had named, and, more- over, each of them would have brought With them into the coalition considerable advantage to the federal empire in the shape of colonial expansion. But Germany had not adopted peaceful means of gratifying her ambition, but, instead, inspired by a philosophy which was I absolutely damnable—a philosophy which, was fatal to all religious and moral ideas and ideals; a philosophy which held that a strong nation was justified in doing whatever it liked with the weaker nations in order to gain its ends— she had embroiled nearly all Europe ire the present terrible conflict. lo the strong," said this philosophy which held her in thrall, •'all things are permissible"; to sum it up, her philosophic-creed was "Might is right!" Ger- many had tried to make a philosophy of her own which would cover sheer depredation and justify her in stooping to actions that were de- grading to a civilised dower.

THE BLINDNESS OF THE BRITISHER. The speaker went on to refer to the singular blindness of the majority of .the people of this ,country who could not, or would not, see whither German policy was trending—they had, he said, been foolishly and stupidly blind to the obvious warnings, such as the notorious telegram to Kruger, the Bosnia-Herzegovina and Agadir incidents, the contemptuous Manner in which Mr. Churchill's proposal for a truce in armaments was received, &c.—and raid that all the time this country refused to pay heed to prophets like the late Lord Roberts, Mr. Robert Blatchford, and, to mention a iooal gentleman, Captain Crosfleld, all the time the British people had displayed a strange apathy and had con- tinued their pursuit of pleasure, Germany had been quietly perfecting her plans in a way which the world was only now beginning to properly realise. The speaker went to deal with the diplomatic negotiations which preceded the I day when "Armageddon burst out in all its unholy flames of world-fury," ani asserted that at the back of the Kaiser's mind, in all lie had done, was an envy of England and a desire to aggrandise himself at her expense—the England whom he conceived to be so feeble and flabby as to be afraid to fight. lf the origin of the war did not justify to the hilt England's entrance into it, the methods that were being adopted by the enemy most certainly did, for they were contrary to all civilised usage; there was nothing knightly about them, and they were a disgrace to the traditions of warfare.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
"NEVER HAVE ENGLISH LADS FOUGHT MORE BRAVELY."

"We knew the English Inds were trave," the Alderman continued, "but never in the finest battles of the past, never in our meat golden' days have they fought more bravely or suffer3d more splendidly. I feel like taking my hat off to every 'Tommy' I meet in the street." (Ap- plause.) It was, he added, the duty of every Englishman to see whether he could not do something for his country et the present junc- ture, for the whole future of the Empire and of civilisation depended upon our being victorious in the present conflict. Never in the history of the world had a nation fought with a (dearer conviction that the principles for whi2,l it strove and bled were right and justified in the eyes et God and man. The Allies were bound to win. He did not want to be vindictive, but there must be no "calling it a draw," or laying down of the sword until they had achieved the things they set out to achieve, or it would be a fatal mistake. Out of the present horror and con- fusion they might hope for something like a durable peace; they would like to think that this was the war which ended war, and, if the people of the civilised nations of the worl.l were wise, it would be.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR EMPIRE, FEDERATED, COULD FACE

THE WORLD. Alderman Bennett went on to deal with the proposals put before the public by the Union of Democratic Control with a view to rendering a great European war impossible in the future, and submitted that if England really made the best of her own opportunities, if she really organised her Empire on sound and sensible, thorough and far-reaching lines; if she really were true to herself and to the ideals which had inspired so many of her statesmen and dreamers, she would be able to stand alone. The British Empire already covered a quarter of the globe, and if she were organised for

===== 0002601_19141205_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW ENGLAND WHEN THE WAR IS

OVER!

By carrying out such a policy of Imperial Federation they would again be in a position to revert to the principle of "splendid isolation." He thought they would have a new England when the war was over. They had been pleased beyond measure to find that the fine old spirit of their land was not dead; that men of all shades of opinion had shelved their differences, and now stood back to back in face of the common foe. bA new spirit had breathed through the life of England, or, rather, the old spirit, which some had feared had begun to decay, had re-asserted itself. They had found that she was not an England of putty, brass and paint, but a real England, after all! (Ap- plause.) WAR AS THE PARTY SYSTEM'S DEATH-

===== 0002601_19141205_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
KNELL.

Mr. FORSHAW proposed a vote of thanks- to Alderman Bennett for his "extremely clever and practical address." Mr. BAMFORTH seconded, and after one or two other members had supported, the proposition was carried with applause, and in acknowledg- ment

- Alderman BENNEIT took the opportunity saying a few words with regard to the present political truce: He hoped and prayed, he sad, that when the war ss, as over the r:onntry wcqld not go back to the cid bitterness of par+v poli- tics, which had disgraced and disfigured the country in the past. If in the time of a great national emergency they could drop their Toryism, Liberalism and Labourism and work together for a common end, why, in the name of Fortune, could they not do the same in itimes of peace? The time Would come—and this. war might have the effect of hastening it—when party politics would be abolished and there would be only one party in the country, and that a party which placed the welfare of the country as a whole above every other consider- ation. It was not impossible!

CHRISTMAS BOXES FOR THE POCR.

Runcorn Guardians Vote Extra Out-Relief.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presiding. Yuletide " Extras" That Will Be Doubly

Welcome. The Clerk to the Warrington Union wrote stattrig the Guardians had decided to grant extra relief during Christmas Week as follows: Adults, Is. Gd. each; children, 9d. each. They asked the Guardians to grant a similar amount of extra relief to the cases chargeable to their Union resident in the Runcorn district.

The Bucklow Guardians wrote announcing their intention of making an extra allowance of Is. to all widows and deserted women with children) chargeable to then, and to other women with children, and to all old. people on their books as being in receipt of outdoor relief, sixpence extra. _ _

On the proposition of Mr. Gibbs, seconded by Mr. Mclntyre, the Guardians decided to act according to the letters. .

At a later stage Mr. Hallows moved that the usual extra out-relief of Is. or adults and 6d. for children be granted by the Guardians. Mr. Britch seconded, and the resolution was heartily carried. Preparing for the Children's Christmas.

The Children's Home Committee's minutes, which were confirmed, contained the following items: "That the usual Christmas allowances be granted," and "that the question of buying, toys, &c., for the children be deferred until the next meeting." Clara Butt's" Kind Offer.

The Clerk read a letter stating that with the express approval and sympathy of Her Majesty the Queen, Madame Clara Butt and Mr. K. Rumford were devoting the proceeds of twenty concerts which they were giving before Christ- mas to a fund for providing employment for necessitous members of the musical profession who were so badly affected by the war. Madame Clara Butt was arranging to send touring parties to visit workhouses and asylums, and she would be glad to know if the Runcorn Guardians would care for a party to visit the Workhouse. No fees were expected, and the tour would take place between December 7th and 19th, and January 4th and 16th. The Guardians decided to allow a concert, the arrangements for the, same being left in the hands of the Master. ,

Mr. Hallows pointed out that the concert would brighten the inmates up.

KILLED BY RED CROSS CAR.

Young Child Run Over in Knutaford Road.

Mr. S. Brighouse conducted an inquest at Warrington on Thursday evening on the body of Arthur Griffiths, aged one year and eight months, 81, linutsford-road, who was knocked down and killed in linutsford-road on Tuesday evening by a motor car which had been con- veying wounded soldiers to Warrington for the British Red Cross Society, and which was re- turning to Manchester.

The mother said that the child must have gone out when she went in the yard to hang out some clothes.

Fredk. William Patch, chauffeur in charge of the car, said that the accident occurred near to where the road was undergoing repair. He did not see the child until he was within two or three feet of it. The car passed over the child, and the body was recovered from under the back axle. s

Detective-Sergeant Holland said that the re- sult of his inquiries were in favour of the driver.

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and exonerated the driver from blame. RED CROSS SOCIETY'S PRACTICAL SYMPATHY.

Mr. James Chapman, of Manchester, for the Red Cross Society, said that the car was per- forming an errand of mercy, and the Society desired to tender their deepest sympathy with the parents. They offered .210 towards the funeral expenses. The Society had carried be- tween 10,000 and 12,000 wounded soldiers in motor cars, and that was the first accident which had occurred.

The jury expressed their sympathy witu the parents and their appreciation of the Somety s offer.

NURSING ASSOCIATION.

Over 16,000 Visits to the Sick

Last Year.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR AND THE FUNDS.

Working men of Warrington are always to the fore in helping any and every institution which has for its objects the alleviation of suf- fering or the bringing of happiness into tbe home. Therefore it is not surprising that the Warrington and District Nursing Association receives an enormous amount of support from this section of the community, and at the annual meeting of the Working Men's Com- mittee of the association, held in the Town Hall on Saturday afternoon, there were many evidences of active interest in an institution that does so much for the benefit of all classes.

CONFIDENCE IN WORKERS' GENEROSITY

_ Mr. F. W. Monks presided over a large attendance, and letters, of apology for non- attendance were read from Mr. W. P. Rylands (president of the committee), Councillor H. Roberts (hon. treasurer of the association). and Mr. J. A. Ransome. Mr. Rylands explained that an important business engagement pre- vented his attendance. ,There was no small danger, the letter continued, lest the heavy calls made upon their sympathies arising from the- war might injure, to some extent, existing charitable efiorts. He hoped that that wc-tik not crave to be the case with the Nursing Asso- ciation, which ciid such 9. splendid and alil":.!!i• needed work. fie was confident, in spite ot the heavy sacrifices the working men had to face, they would not relax their efforts to main- tain their contributions to the high level of recent years. Councillor Roberts stated in his letter that lie was suffering from a cold and had to stay in the house. He thanked the committee, officers and members for the splendid support they continued to give to the association in contributing so large a proportion to the total income of the association.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREASE IN SUBSCRIPTIONS.

In presenting the financial statement, Mr. Hon, the honorary treasurer, said that it was very pleasing and gratifying to note the increase in the subscriptions from the works. There were six new subscribers, and six of the lowest had increased their contributions. Appeals which had been sent out to Approved Societies had met with 'good results, and the subscrip- tions from Friendly, Societies had been almost doubled. Various special efforts had resulted

in £167 7s. 3d. being raised, and towards the new year they had received £lOO from the Old English Fair Committee, and £l5 from the Works' Bowling and Caledonian Associations. The total income was £3Ol 16s. 11d., and, after expenses had been paid, there was £297 Bs. 10d. to be handed over from that committee to the association, as compared with £2ll. 4s. 4d. for the previous year. The services of the nurses were in great demand. They had made 16,226 visits and had given their services to 773 patients during the year. in one month alone 1,578 sick visits were paid. A suns of £6OO or £7OO was needed annually for the upkeep of the association, and nearly the whole of that had to be raised by voluntary effort. The CHAIRMAN moved the adoption of the re- port and proposed that a special letter of thanks be sent to the promoters of special efforts.

Mr. G. WALKER seconded, and the proposition was heartily carried.

ELECTIONS.

Mr. W. KILDITIT moved the re-election of Mr. Rylands as president, and spoke of the earnest manner in which that gentleman applied him- self to any work with which he was assoriated. He believed that the association was so highly appreciated in Warrington that it Toni(' not suffer very much through the war. Mr. W. DAINTITII seconded, and the resolution was carried. The following gentlemen were elected vice- presidents: The Mayor, Aldermen Sme- thurst and Burton, Captain Crosfield, Council- lor H. Roberts, and Messrs. P. W. Monks, W. Carson, W. Milner, C. E. Bunch, T. W. Fletcher, T. Atherton, J. A. Ransome, R. Chad- wick, W. Murray, and 0. Lofthouse.

AUSTIN MOTORS.

Large Orders From The Allies.

The Austin Motor Co. (1914), Ltd., inform us that they have been successful in securing some large contracts from the Allied Governments. We understand that tenders for motor vehicles, Sze., have been accepted ,from amongst many other competitors. This seems to point to the fact that the supremacy of the Austin in design and manufacture has again been the cause of its success. One would accept such proof as a safer indication of the worth of any vehicle than if it formed part of a number promiscuously commandeered. _ _ .

The orders referred to, coupled with the satis- factory reports concerning the behaviour of the lorries now in use by the Allied Forces, confirm the appreciation already expressed by private owners as to the superiority of the vehicles constructed by the Company. The factory has in constant employment nearly 2,500 men, and the works are running without cessation. In spite of urgent demands of the Governments concerned, we are informed that the company have facilities for dealing with orders from their civilian clientele, for pleasure vehicles, as well as commercial vehicles.

IN THE COURTS.

BAD BOYS BIRCHED.

Pledged Vest With Pawnbroker From Whom They Stole It.

Two boys—one twelve years of age and the other thirteen—were, before a special Children's Court at Warrington on Wednesday morning, charged with stealing a man's overcolt, two men's vests, and Is. 6d. in money, the property of Messrs. Briggs, pawnbrokers, of Powys-street, Warrington. Chief Superintendent Williamson told the Court that the boys had been in the habit of taking articles to the pawnshop for their mothers. On Saturday night they called at, Messrs. Briggs' shop and one of them pledged a bundle. Shortly afterwards they endeavoured to pledge a vest at another pawnshop. The manager at that shop, however, was not satis- fied with the explanation they gave as to the ownership of the vest, and sent for the police. Sergeant Summers and P.C. Anderson inter- viewed the boys, who admitted stealing the vest. Further inquiries showed that they had pre- viously stolen the other vest mentioned in the charge and had actually pledged it at the shop from which they took it. The overcoat was stolen about three weeks ago and was sold to another pawnbroker for Is., one of the boys stating that it belonged to his father. The stolen money was change that had been placed on the counter for a customer to pick up and was " lifted" whilst the customer's back was turned. One of the boys told the magistrates that they stole the things in order to get money to go to picture palaces. The Director of Education said that his com- mittee had had a lot of trouble with one of the boys through the neglect of the parents, and everything pointed to that lad having led the other astray. _ - - - - -

One of the boys was ordered six strokes of the birch-rod and the other four strokes.

says a Warrington Woman.

Presentation to Mr. H. Stirrup.

The members of the Fairfield and Howley Company of the Preparatory Training Corps having experienced the patience and aptitude of Mr. H. Stirrup as a sergeant-instructor, marked their appreciation on the occasion of his trans- fer to the Stockton Heath Company, and Mr. George Beckett (Howley.), in presenting him with a "smoker's companion," hit off the situation ' to a nicety. Sergeant Stirrup suitably re- sponded, and the interesting event took place without interference with the Battalion parade at the Cricket Field on Saturday.

Musketry. • At the L. and N.-W. Railway Company's Rifle Range on Tuesday evening, the Fairfield and Howley Company made useful practice, the best cards being returned by the following: 1, T. Newton, Fairfield No. 2 Section; 2, S. Williams, Howley No. 4; 3, 11. Gaunt, Fairfield No. 1; 4, A. Bailie, Fairfield No. 3.

The result was very close, and Newton, by • consistent effort, took the honours by the nar- rowest possible margin on a total for the best five shots. E. Cawley (Howley No. 4) had two centres, yet obtained only eighth place in the company firing.

The Disturbing Telephone.

". The telephone has destroyed all the privacy of society," said the society girl. "It breaks in on everything. Nothing is sacred to it. You may be saying your prayer. The telephone. Or in the midst of your bath. The telephone. Or doing up your back hair, or, worst of all, a delightful man may be making love to you, when k-ling, k-Hug, k-ling ! The telephone breaks off the thread of his theme and he fails to resume it."
