SHOULD FOOTBALL CEASE?

We desire to do full justice to the Opinions of those ranged on both sides in the controversy regarding the advis- ability of continuing the game of foot- ball during the continuance of the war. It is, however, somewhat difficult to determine the grounds upon which the anti-football agitators stand. In the abstract, and, if we consider the matter superficially, it does, admittedly, seem wrong that while our sons and brothers are shedding their blood in the trenches in Belgium, we in England should be playing football, or witnessing the playing of football, in thousands. But it is also admittedly impossible to prove, and it ought to be impossible to suggest, that anyone in this country is so indifferent to the well-being of our national life as to stand by callously in the way some extremists allege against their fellows. It is only the extremists who go thus far. The anti-footballers split themselves up into sections. One section say the footballers should be playing the nobler game ; another that the professionals should enlist, or be forced to do so ; a third that the lament- able part of the affair is that thousands of spectators are kept from the field by the attractions of the less noble game. They are divided among themselves, as they were bound to be. For in- stance, the "Times " urges that "it is not football that we need to abolish just now, but professional football— just as we should need to abolish any other game that hired large numbers of able-bodied young men away from their country's service." White. on the other hand, the "Manchester Guar- dian" draws attention to the difference between Berlin, where the women are acting as tram-guards in place of their husbands, away on active service, at a time when the Manchester ...ars are " stuffed " with spectators going to football matches. As a matter of fact, there is no need to get hysterical. Footballers and football spectators have done their,duty quite as well as other sections of the community. Upwards of 100,000 re- cruits have gone from the ranks of Association clubs, and the Rugby clubs have proved themselves propor- tionately patriotic. If the agitators narrow their demands down to profes- sional players, there are only a few hundred unmarried professionals rely- ing upon their pay in the Association I code. The number of Rugby players of course is fewer. Suppose these men enlisted in a body, there would not be a battalion. Suppose football ceased, what would the numerous married professionals do? They would be forced to enlist, and they would have no help from their employers, whose incomes would be cut off. But is it necessary to go into such arguments? If the country is to go on as nearly as possible "as usual" our sports must I go on like everything else. It is the maintenance of our ordinary life that will find the sinews of war, and reason- able recreation is absolutely necessary if workers are to succeed. Let us be fair and admit that if the footballer goes there are many other sources of plea- sure that must go also that have not the advantages of football, a game which turns out some of the best fight- ing material we have. In all these circumstances, we are glad to know that the great Associations of the king- dom, after conferring with the War Office; have decided to proceed "as usual," except that the internationals will not be played. There was no evi- dence, it was officially stated, "that the playing of football has hindered, or is hindering, recruiting. On the contrary there is good reason to conclude that

===== 0002601_19141205_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 5, 1914.

football has encouraged and assisted recruiting." The meeting was also of opinion "that to deprive the working people of our country of their Saturday afternoon recreation would be unfair and very mischievous."

;he ships. Our naval strength, says ,he FIRST LORD, is greater than ever.

" Between the beginning of the war and the end of 1915, while the Germans will be receiving an accession of three ships, we shall receive the following ships :—The Agincourt and the Erin, acquired from Turkey, the Tiger, the Benbow, the Emperor of India, the Queen Elizabeth, the Warspite, the Valiant, the Barham, the Reso- lution, the Ramillies, the Revenge, the Royal Sovereign, the Malaya, and the Ammirente Latore, which has been acquired from Chile and will be re-named the Canada—a total of 15 ships in all. All these ships are, of course, of the greatest power of any vessels that have ever been constructed in naval history, and it is no exaggeration to say that we could afford to lose a super-Dreadnought every month for twelve months without any loss occurring to the enemy, and yet be in approximately as good a position of superiority as we were on the declaration of the war."

Mr. LLOYD GEORGE, in his financial statement, was able to announce that, in spite of the Stock Exchange being closed, the War Loan had not only been subscribed, but had been over- subscribed. The confidence of the nation was plainly shown by the enor- mous number of small applicants, nearly one hundred thousand. "The manner in which the storm and stress had been met filled him with the con- viction that British credit was built on solid foundations which no foreseeable contingency could- destroy." After describing the means which the Gov- ernment had adopted to meet the un- precedented crisis when a general European War, affecting two-thirds of the population of the world, "crashed into the fine and delicate paper mach- inery" of modern credit, Mr. LLOYD GEORGE said that up to the present the position of Great Britain in inter- national commerce was a unique and commanding one and without parallel.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
A DESPERATE FOE.

We have now entered the fifth month of the great war, and so reached the period when Germany, if she was to be successful, was to have mortally wounded France and turned in undim- inished strength to strangle a -slowly- awakening Russia. Incidentally, external troubles were to have brought Great Britain to a state of impotence. What are the facts? Great Britain and France have " pinned " the enemy in Northern France and South-West Belgium, and in the process have inflicted such punishment upon the KAISER'S hordes that the losses to the enemy exceed those which a thought- ful Monarch considerately said he could afford to make in the whole of the campaign. Those losses were to bring him success; his actual losses have brought him failures in the field and shame before the civilised world. Russia, surprisingly awake from the first, has all but crushed Austria, and at this moment, when the KAISER'S legions are aimlessly and hopelessly wasting their substance on a long, weary line from Switzerland to Ostend, the CZAR'S soldiers are thundering at the gates of Cracow. as the preliminary to over-running Silesia and making the direct road to Berlin easier for the GRAND DUKE'S Armies in the centre. On our side, the loyalty of India and Egypt, and the dramatic disappearance of DE WET before the pressure of the loyalists of South Africa, have given the final touches to such a discomfiture of a bloodthirsty Monarch as was never before witnessed. .

If we would see the real straits to which our adversaries have been re- duced, they have themselves supplied ; us with fitting material this week. ' Take th case of Austria. The aged EMPEROR, FRANCIS JOSEPH, we are told, has received the following message from GENERAL FRANK, commanding the Fifth Army On the occasion of the 66th anni- versary of your Majesty's accession "permit me to lay at your feet the "information that Belgrade was "to-day occupied by the troops of the "Fifth Army." We should imagine that by now GENERAL FRANK has been cashiered for reminding his Sovereign that it has taken the Austrians four months to capture a city that lies on their own boundary and belongs to the small country, Servia, which has given the Austrians such a lively time since the conflict began. Belgrade is a poor exchange for lost provinces to the North ; but if such a success provides any consolation in Vienna we have no right to complain. The other good sign is the whining that comes from Germany. The CROWN PRINCE has been saying that Germany did not want war, which was "unnecessary "; and the German Imperial Chancellor, HEnR VON BETHMANN-HOLLWEG, has been telling the German Reichstag that England caused the war of malice aforethought in order to steal Ger- many's trade. The first gentleman is the Royal Looter, who evidently at one time revelled in the peculiar opportu- nities the war opened out to him ; the second is the gentleman who announced that Germany would hack a way through Belgium though it was a wrong—for which compensation would be paid. What becomes, we should like to know, of the German contention that Might is Right, and that Germany, being the strongest military Power, had the right to leave Peoples nothing but their eyes to weep with? Now the CHANCELLOR says it is all our own greedy fault that the greater part of the world is plunged in tears. It seems that we made the fatal false step when we refused to join in friendship with Germany a few years ago--and throw over our existing friends. As Germany looks upon formally signed treaties as mere scraps of paper, it is scarcely reasonable to expect us to trust in her mere professions of friend- ship. We may be knaves, but we plead guilty to not being fools.

THE MEN, THE SHIPS, THE MONEY. Our heads of departments have been taking stock, and Britons the world over have every reason to be proud of the result. Notwithstanding the pessimists, recruiting is going on quite satisfactorily. LORD KITCHENER told the House of Lords that he had nothing to complain of as regarded recruiting, for 30,000 men were offering themselves weekly, in addition to those who were coming forward for special regiments in different localities. While we can raise two divisions a week, composed, let it be remembered, of the best type of manhood the nation can produce, we have nothing to fear, and Berlin has not much cause for rejoicing. What our men can do when they get to the front is shown by the glowing dispatch sent by GENERAL FRENCH of the opera- tions that so effectually block the Ger- mans' way to Calais. "No more arduous task," says SIR JOHN, "has ever been assigned to British soldiers, and in all their splendid history there is no instance of their haring answered so magnificently to the desperate calls which of necessity were made upon them." We were few ; the Germans many. •Wave after wave of the enemy came up as the first ones were swept down. On November 11th, for instance, the "Germans continued to come on almost up to the guns, some bodies being picked up at a distance of only 70 yards from them. Realising that all might be lost unless a firing line of some kind could be established, the battery officers man- aged to form a line of gunners, regimental cooks, and details of various descriptions. These men stood firm, kept up a steady rifle fire, and checked the assault at a most critical moment, thus enabling other troops to come up to repel it more completely." British Army cooks defeated Prussian Guards! It was a desperate expedient, but we have firmly established our- selves in Belgium now, and, with the reserves that are pouring in, the cooks will be able to keep to their own parti- cular department. Still, we shall want more men, and more men. Mr. WINSTON CHURCHILL told the country how we are placed as regards

===== 0002601_19141205_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
TO HELP THE BELGIANS.

Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfleld's

Forthcoming

GRAND PATRIOTIC CONCERT.

AN ATTRACTIVE PROGRAMME.

it will be seen from our advertising columns that a concert, organised and arranged by Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, is to be given next Saturday evening in aid of the Belgian Fund. Those participating in it are, it is pleasing to note, Warrington people, and from this point of view, therefore, it is not unreasonable to predict for the venture a great success. Three well-known and deservingly popular local vocal- ists—Miss Marie Houghton, Mr. J. Babbitt, and Mr. J. B. Poole—are announced to sing songs, the Warrington Male Voice Choir will contri- bute some stirring part-songs, the Musical Society's Orchestra and Crosfield's famous Prize Band will, separately, perform some very attrac- tive pieces, and Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield and Mrs. Crosfield's sister, Miss Elliadi, are themselves set down for prominent parts as instrumental soloists.

Much of the music is of a martial or quasi- military character, as very plOperly befits the time and season, but there are one or two out- standing items of more serious import, to which especial attention may be directed, and as to which a few words in elucidation of their points of interest may not be out of place. The first is the lyrical prologue, to be sung by Mr. Poole, to Leoncavallo's opera " Pagliacci," which, it is agreed by all competent critics, is unexcelled for being vividly picturesque and musically ster- ling. The gamut of its moods may be gathered from the printed words of the programme. It is replete with pulsating human interest, each phase and incident being wedded to music of the modern school in a manner predicating in the most unquestioning way the possession of genius on the part of the composer. Among the instrumental numbers the Polonaise in E flat for Piano and orchestra, in which Mrs. Cros- field will take the solo part, calls for special notice. The strongly marked dance rhythm of the melody, and the wealth of ornament in which it is set, are quite representative of Chopin in his lighter moods.

Mr. Crosfield will also be 'heard as a Chopin exponent in one of the Nocturnes which are so intimately associated with this composer's more tender and graceful moments, and this charm- ing example of Chopin's art may be expected to gather emphasis by being placed in contrast with Stephen Heller's lively "Tarantella," to which it is coupled in the programme. Lastly, but by no means because it is of least importance, may be mentioned the Romance in F by Beethoven, in which Miss Elliadi will be heard for the first time in Warrington as a violin soloist. To all concert frequenters this item is familiar as a theme embodying captivating melody treated in the composer's inimitably tender manner, and developed on the lines of broad contrasts, of which he was a veritable master.

The programme is, as might be expected, strong in its appeal to patriotism, many ex- amples of national airs and anthems being cited, while numerous excellent concerted vocal items such as Elgar's "Reveille" and Walford Davies's "Hymn before action," along with songs like "The Trumpeter" and "Land of hope and glory," serve to remind members of the audience of the stirring times in which our lot is cast.

EVERYWHERE.

Nothing takes the point out of a joke so much as for somebody else to get it off ahead of you.

When a dentist in China is extracting a tooth for a patron an assistant pounds on a gong to drown the cries of the patient.

Mr. Cecil Chrisholm, in his biography of our Commander-in-Chief, draws attention to the fact that both Sir John French and General Joffre are square men. This no doubt accounts for the difficulty the enemy has in getting round them.—" Punch."

A Versifier.

"If you move, you're dead," the robber said. The victim laughed with glee. "Why, if I move I can be sure that I'm alive," said he.

The Cook's Reward.

Professor (to his aged cook): "You have now been 25 years in my service, Regina. As a reward for your fidelity I have determined to name the bug I recently discovered after you.

A Better Plan. Kind Parent: " Now, darling, tell papa all the things you wish for Christmas." Little Girl: "All right, I will; but it'll be quicker if I just tell you the things I don't want."

Every Little Helps. Officer (commanding skirmishing party) : "Very sorry to put you off your game, sir; but we had to come across here." Golfer: "Don't mention it, sir. It makes me feel I've done my bit."

Johnny and His Little Friend. "You and that :ittle Wattles boy seem to play very nicely together," said Johnny's mother. "I am glad there is one boy in the neighbour,ood that you can got along with." "Yes," replied Johnny, "I lick him every morning and then he's nice to me all day."

AN IDEAL CHAIRMAN.

Councillor Cough's Eulogy of

Alderman Burton's Services

===== 0002601_19141205_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
TO FIRE ENGINE COMMITTEE

"Freedom of the Borough" a Suggested Reward.

At the monthly 'meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening, Councillor Gough delivered a long speech eulogistic of the services rendered to the Fire Engine Committee by Alderman Burton, the said speech being made apropos of the following resolution contained in the printed minutes of the committee, of which Councillor Gough is chairman : "That the best thanks of this committee be given to Mr. Alderman Burton for his long and useful membership of this committee, and for the valuable work undertaken by him in re- organising the Fire Brigade, and that this com- mittee record its regret that he has been com- pelled, owing to ill-health, to sever his connec- tion with the committee, and expresses tile rope that he will speedily be restored to good health."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
"COULD NOT FIND A MORE PAINSTAKING CHAIRMAN."

"If you searched through the whole of the committees," said Councillor Gough, "you could not find a more painstaking chairman than Alderman Burton was." Every week, wet or fine, he went on, the Alde.rraan visited the Fire Station, and he had so re-organised the work of the Fire Brigade that, for a borough of the size, it was second to none. Instead of changing the men when the new class of fire engine was intro- duced, he obtained the services of an instructor from the Leyland Motor Works to teach the men how to work and manage the new style of engine, and they were now quite proficient, and were able to work to great advantage both the Steam and the motor engines. Alderman Burton believed in going into details, and also in fre- quent fire practices, so that the men might be I kept fit and always ready. He was an ideal chairman of committee, and never during his (the speaker's) experience under him did he try to over-ride his committee, and seldom did he act without consulting them. He was a gentle- man one could look up to and respect. The very soul of honour, it was a pleasure to sit under him. (Hear, hear.) No member of the Council could charge him with having come there to I grind his own axe. (Hear, hear.) His faithful- ness and honesty won him both admiration and respect.

"-In my opinion," added Councillor Gough, ' " Alderman Burton has attained to the hizjaest standard in human character that a chairman can attain, or that a public man can attain. A man's character is not to be measured by his brain capacity alone, nor by his ability to debate; but rather by the honest and faithi'al service he has rendered. lam delighted to feel that a large section of the members of this Coun- cil are desirous of conferring upon him the honour of the freedom of this county borough, and—

The MAroa (ringing his bell): That's not on the minutes!

Councillor Goron (continuing): I pray that this testimony by members of the Council— The MAyoR (again ringing loudly): You are going into quite another matter. Councillor GOUGH : I think it is in respect of his character, if you will allow me to respect his memory. (Laughter.) I pray that the testi- mony by the members of this Council for the honest and faithful work he has done for the town of his adoption will be a consolation and a joy during his sojourn upon earth. The minute was adopted.

THE WATCH COMMITTEE.

Councillor Roberts's Value to Fire Engine Committee.

COUNCILLOR GOUGH'S "REGRET."

At the meeting of the Town Council on Tues- day, the Mayor (Alderman Peacock) presiding, the minutes of the Fire Engine Commiftee were presented by the Chairman of that committee (Councillor S. Gough) for approval. Councillor Gough made indirect reference to the change of personnel in the Watch Committee decided upon at the last meeting of the Council, by passing in review the letter written by Councillor Roberts, declining to serve on any Corporation Committee, with the exception of the Education Committee, because of the action of the Council in excluding him from several important com- mittees on which he had hitherto served. Councillor Roberts moved the adoption of the minutes of the Education Committee, of which he is chairman, and then left the Council Charo_ her, having presumably no interest in the min- utes of the other committees to be presented, so that he was not present when Councillor Gough spoke. When the Fire Engine Committee minutes were reached, Councillor Gough, in moving their adoption, said : I should like to call the attention of the members of the Council to the letter from Mr. Councillor Henry Hoberts. Personally, I should like to say nothing in regard to that letter, but. as chairman of the Fire Engine Committee, I feel it my duty to express my great regret that a gentleman of Councillor Henry Roberts's ex- perience should so far have forgotten himself as to cast such a grave accusation against the majority of the members of this Council, and also the members of my committee. The im- portant work of my committee has long—a—go been acknowledged and recognised by this Council. Some of the oldest members of this Council have sat upon it, and some are still sitting upon it. Councillor Roberts was placed upon this com- mittee by the wishes of the large majority of this Council because they thought he was in a position, through his experience at Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, who own one of the best equipped private fire brigades in this country, to be of special service to the committee. One would naturally think that if Councillor Roberts desired to serve the ratepayers to the best ol his ability, now was his opportunity; and rtliought he would have been only too pleased to give the committee the benefit of his experience in a great work for his native town. Therefore the committee have, I think wisely, refused to accept his resignation. In conclusion, Mr. Mayor, I should like to say that a great many members voted for Councillor Roberts on this committee because they thought that the time of meeting—in the evening—would suit him better than afternoon meetings. It is recorded that Councillor Roberts has not attended the meet- ings of the Watch Committee, having only made two attendances—(the Mayor rang his bell Councillorvigorously, i n te Goughr i3t i g continuing) Councillor dGuorg ugi ) . the last twelve months. (Mayor's bell again.) The Mayor: I think it is the Fire Engine Committee we are talking about. (Laughter.) Councillor Gough: Yes; I only allude to this as an incident. Councillor Cunnington seconded the minutes. which were carried.

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE.

Important Nursing Appointment. Nliss Lilian Toft, of Warrington, has been aPPointed Matron of the Isolation Smallpox and Tuberculosis Hospitals at Chester. She was trained as a fever nurse at the Isolation Rospital. Aiken-street, Warrington.

Christ Church Sale of Work To-day. The annual sale of work in connection with Christ Church, Latchford, will be held in the Parish Room to-day, Mrs. R. I. Wynne-Jones !laving kindly promised to officiate at the open- ing ceremony, which is timed for three o'clock.

Scarlet Fever at Norley. • The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural bistrict Council was held at Dutton on Tues- day, when Mr. C. E. Linaker, sen., presided. total of 14 cases of scarlet fever was reported by the Medical Officer. The main outbreak as at Norley, no reason being assigned for it in the report—The Inspector of Nuisances said the outbreak had arisen from two original cases in which children were affected.

Councillor Taylor's Illness. At the 'I own Council meeting on Tuesday evening the Mayor announced that Councillor Taylor had notified that he regretted that he Was still unable to attend to his duties owing to illness. He hoped, however, to be able, very shortly, to attend not only the meetings of the Council, but also of the committees to which he belongs.—This welcome intelligence was re- ceived with a chorus of "Hear, hears," by the assembled councillors.

Shorthand Writing. At a well-attended meeting of the Warrington Shorthand Writing Association a demonstra- tion lesson on the "diphones" was given by one of the members. The discussion that took Place at the close of the lecture gave the mem- bers an opportunity of expressing their opinion regarding the recent changes in the Shorthand Instructgr. The remainder of the evening was devoted to speed practice, speeds ranging from 80 words to 150 words per minute being read out.

Annual Meeting of Savings Bank. The annual meeting of the Warrington, Run- corn and District Savings Bank will be held at Warrington on Tuesday. December 15th, at 11 a.m.

"Christianity and War." "Christianity and War" will be the subject of a public address to be given in the Friends' Meeting House by Mrs. Edith J. Wilson, of Birkenhead, to-morrow (Sunday) at 6.30 p.m.

" World Appropriating Power." The Rev. Irvine Lister concluded his series of special addresses on "The Influence of Religion upon Society," on Sunday evening, when the subject was "The World Appropriating' Power of Christianity." A large congregation assem- bled to hear this instructive discourse.

Market Prices.

The prices obtaining in the Warrington Grain Niarket on the Ist of the month. were :—Wheat, Ss. 11d. to 6s. per 701 b.; oats, 3s. 6d. to 3s. 101c1. Per 4.51 b. Wheat markets have hardened again, and there is an upward tendency. Oats are Still well looked after, and prices are firm. Straw prices rule at late rates, viz., 21s. to 225. 6d. per ton, f.o.r. Clover-hay finds a ready Zuarket at ..63 7s. 6d., f.0.r., and meadow-hay at ...t2 10s. to £3 15s, per ton.

Class Teachers' Association.

At the annual meeting of this Association, held on Friday last week, the following appoint- tnents were made: President, Mr. W. Minards,

Who gave an address criticising various phases of the educational system; vice-president, Mr. Whitehouse; hon. treasurer, Mr. T. Edden ; hon. secretary. Mr. G. Newburn ; committee, the Misses Kitchen and Brookes, and Messrs. Arm- strong, Malcolm, Dendy, Glover, Turner, Phillips and Knowles. An increased balance in hand of ~02 10s. was reported by the hon. treasurer, Mr. T. Edden. Assessment Committee.

At a meeting of the Warrington Assessment Committee yesterday, Mr. W. Furness presid- ing, letters were read from Lord Wollner's sec- retary and Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., with refer- ence to the proposed Government valuations for local rating purposes. Lord Wolmer's secretary explained that his Lordship had joined his regi- tient, but that the matter would be laid before hint on his return.—Mr. Smith stated that the /natter would have his careful 'consideration, although he thought it unlikely that any imme- diate steps would be taken in the matter.—The Clerk mentioned that copies of the proposals had been sent out to the members of the committee. Suspended Street Widening Scheme. At Tuesday's meeting of the Town Council, Alderman Bennett, though, at the same time, realising the wisdom of the proposal under Present circumstances, uttered words of regret that it had been found necessary to, for the time being, hang up the big street widening xcheme which, when carried into effect, is to transform the four main shopping and traffic hrteries of the town. The Alderman said that,

as the Council were aware, considerable discus-

sion had taken place as to whether, in view of the present state of affairs, it was not advisable to defer the scheme for widening the main streets of the borough and to limit the Council's iromediate activities to the completion of the Projected circus. Whilst he regretted it—for the scheme had been very dear to the hearts of some of them—he felt that, at the moment, it Would be better to go easy. Still, they were, 4t any rate, going on with the Market Gate iMprovements, and he was only sorry that, linder the circumstances, it Nuts not practicable to go on with the larger scheme.

Local Help for Belgians. In aid of the Belgian Refugees' Fund a recital cf sacred music is to be given in St. Mary's Church, Buttermarket-street, Warrington, on Thursday next, at 8 p.m., when admission will be by programmes, Is., 6d. and 3d. each. Haydn's 3rd Mass will be sung, the soloists being Mrs. Macbeth (soprano), Mrs. Johnson (contralto), Ur. J. M. Bate (tenor), and Mr. J. W. Bibby, With Mr. W. J. Wallington at the organ and Mr. J. Eckersley as conductor of the choir. The organist will also render the St. Saen's "Synod March," Wagner's " Tranermarsch," Massenet's The Last Sleep of Our Lady," and the Halle- lujah number from Beethoven's " Mount of Olives." Following the recital a service of Benediction of the Blessed Sacrament will take Place. The local fund for the Belgians, as also other local war funds, will benefit by an entertainient to be given by the pupils of the Priory School in the Cairo-street Schoolroom on Tues. (lay and Wednesday, December 15th and 16th. Tickets may be obtained from the pupils, or on application at the school, and should be pur- chased early, as the accommodation is limited.

Atough presided at the business meeting. Jones; committee, Mesdames R. tanner and G. Dale, and Messrs. A. Tilling, A. Walker, G. Dale, R. Jones, G. Head, A. Lowe, A. Gleave and P. Gleave.

ONE PENNY.

An In Memoriam Service.

What- -p7ov-ed-- ;;-be an impressive and very helpful memorial service for the late Mr. T. P. Taylor, was held at the Working Men's Mission Church, Bank Quay, on Sunday even- ing. The preacher was Councillor R. Henshall, of Latchford, and a very impressive obituary was given by Mr. George Dale. Special hymns were sung by the congregation, and solos were rendered by Mr. H. Foden and Madame Lily Fairhurst, the latter giving a very fine inter- pretation of "0, rest in the Lord."

Music and Poetry. For young people a special service was con- ducted by the Rev. T. T. James at the Wycliffe Congregational Church, Warrington, on Sunday evening. There was a good attendance. Early in the evening the organist, Mr. W. H. Payton, gave an impressive organ recital, which in- eluded: Russian March (Clark), Andantino (Schubert), Offertoire (Batiste), and Meditation, from "The Holy City" (Gaul). The sermon was upon "The Religion of a Modern Poet—Francis Thompson," and the preacher illustrated his discourse with many inspiring passages from the poet's works.—During the service Mr. Harold Hawthorn rendered the solo, "The Voice of the Father" (Cowen).

Operetta at Brick-street Mission. ' An operetta, entitled " Sherwood's Queen," by P. Mee Pattison, was given at the Brick-street Mission on Thursday, in aid of the Chapel Re- demption Fund. The entertainment was a great success, Mr. A. Morris and Miss Eva Shaw featuring as Robin Hood and Maid Marian re- spectively. The leading soloists were the Misses E. Brookes (maid to the Queen) and E. Wainwright (the widow), and Messrs. C. Turner (Little John), H. rfolt (Allan-a-dale), W. H. Dunkley (Friar Tuck), W. Barton, A. Yates Dutton and W. Yates Dutton (the widow's sons). The principals were supported by a splendid chorus of boys and girls. Mr. W. H. Dunkley, as Friar Tuck, was at his best, being the humorist of the evening. The performance was preceded by a tea party.

Boy's Death from Unusual Cause. An inquiry was conducted by Mr. S. Brighouse at Warrington on Thursday into the circum- stances surrounding the death of Frank Rid- bone (6), son of David Radbone, organ grind6r, Shepherd's-row, who died in the Infirmary on Tuesday.—The mother stated that on Monday the boy's face was swollen. She bathed it with warm water and he went to school. His face seemed to be better at night, but on Tuesday morning it was again swollen. She bathed it once more and the child improved, but about 6.30 he complained of severe pains in the head. She set out to take him to the Infirmary, and when she got him there he collapsed and died.— Dr. Forster attributed death to status lymphati- cus, which was a natnral cause.—The jury re- turned a verdict of "Death from natural causes."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Shelters for Tram Travellers.

At the Town Council meeting on Tuesday night, Alderman Bennett expressed satisfac- tion at finding the following among the min- utes of the Electricity and Tramways Com- mittee :—" The Tramways Manager was in- structed to report on the provision of shelters at the tramway termini." The matter, said the Alderman, was urgent, and he did hope that it was the intention of the committee to deal with it forthwith. He had heard constant com- plaints about the lack of such provision, which would confer a great boon and convenience upon the public using the trams.—The Deputy-Mayor said he brought the matter before the com- mittee, and it was considered by them that shelters would be a real benefit to those who travelled by the trams. They wanted shelter accommodation at every terminus—not to pick out one or two termini, but to provide shelters , for the whole lot. (Hear, hear.)—The com- mittee nstruction was endorsed.

£lOO Cheque for the Infirmary. The Chairman of the Board of Management of the Infirmary has received a cheque for £lOO from Mr. William Fairhurst, Rose Bank, Whit- ley, near Northwich, "in appreciation of the institution and in commemoration of our Diamond Wedding."

Mr. Harold Smith and the Naval

Reserve.

The Admiralty announces that the following M.P.'s will shortly receive commissions as Hon. Lieutenants in the Royal Naval Volunteer Re- serve: Mr. Beck, Mr. Mitchell Thompson, Mr. Harold Smith, and Mr. Whyte.

Christmas Toys for Poor Children. The big assortment of toys, &c., resulting from the Mayor's happy thought to request each guest accepting the invitation to attend the recent. "At Home" given by himself and the Mayoress, to bring with them some little gift for the poor children of the borough, will be distributed under the direction of the Director of Education (Mr. J. Moore Murray) just before the schools "break up" for the Christmas holidays. In his task of bestowing the gifts in deserving quarters, Mr. Murray will have the assistance of the school teachers.

Brief Town Council Meeting. The December meeting of the Town Council, held "on Tuesday evening, was of unusual brevity, the agenda being got through in a little over a quarter of an hour. Those present were: The Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock), the Deputy- Mayor (Councillor Joseph), Aldermen Bennett, James Evans, John Evans, Pierpoint, Sme- thurst and Tinnion, and Councillors, Archer, Barton, Broadhurst, Chantler, Chrimes, Cun- nington, Dickenson, Gough, Halliwell, Haw- thorn, Lashford, Lindley, Locker, McKee, Osborne, Pemberton, Roberts, Shaw, Skelland, Wilkinson and Wilson.

The Local War Funds.

During the week ending December 4th, £5B Is. 21d. was forwarded from Warrington and district to the National Relief Fund, making the total raised in this locality since the open- ing of the fund up to £4,234 13s. 7d. The Local War Relief Fund now stands at £331, ..el7 14s. 2d. having been added during the week, whilst a total of -C758 17s. nd. has been subscribed to the Warrington War Refugees' Fund. The total received on account of the Tobacco Fund for the Warrington Line Battalion (2nd South Lancashires) is £ll3 3s. 2d. The Prince of Wales' Fund now stands at over _C4,000,000.

COMFORTS FOR OUR SOLDIERS.

SCHOLARS AND WAR FUNDS.

Nearly £6O liaised at Secondary School

At the Warrington Secondary School on Satur- day evening was held the fifth of the series of socials in aid of the various war funds, Form Vb. this time providing a most enjoyable pro- gramme, under the guidance of Miss E. Wil- kinson. With pupils, parents and friends, over 250 attended.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
FORM Vb.'s SOCIAL.

Dancing (including an Irish jig .competition) and a humorous sketch attracted the majority of the youngsters; whilst the remainder were catered for in the various exhibitions of skill— such as pig-drawing, houp-la and doll-dressing. The temporary " Post Office" proved a strong attraction, and realised a considerable amount by means of penny correspondence; whilst a set of Army and Navy framed portraits, which were raffled, realised £1 9s. The refreshment stall, which was under the charge of the Misses F. and M. Wilkinson, was well patronised during the whole evening, and largely contributed to the net profit of ..t9 18.—a handsome result. Naturally, the pupils who made the effort are very proud of the success which attended it. The proceeds are to be devoted to the Belgian Refugees' Fund, the Blue Cross Fund (for wounded horses), and the Mine Sweepers' Fund.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
ThE STUDENT-TEACHERS.

The student-teachers decided that they would contribute their share by means of a raffle, the prizes for which were a set of Thackeray's works (kindly presented by Miss A. Place, of the Bolton Council Schools), and a fountain pen. By energetic canvassing amongst their friends, they increased the school contribution to the war funds by the sum of £s—which was forwarded to H.R.H. Princess Mary, as a dona- tion to her Christmas Fund for Soldiers and Sailors.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
OVER £5B RAISED.

In response to the various local and national appeals in connection with the war, the teachers and pupils have now collected a sum exceeding £5B. In addition to this, letters of thanks to the school have recently been received from the Mayor and from Mrs. Fairclough, in acknowledgment of a second consignment of woollen articles for .the comfort of the soldiers belonging to our local regiment.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
HELP ALL ROUND.

Mrs. Edelsten reports : A substantial bundle of second-hand suits, mufflers, &c., was dispatched to the Base Hos- pital at Fazakerley, but a plea had been put in on behalf of the wounded soldiers at the War- rington Infirmary for shirts and socics, which, needless to say, were gladly given. • It was represented by one of the helpers that fifty Warrington men, now serving in the Bth Lancashire Battalion on Salisbury Plain, have been overlooked by the good old town; belts and mittens were therefore sent to them, with a promise of more if required. One parcel labelled "For the soldiers in France," was sent to Mrs. Ireland-Blackburne, for the South Lancashires at the front.

Welcome gifts were received from the girls of the Secondary School, the children of the Sacred Heart School, the Training College, and many others.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT "TOMMY" NEEDS.

" I am pleased to say we get plenty of every- thing. The only thing I would like is two handkerchiefs a week, a packet of cough lozenges, three pairs of very thick woollen stock- ings, and a pair of thick woollen gloves covered with leather to keep them dry. It does not matter about the neatness of the leather being put on as long as they will keep dry."—Letter from the Front.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT ABOUT THE ARMY VETERINARY CORPS?

Though little is heard of the above corps dur- ing the war, its work is none the less useful, and Mrs. Shelton Jones, of the Causeway, Wilderspool-road, the local collector for the corps, desires to thank the very many friends who have generously contributed comforts for the members of the corps now at the front. She has received a letter from Mrs. Moore, the wife of Brigadier-General Moore, commanding the veterinary corps in France, thanking Warring- ton friends for their contribution. More "com- forts " are still wanted.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
TARIFF REFORMERS BUSY.

The sewing meetings have been running merrily on at the headquarters of the Women's Unionist and Tariff Reform League. The com- mittee are still carrying on their good work, and the willing band of workers are kept con- stantly busy making up and dispatching par- cels to soldiers and sailors. This week, letters have been received from Egypt and India as well as from the front. The applications do not grow any less, and Mrs. W. Peter Rylands, who is in charge, is assisted in her work by Mrs. Henry Greenall, Mrs. Armitage, Mrs. Bow- man, Miss Benn, Miss Armitage and Miss Tay- lor. The committee wish to thank the many ladies and gentlemen who have sent welcome gifts of money, clothing, pipes, tobacco and cigarettes to the fund during the past week.

The Russians having objected to Being called a steam roller, the London and North-Western Railway have tactfully taken their fast engine "Teutonic" and re-christened her "The Czar." —"Punch."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
A CALL TO IRE FRONT FROM

THE FRONT.

" I think it is every man's duty, to come here and do his bit, and not be loafing at football matches and seeing the war at picture palaces." This striking sentence is quoted from a letter which Mr. J. Lovatt, of 61, Hale-street, Warring- ton, has received from his son, Corporal Frank Lovatt, of the Inniskilling Dragoons, who is serving at the front as a member of the Army Mounted Police.

"We are all waiting for Kitchener's Army to arrive here," he adds, "as it is men we want. The more men we get, the sooner the war will be over. It would be a good thing if we had conscription now. It would save a lot of money, as the war would end more speedily."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW LIEUT. PERCY WYNDHAM

DIED.

Private H. Turton, 3rd Coldstream Guards, writes:— .

" I have seen Lieutenant Percy Wyndham mentioned several tithes in the papers, but I don't think anyone can give a clearer account of his end than myself. He was my platoon commander. I fought with him from the be- ginning. He was always so dashing and brave that we would go anywhere with him. He was shot down when the Guards' Brigade crossed the Aisne and fought up the heights at a village called Soupir, near Soissons. I was about ten yards away, but after firing five rounds I found that he was dead. I told one of our officers that he had fallen, and he said terrible things about the Germans. Next morning the same officer asked me and three more men to get spades and bury him. We did so, and a more sorrowful party never went out. One officer cried like a child after reading the burial service. When we were finished we all agreed that one of Eng- land's bravest lay there."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
LIFE IN WAR PRISONERS' CAMP IN GERMANY.

Lieutenant W. George .Clark, of the South L,lncashire Regiment, who has fought in the series of battles from Mons to the Efarne and back across the Aisne to the Belgian border, and was captured by the enemy on October 21. st, sends an interesting letter to his parents in Southport, in which he describes how at two o'clock in the morning of October 21st the Ger- mans broke the line some distance away, and coming in on the flank overpowered a small body of British troops from behind before a blow could be struck in defence, as at that moment the latter were heavily engaged with another force in front, which they were able to hold in check. There was nothing for it but to surrender, as they were only a handful com- pared with the enemy. The officers and men were quickly got out of the danger zone, and sent to Douai, about 15 miles north-east of Lille, where they were detained for several days. On October 30th the officers were entrained for Mainz, on the Rhine. The officer in command of the citadel, he writes, is very just, and has treated them with courtesy and consideration; they are as com- fortable as prisoners of war can be. The daily routine commences with breakfast at eight, con- sisting of coffee and rolls; there is exercise from ! nine to eleven; a good square meal at one; ! exercise again from two till four; " cafe an lait" I at 4.30; supper at seven; lights out at nine. The I quarters are clean and comfortable, with a canteen near at which the prisoners can pur- chase various comforts at a moderate price.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
A LETTER FROM H.M.S. GLASGOW. Former Warrinutonian Writes Rome

Prior to the-Battle Off Chili.

Mr. D. Purcell, of 158, Whetstone-lane, Bir- kenhead, and formerly of Warrington, has I received an interesting letter from his son, Lance-Corporal Alfred Purcell, R.M.L.1., who was borne on the books of H.M.S. Glasgow, one of the vessels engaged in the recent naval battle off the coast of Chili, when H.M.S. Monmouth was lost. In the letter, which was written prior to the engagement, Lance-Corporal Purcell—who, be- fore enlisting, was employed by the St. Helens Cable Company—mentions the receipt of two "Examiners "—sent him by Mr. F. Allman, of the Whitecross Works—and says that he was very pleased with them. "Up to the time of writing" (September 20th), he says, "we have only entered harbour once—at Monte Video. Salt-pork and corned-beef on alternate days constitutes our bill of fare. We have an aux- iliary cruiser—(a liner)—out here with us, and she has got refrigerators on board and has taken in a stock of fresh meat, so I think we shall be all right for a little while Once or twice we have had to turn out to go into action (?), but when our searchlights have been turned on the enemy' she has proved to be a 'tramp.' decently now. We have captured a German steamer carrying a cargo estimated to be of considerable value - A couple of

steamers have been sunk out here by a German warship. We have searched for the warship, but—well, warships are here to-day and gone to-morrow." .

The writer adds that the ship's printer aboard the liner referred to above is a Warrington boy, who "served his time at Woods'."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT BRITISH PLUCK WILL

DO!

Private T. Dawson, of "A" Company, Ist Battalion, King's Liverpcol Regiment—which is attached to the 2nd Division of the 6th Bri- gade—has written a long and interesting letter to Mr. Dale, the bead of the fuel department at Messrs. Crosfield's Works, under whom he worked prior to joining the colours. He begins with a mention of the fact that he was in the advance on Mons and the subsequent retreat, and that he also took part in the battles of the Marne and the Aisne. In addition his regiment figured in the lesser fights at Mons, Bavai, ViHors, Colterets, Hautevesnes, and elsewhere. "The people of Liverpool," he adds, "have been very geed to us in sending us gifts of tobacco, &c., and, here in the trenches, cold, wet and hungry, and with our last cigarette and almost with our last match gone, you can imagine the joy with which these gifts have been received. . . . . I understand that our wives and fami- lies are being well looked after, thank God! That is a great strain off our minds, and God only knows the strain there is on them—stand- ing in the face of death, with hearts like iron, under heavy shell fire, day and night !" GERMANS IN BRITISH UNIFORMS.

The writer goes on to relate how on the morning of October 24th his regiment came into contact with a strong force of the enemy. "The first lot we encountered," he says, "were dressed in the uniforms of some of the English regiments. Some were in the uniforms of the Warwicks, and some in those of the Queen's. Our regiment was one of the first to advance into' the firing line—against overwhelming numbers. Our losses were pretty heavy, but we fought all day under a very heavy rifle-fire, with shell-fire at night. The Germans took up a position only 250 yards from our trenches, and we could hear one of them playing on a cornet 'A little ship was on the sea, It was a pretty sight' !

TERRIBLE TIMES IN THE TRENCHES. • " Twenty minutes later they attacked us all along the line. We fought all night and all next day, till we were nearly exhausted. We were determined not to be beaten but clung to our rifles, with boiling oil running from the woodwork on to our hands, and all the steel-work red-hot and burning us as we loaded up. It showed what British pluck will do There were 112 of us in my posi- tion, and the Germans must have outnumbered us ten to one. . . . . . They attacked us twice again the next night, but we held our own, with only a couple of casualties. All through the next day we were fighting, our artillery doing fine work. Every now and again you could see the Germane being blown out of their trenches, arms, legs, heads, helmets and rifles being blown into the air. The sights we saw were horrible. Pigs and cows and goats were also being blown to pieces. The screams of these were worse than the moans and wails of the wounded and dying. When ,1 the fighting was at its thickest a young goat jumped into my trench. . . . That night the Germans sung their National Anthem and attacked us four times, but each time we drove them back with heavy losses."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
"RIGHT AMONG THE FUN." Soldier-Brothers From Warrington

Write llome.

Mr. Septimus Machell, of Pinner's-brow, has two sons serving at the front, both of whom are transport drivers in the Army Service Corps. In a letter to his parents, one of the brothers, Driver Harry Machell—formerly an employe of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Company, Ltd., and now attached to the Indian contin- gent—writes asking for a muffler, as " it is very cold out here." He states that the troops are experiencing snow, and adds ironically: "You know what it is like—living in tents and some- times at the bottom of the waggons. It is just the same as a feather bed—only a bit softer and wanner."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
QUITE HAPPY AND WILLING TO DO HIS

SHARE.

Attached to the No. 1 Headquarters Company, 4th Divisional Train, is Driver Fred Machell, who, before going to the front, was in the em- ploy of Mr. J. llallwood, baker. In a letter home the gallant driver says: "You would be surprised if you knew where I am. The only thing I can tell you is that I am right among the fun. lam writing this letter in a summer- house where we are sleeping. lam seeing sights here that I never experienced in my life, but I am quite happy, and am willing to do my share."

Mr. Walter Watts, a son-in-law of Mr. Septi- mus Machell, has been called up with the Naval Reserve, and is now stationed at Chatham.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FLIGHT BEFORE THE HUNS. Penheth Soldier's Graphic Pen-Picture of Belgian Exodus.

WAR'S SAD SIGHTS.

Private D. A. hackney, who belongs to Pen- keth, and who is serving in Kitchener's Army at the front, has written a long letter from Caestre, France, to Mrs. Johnson, one of the principal workers in connection with The Pen- keth and Sankey War Fund endeavour, in which he thanks her and her co-workers for a parcel of clothing, &c., received by him for himself and comrades, and goes on to describe in inter- esting fashion some of his experiences during the time he has been on the Continent.. He writes:—

"I really cannot express in words my grati- tude to you and your workers or the very appropriate parcel which I received quite safe and intact yesterday morning. The contents, I can assure you, were very welcome, as at the time of writing we are having, as regards weather a real good old English winter. We have had snow-storms, hail, rain and wind for the past ten days, and now we have ice to the thickness of about three inches.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
Unforgettable Kindness.

"Your kindness my comrades and I shall never forget, as I shared out with them your gift. It was better than a Beecham's to see the Radiant smiles light up their faces as I showed the con- tents.

"Through the generosity of General FrenOh— for the most excellent work we have done, as he terms what was our duty—we have come from the firing line to this village (Caestre) to rest and refit for an indefinite period, and some lucky non-commissioned officers have a leave of seven days for the purpose of proceeding to England. "Of course, now I can find time to recuperate and write letters, as well as have a little recrea- tion. We arrived at Rouen, after having a splendid voyage across, coming up the beautiful Seine. We had a most warm-hearted recep- tion by the peasants. Then we travelled by train up north as far as Veaux. Belgium, where we detrained and marched on to Mons.

The Butchery at Mons. "You will have read in your papers more than I could tell you about the daring deeds, the brave acts, our men did against such odds. It was simply slaughter—just like a butcher taking a flock of sheep to the slaughter-house. "I might say that when we detrained and were on the road the Belgian people couldn't do enough for us, giving us fruit, tobacco, cigars, bread, meat, wine in bottles, &c.; in fact, they ran after us gesticulating to convey to us that everything they possessed we could have or make use of. 'They knew what we had come for, viz., to save them and their country from the atrocious Germans.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Historic Retreat Graphically Described.

"But, alas! I wonder what their thoughts were when, after three days, we had to retire and leave them to their fate, for they had put such confidence in our ability to drive the Ger- mans back. Still, it was part of the strategy of General Toftre that we should retreat. And what a retreat! What a sight were those sixty un- broken miles of fleeing people !

" They were all people who had despaired of breaking through the dense masses of fellow citizens, who had camped around the railway stations and who had decided to take to the road as the only way of escape. They came in every kind of vehicle—taxi-cabs, for which rich people had paid fabulous prices; motors, which had escaped military requisition; farmers' carts, some laden with several families, and others with piles of household goods; shop carts, drawn by horses already tired to the point of death; pony traps and governess cars. Many people, well dressed and obviously well-to-do, were wheeling vehicles like costers' barrows, but, instead of trundling cabbages like the coster, they were pushing forward sleeping babies and little children, who seemed, on the first stage of the journey, to find a new amusement and excitement in this journey from home. But, for the most part, the people were on foot.

War, the Leveller of all Ranks. "They trudged along, oh, so bravely; some by the side of our horses, others on the path, and others still by our waggons and guns, taking hold of any loose straps that might help them along; some of our officers ordered them to leave go. Some were carrying babies on their backs; others holding the hands of little chil- dren. They were of all classes; the barriers of rank and fortune being broken down by the common tragedy of it all. Elegant women,

whose beauty is known ip the Paris saloons, whose frivolity, perhaps, in the past was the main purpose of their lives, were now on a level with the peasant-mothers of their country. Along every mile of the way there were people who had fainted by the roadside, and poor old people who could go no further, but sat on the bank below the hedges, weeping silently or bid- ding the younger ones go on and leave them to their fate. Young women, who had stepped out so bravely at first, were footsore and lame, so that they limped along with lines of pain about their lips and eyes.

Sights that would have moved Hearts of Flint. "Many of the taxi-cabs—bought at great price —and likewise many of the motor cars had broken down and had been abandoned by their owners, who had decided to walk. Farmers' carts had jolted into ditches and lost their wheels. Wheelbarrows, too heavy to trundle, had been tilted up and all their household goods spilt in the roadway, only the children being carried on. "All this was what we saw on our retreat. I am sure the most hard-hearted of criminals could not have helped sledding a tear at such sights. Often as I lay in my bivouac, or out in the open, or stood on sentry-go at night. I thought: What if this was in England? What wouldn't men do? And yet the brave Belgians have borne it with a stout heart! God knows when it will end.

In the Wake of the Devastating Hun!

"We arrived at a town called Coulommiers, and then we turned. Yes, turned—and I am sure God turned us at the right moment, and that turn was to save Paris. The Germans dreamed of a prize which they did not, and never will, get, but which they were in sight of. "Well, we drove them over the Marne. But what a sight met our eyes as we came to the various villages and towns! The destruction they had dealt to innocent babies, women and old men was indescribable. They had looted every building they came across, from the poor- est cottages to the most fashionable mansion. In the roads and fields we came across boots, books, cooking utensils, bedding, Ix., and in the cafes they left traces of their drinking bouts. .

'Then we fought our way to Aisne, where we met their `Jack Johnsons.' There was a swish, a plonk, and then the bursting, and after that a hole large enough to hold a horse

and cart. You never knew where they would burst, although we could hear them coming. Spies Who Lived in a Haystack. - _

At a village called Courcelle on the Aisne, we caught several spies—the pest of the country! One, a man with a wooden leg, had about 300 yards of wire round his body, which Wa.73 in- tended for signalling and telegraph purposes. Another two men were inside a haystack, which had been hollowed out, and at night time they signalled with lanterns from the top of the stack. They had twelve weeks' rations with them when captured. "We held this position for about a month; I then were relieved by the French. We entrained for Belgium to take and hold Ypres, a splendid old-fashioned city. We were just in time. As we went in at one end of the town the Germans came in at the other, and a great slaughter took place, our guns getting into action in the streets and mowing the Germans down like mowing corn, and our infantry also charged them. The Black Watch went through them like hot grease, but lam sorry to say they lost heavily. It was I nothing to see men's arms, legs and heads flying about in all directions. "I might say we are all looking larwtrd to peace, but I expect I shall eat my Christmas dinner on the Continent. Peace, in my opinion, will not come until April, at any rate."

===== 0002601_19141205_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
" DODGING SHELLS AND BOMBS."

AVarrington Sapper's Exciting Time With War Telegraphs.

Sapper Frank Scragg, of 17, Myrtle-grove, Latchford, a Post Office clerk and the son of Mr. William J. Scragg, was a special reservist in the Royal Engineers, and now belongs to D Section of the Army Signallers. For some months he has been busily engaged in the actual area of war. Writing to his wife on November 25th, 1914, the sapper says: I have had no letter from you for some time now, but am putting it down to the fact that I have been moving about again. At present we are a happy little party of six, in a cottage (with a tele- graph instrument). We are here with the 2nd Division, which is resting after a hard time in the fighting line. . . A WONDERFUL ESCAPE.

"You will have read of a !lace called —, and what has taken place there. Well, I was there for three weeks, and got quite used to dodging shells and bombs. In five days, our party were shelled out of three iirtferent offices. At one time the man I was talking to was struck by a splinter, and at the last a " coal- box " dropped by the door, killing five and wounding six. However. I escaped with simply being lifted out of my chair into the window.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
"SHOWERS OF DEBRIS."

"Next day, as we were travelling from an office to our billet, a bomb dropped on a roof three yards away, throwing showers of debris on the cover of our motor, and throwing us all in a heap inside. After another week of this, we had to move outside the town, and began to breathe more freely. " I left the cable section a month ago, and am now on a telegraph section attached to G.II. 2. I am in the best of health, and enjoy the quietness here, after days and nights of roar- ing guns and bursting shells. What a change to be sitting in an easy chair, after sleeping in fields and railway trucks! . . I expect to be here for a week or two at least"

KILLED IN HISTORIC NIGHT ATTACK.

Warrington Trooper Lost in Assault

on Prussian Guards.

News has been received in Warrington from the War Office of the death,in action of one of her brave sons, in the person of Trooper Sidney George Taylor, of the 3rd Dragoon Guards, who was mortally hit on November 6th, during a night attack on the Prussian Guards in the vicinity of Ypres. Trooper Sidney Taylor was the second son of Mr. and Mrs. D. G. Taylor, of Walter-street, Padgate-lane, and, after leaving school, served his time with Mr. George Baker, gauge-maker, Winwick-road.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
LOST HIS LIFE IN HIS FIRST FIGHT.

He entered the Army about five years ago, and for the past two and a half years he was stationed in Egypt, but came over with the troops from there about six weeks ago. He was able to obtain forty-eight hours' leave in which to visit his home before going to the front, and he had only been out there a few days when he met his death in his first engage- ment. News of his fate was first received in Warrington from Trooper Fred Kershaw, of Willis-street, who is in the same regiment, and who announced Taylor's death on a post-card to his mother. But, as time went by and no confirmation was received from headquarters, hopes were entertained that the news might be unfounded. However, it proved to be only too true.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OLD ORFORD SCHOOLBOY.

Trooper Taylor was 26 years of - age, and was of fine physique. He was well known in War- rington, and especially in the Padgate district. He was educated at the Orford School. Much sympathy is expressed with the bereaved family. . _ _

In Connection with the death of Trooper Taylor it is interesting to note that his regi- ment was attached to the 7th Division, whom Sir John French has specially mentioned in his dispatches for their fine night attack at Ypres-- the attack in which young Taylor lost his life, and of which the great General wrote: "It will always be remembered in history !"

===== 0002601_19141205_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====

Mr. and Mrs. Taylor, it is interesting to note, have another son—their eldest—serving his King and country, in the person of Sapper L. Taylor, of the Royal Engineers, who has been out with the Expeditionary Force since the beginning of the War. He has been in the "thick of it" at various times, and now, we believe, is stationed at Rouen.

OUR WOUNDED GUESTS. Ways in Which We Can Show

Appreciation.

The wounded soldiers at the Infirmary may be visited every afternoon from two o'clock to four, and we can assure our readers that the gallant fellows deeply appreciate a chat and a handshake with sympathetic admirers. It cheers them up and shows them that they are not forgotten, and that their services are appre- ciated. There are at present 24 of them in the special ward, in addition to 12 convalescent out-patients. There have been ten new arrivals during the week and five discharges. When an "Examiner" representative visited the institu- tion yesterday he found the soldiers enjoying a concert. This was only one of the occasions on which such a treat has been given the men, and we might inform the public that anyone de- sirous of brightening the lives of these ailing warriors can arrange for similar concerts to take place in the ward between 6.30 p.m. and 8 p.m. Gifts of clothing, fruit, eggs, vegetables, cigarettes, tobacco and matches will be grate- fully received.

Similarly, the wounded men at present quar- tered in the Haddon Court Hospital—a fresh batch of whom arrived a week ago—are open to receive visits from appreciative and admiring sympathisers, between the hours of 2 and 3 on Monday afternoons. and 6 and 7 on Thursday evenings. Next Thursday being "Pound Day" at the Court, visitors may drop in at any hour of the afternoon or evening. ONE WAY OF HELPING. .

Readers are reminded that another way in which they can show a mead of appreciation is by attending the lecture on " Florence Nightin- gale," which Mrs. Ridgway will deliver in the Parr Hall at 8 o'clock on Monday evening next. Before the lecture a Male Voice Choir will give two selections and Mr. and Mrs. Sepliten will sing duets, whilst at the close the choir will render the National Anthems Of the Allies.

The Hon. Treasurer of Raddon Court Hospi- tal begs to acknowledge with many thanks the sum of £7 55., per Mr. Mercer, being the pro- ceeds of a whist drive on November 19th. Next Thursday, December 10th, has been set apart as a "Pound Day" in aid of the Raddon Court Hospital, and it is announced that the gifts which will be specially welcome are vege- tables, brawn, tongues, hams, fruits, mince- meat and coffee. The Borough Treasurer begs to acknowledge the gift of a large parcel of magazines from Mr. John Povey, 109, Willis-street, for wounded soldiers.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
YOUNG MEN!

Still More Warrington Recruits

W anted.

More, and still more, men are required to give themselves to their country as " soldiers of the King" to ensure that the war shall be brought to the earliest possible and most glorious con- clusion. Recently Sir John French wrote: "It is an honour to belong to such an army as ours;" and the honour is one that every fit man from 19 to 38 years of age may share. Splendid as has been Warrington's response to file call to arms, it is necessary that yet more should feel the call, and, feeling, answer it. During the past few weeks there has been a falling off in the number of men who have offered themselves, and it is hoped that the young men of the town will take the fact to heart and do all in their power to wipe out the reproach. As an inducement—if one needs other induce- ment beyond that of rendering serv:ce to his country—it is pointed out that there is rapid promotion for men of intelligence and zeal, and that already hundreds, who enlisted as privates, have become officers because of their merits and courage, whilst thousands more have attained to non-commissioned rank.

Up to last night 63 men had recruited at the Central Recuiting Station in the Police Build- ings since the beginning of the week, as against 77 the week before, and 166 the week before that. The largest number of recruits recorded locally in any one week was 345. NATIONAL RESERVE GUARD. Abont.thirty men are still required to com- plete the first Warrington company of the National Reserve Guard, 34 having already joined. Upon enrolment the men are equipped and armed. They are required for guarding German prisoners, railways, and like duties. Full particulars may be obtained at the Drill Hall.

HOSPITAL SUNDAY. A Balance of £241 for the Infirmary.

A meeting of the Hospital Sunday Committee was held at the Infirmary yesterday afternoon. Mr. F. A. Frost presided and there were also present the Revs. W. T. Derry, H. Ogden and G. C. Briggs, and Messrs. J. Fairclough, R. C. Parr, W. Daintith, and C. Jack-son Holmes, with Mr. A. J. Jolley (secretary of the com- mittee), and Mr. J. 11. J. llampson (secretary of the Infirmary). RECEIPTS UP AND EXPENSES DOWN.

_ In his report, Mr. Jolley stated that ten places of worship which contributed to the fund in 1913 had failed to do so this year, and on the balance there was a net decrease under this head of .-,t7 3s. 11d. The total receipts had been £257 9s. 10d., as against .i:253 4s.'Bd. for the previous year. A sum of £l5l 12s. 3d. was collected within, and £lO5 17s. 7d. without, the borough. The total expenses had been ...V.16 Bs. 2d., as against ..tl9 is. 9d., leaving a balance of £241 is. Bd. He thought there were local circum- stances to account for the decrease in some of the contributions from the churches. Mr. J. Fairclough proposed that Mr. Jolley be re-appointed secretary, and that he be heartily thanked for his services. He did not think they could find a better man for the office. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Daintith seconded, and several members including the Chairman having supported, the proposition was carried unanimously. In acknowledgment, Mr. Jolley announced his willingness to do anything he could for the in- stitution.

It was decided that March 14th should be set apart as Hospital Sunday next year. LOCAL PEOPLE'S STRANGE PREJUDICE. The Rev. W. T. Derry said that he had re- ceived a circular from the Manchester Medical Charities asking them to devote a Sunday's col- lections to their funds. That was impossible, se far as the Free Ch irch.-s were concerned. Be noticed that 447 patients went to the Man- chester Infirmary from Warrington last year. It was true that there were seven other towns who were helped more than Warrington and had not subscribed, but he wondered if the com- mittee had taken into consideration whether it were possible to make some grant to the Man- chester Infirmary, which gave them so much help.

The Chairman said he thought the trouble was that Warrington people were prejudiced against the Infirmary. It N%ll, so to speak, the old story, "A prophet is not without honour, save in his own country." People had the erroneous idea that they secured better treat- ment in Manchester.

The Rev. W. T. Perry: Are you full up? The Chairman: No; we can treat them here. Mr. R. C. Parr: If we send to Manchester, we shall have to send to Liverpool. Mr. Jolley pointed out that there were some people who preferred to send special cases to Manchester er Liverpool. The Chairman: Not 447.

HOME FROM A MERICA. Warringtonian Discusses Effect of

War on "The States."

Mr. Rennig Scragg, youngest son of Mr. William J. Scragg, of 17, Myrtle-grove, Latch- ford, arrived in Warrington on Thursday after- noon, having journeyed from Newark, Ohio, to enlist in the British Army. As he remarks: "You don't forget the old country when you get out there." lie has been "out there" since 1910.

THE JOURNEY HOME. Mr. Scragg set sail from the United States on November 25th, and the voyage was in many respects a remarkable one. It was made in darkness for one thing; and the company On board the vessel also was a peculiar one, being composed of people gathered from all parts of the States, most of whom were coming "home" to enlist in one or anott.cr section of the Brit- ish Army. On the way across the Atlantic, it was repeatedly rumoured that the vessel was being followed by a German cruiser. The pas- sengers did not see one, however, much to their relief. There was general goodwill 'amongst the company, both in the saloons and in the steer- age; in fact, altogether they "had a good time" throughout the journey.

AMERICA AND THE WAR. The American-born citizens, said Mr. Scragg, are in sympathy with the Allies generally; but, in accordance with President Wilson's appeal, they refrain from commenting upon the war. It is interesting to note that a copy of the Presi- dent's appeal is thrown upon the screen in the picture halls at each performance, before any war films are shown: The people of the United States also think, in the whole, that vic- tory for the Allies is certain; and the German- born citizens, who cannot be deceived any longer, are rapidly losing confidence. Despite the precautions taken by the Ameri- can Government against tho discussion of the war, many saloon brawls have, nevertheless, taken place, particularly between the svmpa: thisers with Russia and Austria respectively, though these brawls have been promptly quelled by the police on every poseible occasion.

Lecture for Belgians Realises over £l3.—A sum of £l3 13s. 9d. was raised in aid of the Bel- gian Relief Fund at the lecture on "Why we went to war," which was given by Mr. Henry Lancaster in the Church Room, Stockton Heath, on Friday week.—The lecturer said that the Germans were not losing any opportunities of impressing on the world, and especially neutral countries that the "treacherous and perfidious English had forced the war upon them—a peace- loving, peace-desiring people." (Laughter.) Of course, that was not true, but his hearers would see that this country was up against the stiffest proposition the world had ever known. We ought to fight for all we were worth. The great catastrophe which had befallen would have the effect of binding us all together more closely as an Empire and a people.—Many beautiful pic- tures were thrown on the screen, and patriotic vocal items were rendered by Messrs. W. Howard and A. Greenhough. Mr. F. Booth was an efficient accompanist.

P.T.C. , Members on the Range.—A squad of the members of the Stockton Heath Company of the Preparatory Training Corps put in a useful afternoon's miniature and service rifle practice on Saturday on the •Appleton Rifle Club's range. First they fired five rounds with the miniature rifles and then a similar number of rounds with the service weapons, the possible scores being 25 with each class of rifle. The service ammunition was kindly given by Mr. T. W. Fletcher, of Stretton. The individual scores made were :

Minia- ture. Service. Total. A. Hübne

193 148 341

Musketry-Instructor A. Shepherd was in charge of the men, assisted by Messrs. C. J. Smith and W. G. Featherstone. Prior to the rifle practice the trenching tools made by the Stockton Heath Forge were tested. About 80 members of the corps went for a route march round Grappenhall on Thursday night. The attendance was exceptionally good, considering that the weather was not very pro- pitious.

ELECTRIC LIGHTING. Do Warrington Seek a Monopoly?

In connection with the Warrington Corpora- tion's Electric Lighting Extension Order, the Deputy-Clerk reported, at a meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday, • that plans had been deposited by the Corpora- tion. The Town Clerk of Warrington wrote making application for the consent of the Council to the grant of the Warrington Corporation Elec- tric Lighting Extension Order for the supply of electricity to the Parishes of Stockton Heath, Latchford Without, Thelwall, Grappenhall, Lower Walton, Higher Walton and Appleton. The Chairman (Councillor C. E. Linaker) : Are they seeking a monopoly? The Deputy-Clerk: No, I don't think so. There is another company with powers in Stockton Heath and other parishes, but they have not as yet attempted to earry out a

scheme. Councillor Lister-Kaye: They are asking each parish practically to give them a mono- poly. We would not do it unless we were assured that the price would be so much per unit— The Chairman: And that the work be com- pleted within so. many years?

Councillor Lister-Kaye: Yes. Councillor Linaker, jun.: Does this affect the Mersey _Power _Company? .

Councillor Pilling: That has expired. The Chairman: I suggest that we appoint a committee to go into the matter, which is one of great importance to the townships concerned. The District Councillors of the parishes con- cerned, with the Chairman and Vice-chairman, were appointed a committee to go into the matter, and were given instructions to report at the next meeting.

GREAT SANK EY.

Traffic Diversion Causes Road Damage.—At a meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council on Wednesday, the Clerk read corre- spondence with the County Surveyor with refer- ence to the claim of the Council re the damage done to roads owing to the diversion of traffic over the Sankey Bridge.—The Surveyor reported that he had visited the roads with the County Surveyor, and he was under the impression that they would get a grant. Councillor Forshaw had done a great deal to push the matter for- ward. The wl yle matter had to he considered by the County Committee.

National Relief Fund Lecture.—A lecture, with limelight views, arranged by the Church of England Men's Society, and entitled "The 'Daily Mail' War Fund Pictures," was given in St. Mary's Schools, Great Sankey. on Thurs- day evening. The readings were very ably given by the Vicar (the Rev. J. R. Jones). The

chair was taken by. Mr. W. Furness, who said that the proceeds of the gathering were to be given to the National Relief Fund, and he was pleased to see so many there. The object of the fund, which had been inaugurated by the Prince of Wales, was to relieve civil and military dis- tress. In seven days over a million pounds had been subscribed, and in the same length of time over 0t400,000 had been paid out. It was to re- lieve immediate distress and also to help those thrown out of work by reason of the war, and also dependents of those who die in the war. In that quarter of the country, the speaker went on, labour was plentiful, but it was not so all over the country. Every penny of the fund would be given away, and for the purpose of distributing it, local committees had been appointed, with sub-committees in each parish, to deal with poverty or want, and they had power to deal immediately with any such cases. Dr. Murray was the representative for the Pen- keth district. The committee had been pro- vided with the necessary resources. There was no intention to hoard or capitalise any part of the contributions. The National Fund had the first claim on their duty and self-denial, and he hoped that the local committees would receive hearty support. The pictures thrown. on to the screen included views of Brussels before the war, Melle (a suburb of Ghent burned by the Germans), Louvain (with its a_rtTitreaNsuarmeus)r (with its fortress), h Bruges (with its with andfamous vbnoettlewfrYeor)fp. k s to , Ghent,tha thanks proceedt ings concluded es)_ Vicar, proposed by Mr. Furness and seconded by Dr. Murray.

ORFORD.

Iwim•••••••01•11 "Old Boys" at Church.—At Orford Church on Sunday evening the annual " Old Boys' " service was held, a large number of the members assembling at the School and marching to the church in a procession. Unfortunately, Mr. J. Cann, headmaster of Orford School and presi- dent of the association, was unable to attend owing to indisposition.—ln the church, the ser- vice was conducted by the Vicar, the Rev. G. Vale-Owen, who preached upon " Prophecy." Mr. T. S. Steel, treasurer of the association, read the lessons. There was a good congrega- tion.
