LYMM.

Wednesday Football.—Next Wednesday after- noon on the Lymm ground the Shop Assistants will play the Manchester Taxi-drivers in the Manchester (Wednesday) League Football Cora- petition. A good match is anticipated.

"Drunk and Incapable."—At the Lynam Police Court on Thursday morning, before lifeKrs. S. W. Gould (in the chair) and T. A. Warburton, Patrick Sweeney, an, Arlcy labourer, 'as fined five shillings for being drunk and incapable in the village on the preceding day.

A Job Open at the Gas Works.—At a niceting of the Lyrum Urban District Council on Mon- day it was decided, on the recommendation of the Gas Committee, to employ an addi.donal man at the Gas Works, at a wage of .11 Is. per week, the present lamplighters to do lamplight- ing only, and to be paid 12s. per week, the usual figure.

A " Rise" for the Waterworks Manager.— The Water Committee of the Council dccided at a recent meeting that it be a recommendation to the Council that the salary of the Water- works Manager be increased from 325. 6d. to 355. per week, with house, coal, gas and water.— The Council adopted the recommendatien at their meeting on Monday.

Tennis Club's Concert for Red Cross Fund.— On Saturday a concert, organised by the Lymm Western Tennis Club, was given in the Primi- tive Methodist Schoolroom in aid of the Red Cross Hospital Fund. There was a crowded room, and an excellent programme of vocal'and instrumental music, recitations, &c., was given. The artistes included Mr.. J. H. Brazendale and Miss Mary Brundrett.

Present at the Council Meeting.—The monthly moeting of the Lymni 'Urban District Council was held on Monday. Councillor Warburton presided, and there were also present: Coun- cillors F. Lockyer, J. H. Holt, W. G. Groves, C. E. Cowper, F. Ingham, R. H. Gotobed, A. Maclntyre and G. H. Parker,. with Messrs. W. Mullard (clerk), W. Timperley (assistant clerk), D. Mort (surveyor), T. H. West (gas manager), C. I. Barnes, (waterworks manager), and Dr. De Jong (medical officer of health).

Christmas Football for Red Cross Hospital.— The Lymm Wednesday and Saturday Football Clubs—most of whose members have enlisted since the start of the war, but who, it is anti- cipated, will all be home on furlough for Christ- mas—are arranging for a match to be played on Christmas Day in aid of the work of the Lymm Red Cross Hospital. The Working Men's Com- mittee have this week been applied to for the necessary permission to issue posters giving full particulars of this praiseworthy effort, and other advertisements.

Co-operative Society's Annual Gathering.— On Wednesday evening the Warrington Co- operative Society's annual gathering for Lymm members took place. Mr. Theodore Armstrong gave a lecture on "What the ` Wholesale ' has done for the workers," illustrated by limelight views. This was followed by an exhibition of various living-picture films, amusing and educa- tional. The hall was filled to its utmost capacity, and a very enjoyable evening was spent by the members present. Mr. IL Ward- man presided.

Butcher's " Meritorious Action."—At the Council meeting on Monday the Medical Officer reported that a, butcher had voluntarily re- ported a case of tuberculosis in a cow. That, in his opinion, was a very meritorious action.— Several members expressed similar opinions, and reference was made to the need of a slaughter-house, but, in the opinion of the 'majority of the members, the time for such an institution was not yet ripe. The Medical Officer said it was the first time in nine years that a case had been reported. The action of the butcher showed that there were honest trades- men in the district, and it would give the people a greater sense of security.

Question of Draining New Cottages.—At a meeting of the Sanitary Committee of the T.vmm Council the Inspector reported as to the proposed houses to be built at Heatley by Messrs. C. Moore and Co., who asked for an official letter with regard to what the Council proposed to do with reference to the laying of a sewer.—lt was decided by the commtttee to recommend that when the cottages were built, to the number of 20, that the Council would be prepared to lay a sewer for these cottages, pro- viding that Messrs. Moore would pay the cost of the flushing chamber and adjoining man- hole; also the charges for water for flushing purposes. At their meeting on Monday the Council adopted the committee's recommenda- tion.

Wesleyan Missionary's Visit.—The Wesleyan Foreign Missionary anniversary was celebrated Most successfully on Sunday and Monday.—On the former day, the pulpit was occupied by the Rev. W. Thompson Derry (superintendent min- ister of the Warrington circuit), who also de- livered an interesting address at the afternoon service. There were large congregations, and collections were taken on behalf of the foreign missionary work of the denomination.—On Monday evening a public tea was well attended, during which the Rev. J. A. Alexander (of the Hunan district, China) conducted a table-talk. Later, Mr. L. W. Young presided over a public meeting in the chapel, at which also Mr. Alex- ander spoke. The Rev. A. J. Morton, of Pad- gate, was also present. The collections were again for the foreign' missions.

Proposal to Re-Model the Gas Works.—The question of re-modelling the Gas Works is under consideration. The Council, at their meeting on Monday, adopted the minutes of the Gas Committee, which contained the fol- lowing items: "The Gas Manager presented a report on the condition of the works. It was resolved that it be a recommendation to the Council that a letter be written to Mr. J. H. Brierley, gas engineer, Longtown, Huddersfield, asking if he would be prepared to prepare plans and a scheme for re-modelling the Gas Works, and under what terms and conditions.—At a further meeting of the committee a letter was read from Mr. Brierley, in which he submitted plans, &c., and the Clerk, with the Gas Manager, was advised to write to other engin- eers. Councillor Ingham moved, and Councillor Maclntyre seconded, that it be recommended that Councillors Cowper, Groves, Gotobed, Parker, and Holt, with Mr. West, gas manager, be appointed a sub-committee to visit certain gas works and to report on a scheme for re- modelling the works, and that their railway fares and other expenses be paid.

A TOO-lIEATED WAR ARGUMENT.

Sequel to a Difference of Opinion in a Railway Carriage.

The story of a heated war discussion in a railway carriage on a train running from Oxford-road Station, Manchester, to Lymm, and its sequel, was related before the Stipendiary at the Manchester County Police Court on Mon- day, when Frederick A. Latham, a manufac- turer's agent, living at Lymm and carrying on business in Paton-street, Manchester, was sum- moned for assault and battery by James Rad- _ _ _

cliffe, a tea merchant, of flatl;bone House, Heatley, near Lymm. , Mr. Sam Hall, barrister, appeared for the complainant and Mr. T. W. Harris for the defendant.

Two Blows on the Back of the Ear. Outlining his client's case, Mr. Hall said that on Saturday, November 7th, the complainant and a man named Lowe had been having an argument about the war, in which the defend- ant joined in the railway carriage. The argu- ment became very heated, and Mr. Latham struck MrJ Radcliffe two severe blows on the back of the ear. When the train stopped at Broadheath Mr. Radcliffe asked a guard to re- move Mr. Latham as he had been assaulted by him. On arrival at Heatley Mr. Radcliffe went to see Dr. Thorpe and was afterwards confined to bed. Mr. Radcliffe gave evidence bearing out his counsel's statement. The guard of the train Said that when he was called to the carriage the third gentleman who

had been referred to, Mr. Lowe, said that there had only been a push, and, in consequence, he did not remove Mr. Latham.

Six to One and Half-a-dozen to the Other?

_ Mr. Lowe said he did not see- any blows struck. He thought the men were both alike. They were insulting each other. Mr. Radcliffe was insulting Mr. Latham rather too much. 217: Lo,thAm. Wen:LIAO ç trikc !dr,. Radcliffe, !Jut Om wit-ase gut httwuen them, Dr. Thorpe, of Mill-lane, ileatl2s-r, cieliOsed that Mr. Vadcliffe, when he saw him, had a swollen ear. He was greatly agitated, and in the surgery felt so faint that a stimulant had to be given to him. Witness attended him for a few days. He suffered from nerve shock due to slight concussion—the effect of a blow.

Defendant's Denial.

Going into the witness box, Mr. Latham denied that he struck the complainant. After he (witness) had made some remark, Mr. Radcliffe said: "I am going to agree with you; the Army is no good, and the Navy is no good." The witness replied: "If you put words into my mouth that I have not said, and attribute to me thoughts I have not thought, I will hit you." " I rose to do so," added Mr. Latham, "and got Mr. Lowe's foot in my chest, Mr. Lowe say- ing, Don't be a fool.'" Mr. Harris put it to the defendant that he was "a combative politician." Mr. Latham: If you knew of the eighty or ninety people who have asked me since why I did not kill him you would know how com- bative the man is (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19141205_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
Stipendiary on War and Lost Tempers.

- . In announcing that Mr. Latham would be bound over in the sum of £5 for six months, and that he would have to pay a guinea for the doctor and two guineas as advocate's fee, the Stipendiary said: " I am afraid the war, in sonic railway carriages, has been as fruitful a source of the losing of temper as politics are in a contested election. It is a great pity, because me ought all to keep our temper in these cir- cumstances. We ought not to imagine that we are at.the front, but should leave that work to our soldiers and sailors. They will see it done."

LYMM LIABILITIES. District's Indebtedness Quadrupled hi A Year.

At the monthly meeting of the Council on Monday the District Auditor's report for the year ended March 31st was read. It stated that the receipts for the year amounted to .210;270 4s. 2d., and the expenditure £10,024 13s. ,4d. Loans raised during the year amounted to £27,592, namely, £5,292 for sewerage and sewage disposal works, and £22,300 for the purchaSe of the waterworks, Parliamentary costs being in- cluded in that figure. The expenditure on thosa two objects amounted to .64,869 13s. 10d. and £21,119 19s. respectively. The unspent loan balances amounted to .11,709 14s. 6d., as against .1407 is. 4d. in March, 1913.

Debt Grows From £9,000 to £36,000. • The debt outstanding at the end of the finan- cial year amounted to £36,013 ss. 4d., as against £9,074 75. 4d. at March, 1913, being an increase of £26,968 18s. The balance to the credit of the district fund account amounted to £556 is. 11d., as against £473 7s. 11d. The cash in hand on the treasurer's general account amounted to £l7 16s. 6d. No provision had been made for the payment to the gas. works revenue account for street lighting of a sum of £5Bl 2s. Also, no provision had as yet been made for the cost of water used for street watering purposes. There was a credit balance on the gas works revenue account amounting to £2,322 13s. 10d., as against .£2,058 16s. 10d. and there was a sum of £222 7s. 9d. in hand. in connection with the waterworks revenue account it was stated that the balance standing to the credit of the new account amounted to £1,083 19s. 104., and there was a sum of £2lO 18s. in the hands of the treasurer.

PADGATE.

Concerning the Brooks.—lt was reported at a meeting of the Warrington Rural District Coun- cil on Wednesday that a meeting with the Sur- veyor and the Chief Inspector of the Irwell Joint Committee had been decided upon with respect to Padgate and Orford brooks.

The Condition of Green-lane.—Councillor Mather complained about the condition of Green-lane at the monthly meeting of the War- rington Rural District Council on Wednesday, and said it was worse than Manchester-road was some time ago.—The Surveyor said grsnie macadam was being sent to mend the road. The county traffic did the damage.

A Pre-War Tour in Germany.—A very enjoy- able lantern lecture on "The Land of the Teutons before the War" was given to the members of the Wesley Guild in the Wesley Hall on Thursday evening by Dr. Bennett. The lecturer described a holiday tour made by him from Ostend through Belgium, and the beauti- ful valley of the Rhine, on to Frankfort, and from thence to Vienna, and back through Strassburg and Paris, on to Dieppe. He told of many amusing incidents that befel him on the trip, and also recounted several interesting anecdotes, &e, The Belgian refugees, who have recently taken up their residence j, Padgate, were present at the lecture, and were given a cordial reception. A vote of thanks was passed to Dr. Bennett for his entertaining lecture. The Rev. A. Morton presided.

In Aid of the Wesleyan Church.—A sale of groceries, flowers, vegetables,. &c., promoted by the sisterhood of the Wesleyan Church, was held in the schoolroom last Friday week. The sale was opened at 4 o'clock by Mrs. J. Bennett, the chair being taken by Mrs. A. J. Morton. After the opening ceremony a beautiful bouquet was presented to Mrs. Bennett by Master Arthur Bate, 'and a similar presentation was made to Mrs. Morton by Miss E. Angus. A concert, consisting of songs, duets, quartets, re- citals, &c., was also given in the schoolroom, under the direction of Mr. J. Angus, the fol- lowing artistes providing the programme: The Misses C. Rigby, M. Blackburn and L. Duck- worth, and Messrs. T. Goostrey, R. W. Cook and J. Angus (songs), and Miss E. Morton (recita- tions). Refreshments, teas, suppers, &C., were provided, the arrangements being carried out by the Sisterhood Committee. The proceeds, which were in aid of the trust funds, realised £2O

From Cottage Homes' Boy to Farrier-Ser- geant.—Farrier-Sergeant Fearnley, who was formerly a boy in the Padgate Cottage Homes, has written from Egypt, where his regiment is now stationed, to his old teacher, and forwards with his letter a number of views of the Land of the Nile, some cotton pods which he per- sonally picked, and a piece of alabaster from the Sphinx, which he thought might be of in- terest to the children in the schools. In his letter he says: "I never thought when I was under you at the Cottage Home that I should come out here on foreign service. I have ten years' service to my credit. From what we hear we are likely to see the front. I hope we do, as I think it is awful the way the Germans are treating the women and children. It is Christ- mas (Mohammedan) Day here, to-morrow. In- stead of having snow we are having heat. To- day it is one hundred and four degrees in the tent, so you can imagine what it is like in the sun. We are under canvas on part of the desert. The horses are suffering from the heat. Out of 127 I have 56 in the sick lines. I should think we have nearly 4,000 horses and about 15,000 men."

GRAPPENHAL L.

CHESHIRE'S WORLD'S WAR- RECORD.

Sixteen DaNr'.,sreannedheNsi.ghts in the

Mr. F. A. Frost presided over a very good attendance in the Large Room, Grappenhall, on Saturday evening, when a most enjoyable and instructive lecture on the war was given by Colonel H. B. Hans Hamilton, of the Head- quarters Staff, Western Command, Chester.

Why Should the Fighting Be Left to the Few? After referring to the presence of the Boy Scouts and informing the audience that Colonel Hamilton had expressed his approval of their efficiency, the Chairman mentioned that a nephew of his, whom he had seen in Chester on Friday on his return from the trenches; had told him many things which were of interest, but which, Unfortunately, he could not relate to those present. He might say, however; that his nephew told him that General Smith-Dorrien had spoken to him in the trenches and told him that the Cheshire Regiment, of which he was a Member, and another regiment, had created a record in the military annals of the world by being in the trenches for sixteen consecutive days and nights. (Applause.) The thought struck him (the speaker) : Why should those men be in the trenches for so long; when there wtre so many thousands in the country who had not been to the front? Why should the work be left to the few?

===== 0002601_19141205_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
Allies' Mutual Admiration.

• The English soldiers had acquired an extra- -ordinary friendship with their Allies, his nephew informed him, and a mutual admiration and affection had been generated, whiCh would live long after the termination of the war, and be of great advantage to Belgium, France and England. • (Hear, hear, and applause.)

• Navy's Inestimable Service. Colonel Hamilton spoke of the great work accomplished by the Navy in snaking it possible for us to carry on our business, to be assured of our food supply and to sleep peaceably at night. The Navy, by its activities and vigilance, had saved us from the perils which had beset our grand ally, Belgium. "We declared war," continued Colonel Ham- ilton, "for what was called a scrap of paper' by the diplomatists of Germany, but we must not forget that it was 'a scrap of paper to which we had put our signature, and which, therefore, embodied our honour. Yes, for that little England really declared war against the great Power of Germany. Germany was jeal- ous of• us because our ancestors had obtained for us the richest and most luxuriant parts of the globe. I say we must thank God for our Fleet; because, if it were not for our men-o'-war Germany would have landed in this country long ago. She hates us more than she hates the Belgian nation. She wanted to do worse to us than she has done to Belgium." India's " Grand and Noble" Spirit.

The question was asked, continued the lec- turer, as to what would bring the war to an end sooner than anything else? We had blocked Germany from getting foodstuffs or material for explosives, and such blockade might bring the war to an end sooner than would have been the case otherwise. (Hear, hear.) The German Emperor thought he was dealing with a "rotten little Army," but he found that he had run his head against the British Empire. Take India, for example !, Another 100,000 men had been accepted, and in all parts of that great dusky Empire disappointment was expressed that still more could not be sent to the front. India had exhibited a grand and noble spirit, which they would do well to emulate in this country, and he hoped there would be a ready response to the appeal for more recruits. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

===== 0002601_19141205_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
Rector's Son on Active Service.

-..- After a cordial vote of thanks had been passed to the Colonel—on the proposition of the Rev. H. V. Pigot—he briefly responded and dis- tributed copies of the order of the garrison ser- vice held in memory of the late Lord Roberts in Chester Cathedral on the previous Sunday. Mr. R. C. Parr proposed a vote Of thanks to the Chairman, which was carried with enthu- siasm, and in his reply Mr. Frost referred to the fact that the National Service League had been very energetic in obtaining recruits for the Army, and mentioned that the Rector's son was serving his King and country, a fact which, he thought, ought to inspire and encourage every man in Grappenhall to do his best. (Applause.)

PAROCHIAL AFFAIRS. .

Ratepayers and Dangerous Condition

of a Laile.

At the cluarterlyi meeting of the Parish Coun- cil held in the Council Schools on Thursday night, there were present: Messrs. F. A. Frost (in the chair), A. Earwaker, W. A. Wooding, H. W. Wilde and W. Winstanley, with Mr. C. Pioult (clerk).

Sympathy With the Chairman. Mr. A. Lister-Kaye (the chairman of the Council) wrote regretting that j chill kept him from attending. His letter also urged the necessity of erecting a lamp at the end of Bell- house-lane. . .

Mr. Frost expressed the Council's regret at the Chairman's illness and hoped that he would soon be restored to health.

Ratepayers' Requisition. Apropos of the question of a lamp for Bell- house-lane, referred to in Mr. Lister-Kaye's letter, a requisition signed by tiventy-five rate- payers was handed in. It called attention to the dangerous condition of the end of Bellhouse- lane, expressed the opinion that a grave respon- sibility rested With the Council, and reminded the members that a cycle collision at the spot referred to had led to litigation. On the proposition of Mr. Wooding, sea-Tilted by Mr. Winstanley, it was agreed that the matter should receive immediate attention.

A Long-felt Want Supplied. Mr' W°°ding mentioned that the new lamp at the end of Cliff-lane had been erected, and that he had been personally thanked for the interest he had displayed in the matter. The lamp met long-felt want.

, The Charge for Electric Light. FRENCH'S PRAISE.

Sir John French, in the dispatch just issued describing the gallant and glorious stand near Ypres of the British Army against the flower of the German Army, says of the whole of the officers and men under his direction: Words fail me to express the admiration I feel \for their conduct, or my sense of the incal- culable services they render. I venture to pre- dict that their deeds during these days of stress and trial will furnish some of the most brilliant chapters which will be found in the military history of our time.

"A GLORIOUS EXAMPLE." ABLE-BODIED CIVILIAN (to Territorial) : "That ought to give you a good lead, mate." TERRITORIAL: "Yes—and I mean to take it. What about YOU?"

possible perfected, and such instruction as ear be, given to them in the brief time available in the use of machine guns, &e., is imparted. Several units have now been tent up to the front besides those I have already named, but have not yet been engaged. WARRINGTON TERRITORIALS.

From all the reports to hand we learn that the Warrington Territorials, both the original and the reserve battalions, are rapidly qualify- ing themselves for active service. When the 4th Battalion marched out from their first station in the North on the way to a new station in the South, they presented such an appearance that they stirred one of the most martial districts in these isles to a high pitch of enthusiasm. Their thoroughly businesslike appearance left no doubt that the Fourth Battalion South Lanca- shire Regiment would be equal to meeting all calls made upon them. Now that the policy of those in high command appears to be to entrust Territorials with the most important duties at the front, we have every confidence that, should our local men be honoured, they, like the few pioneer battalions, mentioned by Sir John French, will, by their conduct, justify his highest hopes.

TERRITORIALS DEBCT.

In the period covered by this dispatch Terri- torial Troops have been used for the first time in the Army under my command, adds Sir John French.

The units actually engaged have been the Northumberland, Northamptonshire, North Somerset, Leicestershire, and Oxfordshire Regi- ments of Yeomanry Cavalry; and the London Scottish, Hertfordshire, Honourable Artillery Company, and the Queen's Westminster Batta- lions of Territorial Infantry. The conduct and bearing of these units under fire, and the efficient manner in which they car- ried out the various duties assigned to them, have imbued me with the highest hope as to the value and help of Territorial Troops generally. Units which I have mentioned above, other than these, as having been also engaged, have by their conduct fully justified these hopes. Regiments and battalions as they arrive come into a temporary camp of instruction, which is formed at Headquarters, where they are closely inspected, their equipment examined, so far as

RIXTON.

Cemetery Caretaker Replies to Complaints.— The registrar and caretaker of Rixton Cemetery attended before the Warrington Rural District Council on Wednesday with reference to cer- tain complaints which were made against him in regard to his alleged inattention to duties.— lie pointed out that there was now more work to be done than when he was originally ap- pointed, and for -t2l) per annum for both offices he could not spend all his time at the Cemetery. No complaints had been made to him, but the Parish Council, he understood, had made lots of complaints.--The whole matter was left in the hands of the Parish Council.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY.

The visit of Mr. E. Seymour, of London, has long been an annual event with the Warrington Photographic Society, and one of the most ap- preciated. Tuesday's was the seventh consecu- tive lecture on flower subjects that he has given to the members, and still he found new matter in his favourite department of camera art and science to interest them. It was on colour photography as applied to flowers and fruit that he lectured on Tuesday, and although he has only had one season's practice in this new branch of photography, the results were not very far behind his achievements in mono- chrome. He favours the Paget system, chiefly on account of its smaller cost, but also because it allows some latitude to the personal choice of the worker. It is, therefore, not so mech- anical a process as most of the other methods for direct colour work. Some of the landscapes from Kew Gardens exhibited by the lecturer were greatly admired on account of the truth of the green tints—tints acknowledged by all Photographers as the most frequent sources of pitfall in this work. He also showed how a change of viewing screen improves or spoils a slide.—At the close of an interesting lecture, Mr. F. V. L. Mathias moved, and Mr. W. H. Hartley seconded, a vote of thanks to Mr. Sey- mour, which was heartily carried.—Mr. Seymour, in responding, conveyed the welcome news that he has reconsidered his intention of retiring from lecture work.

LYDDITE EXPLOSION IN YORKSHIRE.

Three lyddite magazines exploded near a picric acid works at White Lea, Heckmondwike, on Wednesday afternoon, with disastrous r^sults. Several men were killed and a number of per- sons are suffering from injuries. Part of the works was wrecked, and considerable damage was done in a considerable area by the concus- sion.

"PRINCESS MARY'S GIFT BOOK." A Charming Volume and a Worthy Cause.

We have received from the publishers, Messrs. Hodder and Stoughton, St. Paul's House, War- wick:Square, London, E.C., a copy of "Princess Mary's Gift Book." All profits from the sale are going to the Queen's "Work for Women" Fund, which is acting in conjunction with the National Relief Fund, and we understand that the demand has been so enormous that over a quarter of a million copies have been disposed of to the booksellers and newsagents of the 'country before publication. At the price of 2s. 6d. net this is probably the most wonderful volume ever placed on the market. From the frontispiece portrait of Prin- cess Mary, specially painted for the book, facing Her Royal Highness's thanks to "those authors and artists who have so generously con- tributed to the Gift Book," to the last page, with its delightful illustration by E. J. Sullivan, the book is full of beauty and charm. "Princess Mary's Gift Book" contains new stories, articles, and poems by many famous authors.

All these stories and poems are illustrated in the most elaborate manner. There are in all fourteen plates in colour by the world-famous artists, and over one hundred other illustra- tions. These paintings and drawings have been specially executed for the work.

Quite apart from the object for which it is published, "Princess Mary's 'Gift Book" is a volume which appeals to all. Every member of the family will find pleasure in its pages. It is indeed a volume which every household will treasure, one which, for years to come, will serve as a worthy memento of what the Empire wrought for the women who suffered through the ravages of the great war. All booksellers, bookstalls and newsagents supply this wonderful gift book.

TWICE ABSENTED HIMSELF. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Lance-Corporal George Wall was charged with being an absentee from the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, stationed at Great Crosby.Detective-Sergeant Holland said that ' when challenged prisoner said he was on leave. When he produced his pass it was obvious that it had been tampered with, the date, November 3rd, having been altered to November 31st.— The Chief Constable said the man was handed over to an escort on a similar charge a month ago.-4 The magistrates ordered Wall to be again handed over.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD THE WORLD OF LETTERS. JOURNALIST. •

Imart and Effectual. Lite other day, many miles from Warrington, • had first-hand information respecting the neanest man whom the national crisis has .yet.

tevezdell.—fieing-;;ry wealthy, he was prevailed' 4Don to add to his household three Belgian l'eftrvees—father, mother and daughter.. He said ;._ _

111 his haste that he -.v01t.1 receive and provide for these unfortunates, but, evidently repenting at leisure, summoned his servants apart, and addressed to them,a "touching" appeal for co- °Deration and self-sacrifice, the purport of which _

Was that they should contribute toward the taaintenance of the trio of foreign dependents who were being sheltered under that roof, and that they should consent to a systematic deduc- tion of a percentage of their wages on this account. One of them ventured to submit that

the three new-comers added appreciably to Work of the staff, and that these extra services, Cheerfully rendered, in.ght be put to the ?f domestics who were not overpaid and 1-sd little money to spare for the purpose suggested. The Master dismissed them with the remark that they might take time to consider his pro- Posal and let him have their decision on the following day. In due course one of their num- ber reappeared with the report that they had Unanimously resolved to fall in with his plan on the simple and surely not unreasonable , condi- tion that their quota should be regularly acknowledged with the rest in the list published ii the local Press. It was now the employer's ttirn to take time for consideration. Of course, the triumphant domestics do not expect to hear Shy more of the Scheme to enable their Master, While posing as a philanthropist, to withhold from their wages sufficient to meet the addi- tional expenseeincurred in the entertainment of the distressed strangers. Concerning 'War. There is a fine old triple argument which has done good service from certain points of view. They used to tell us (as in all likelihood they Will again and again) that if we criticised a war before it came, we were hampering the high and delicate negotiations of the diplomatists. If we criticised the war during its progress, we were encouraging the enemy and discouraging our Country's soldiers, who read. the Radical papers every day. If we criticised the war after its conclusion, we were opening old wounds and not allowing sleeping dogs to lie. Apparently there are in our midst a multitude of counbellors who are not to be deterred or misled by sua false reasoning. In last Saturday's " Exajniner " I teed first an account of an address at tho Priends' Meeting House, according to which n Worthy Quakeress had indulged in beautiful idealism with a freedom and to •in e tent thai- seems to have left little room for logic or prac- ticability.. It was ,perhaps just as well that °lie could read on another page of the same Paper a report Of a local deliverance on the same subject by the Wesleyan, President. The Rev. binsdale T. Young likewise considers war un- Christian, inhuman; irrational; but he recog- nises that after all that is not the point for Objet consideration at the present juncture. He shows that there are worse evils than war; he insists that the immediate duty of our young nianhood is actively to defend our righteous cause, and he frankly rejoices in the' fact that some 60,000 adherents of his own church are now 4hting for their country. As to war in the abstract, all my readers must be in cordial agreement with Mrs. Agatha Watts; but that good lady's theories are plainly inapplicable to the existing situation. One of these days I may trouble you with some ideas of my own on similar lines; as things are, it would be unpro- fi_table and vain, , ,

Respecting Fog. The recent prevalence of dense fog hereabout et me turning over in my mind much that I dave read on the subject generally, and I may reproduce some of it here for what the reader IllaY deem it worth. Will anyone deny that the ainoke nuisance, from which Warrington suffers °O grievously, and which is largely avoidable and therefore wasteful as well as seriously., mis- chievous otherwise, aggravates the fog evil? Can ,°oYthing be more miserable, and even risky, than having to travel iii 'thick, dirty fog such as Ito had lately? It has been pointed out that the cost of such fog as this is enormous. It is ex- Pensive in wastage of time, in extra wages, in lighting, and in many other ways : not least of t_liese in—life. Mr. John W. Graham, MA., of anchester, told a Leeds audience" a while ago 'hat fogs were both irritating and fatal, being responsible in London in the course of a week for as many as 1,000 deaths; they causeil inflam- alation,toul lung trouble. We have all heard of Pte "London particular," and its resemblance pea-soup. I have seen it stated that, while the Tubeikand the chemists profit by it, a single l'ay's fog in London costs from £7,000 to £B,OOO gas alone. But the London,fog is disappear- ; I have seen the figures and studied the 4eeompanying diagram. The records show that knee the winter of 1890-91 there has been a decline in fog prevalence so rapid and 'con- tinuous as to imply a great and seemingly per- anent change 'in the winter climate of the 4tetropolis. I think the striking improvement 41,ay fairly be attributed to the development of leaner methods of heating and lighting;.any- 40w, the fog nuisance necessarily has some tAnity to the black smoke scourge—and that is tl,ie lesson one would fain impress upon War- ngtonians whom it may specially concern. Iftecal Heroes—A Suggestion. Reverting to the war, I. note that there is touch to be done by helpers at home, and espe- °hilly by our gallant and indomitable fellow ,tountrymen on the Continent and at sea, before ritain and her Allies achieve the consumma. lion so devoutly to be Wished. We are well 44nred of it; it is a question of time—and, of °ourse, continued perseverance and patience. All °actions are co-operating heart and soul for the tiecomplishment of the tremendous task on t'llich we have set our hearts. Those whose sex age debars them from military service are by day anxiously considering what more ,11,°Y can do to comfort and enhearten the men "'JIG occupy the post of danger, enduring hard- lips and incurring risks unequalled in history as Sir John French's latest dispatch abund- "Hy shows. I have been thinking of a simple kethod whereby my young lady readers might Profitably employ some of their leisure and gives it good deal of pleasure to soldiers and sailors , ..°oin this district who are on active service; now that Christmas is nigh at hand it Strikes me as especially appropriate and season- Me that the scheme should forthwith material- My suggestion is that readers of the class Plicated, together with any others who care to `,4,°' a little in this way, should make a list of as dnY_local men engaged in the war as they feel

PPable of dealing with, and write at least one qtter to each of such men of their choice. Let le brave fellows be assured that friends at ;!artie have a keen personal interest in their welfare—that our admiration of their pluck and l'ldorance is boundless; that we are full of V'atitude to them for their prompt response to hlla call of Xing and country, and for their "triotic ardour and determination to do their . ta-tost in our 'defence; and that .we continue 4gerl 3 - to follow such accounts ,2f their valour 11 are allowed to come to hand r and that our t%fidence in their ultiaaate triumph remains 1033,1 and unshaken. Then tell them any bits of news or gossip calculated to interest old (llRlibours who have been cut off from such taciations for months. All this may appear tOrial to the stay-at-home, but I can promise ,113' who will take the trouble to write "Tommy" hti:',lack" a letter from home that it will be appreciated by the recipient. Ladies not hesitate to address thus familiarly p;11116. men personally unknown to them, for my Onosal is, not that they should append their 411atures to these epistles, but that they should 4.,:t0 anonymously—and I will explain my for this. New Books.

The Industrial History of Modern England. By G. H. Perris. London: Kegan, Paul and Co., Ltd. Pp. xix. 603. 6s. net.

It can hardly be questioned, we think, that the interest taken in economic. history is grow- ing, and this not alone, or chiefly in academic circles, but among the larger general public. Stimulated by various political and educational activities, an 'increasing number of people are realising that if they would understand aright the great movements of our time they must seek their origins in the past. They are com- ing to recognise that the social phenomena which at first sight appear so perplexing and portentous are the, outcome of long and often silent and underground process* whose his- tory it is necessary to know before they can be dealt with in a rational and hopeful manner. It is not surprising, therefore, that the output of books dealing with industrial and social his- tory is increasitrg. .There is ample•margin left before the supply will outrun the demand. The book before us is au attempt to Meet this demand. Its purpose, as Mr. Perris explains in his intrOduction, is to outline the facts and to interpret the spirit of the economic historY.of Great Britain in the last hundred and fifty years.

Mr. Perris commences his history with a 'description of the state of things at the close of the eighteenth century, and then tells the story of the transforthation of England during the nineteenth century. It is a story, tragic .yet inspiring, for what was accomplished during the last century, in social amelioration, While sur- passing any previous achievements, bears the promise. of far greater 'things to come. It is hardly, perhaps, sufficiently realised nowadays how terrible were the conditions of work in the early years of the nineteenth century. Mr. Perris gives the following accohnt of the treat- ment of young children in factories: "The children were kept working for fourteen and even sixteen hours a day; they were beaten for the slightest mistake or offence; and sometimes they were tortured by the overlookers, who would tie them to a beam close over the whirl- ing machines by way of teaching them to hold their feet up, or would rivet irons on their ankles and hips to teach them not to try to run away. Locked in the factory, while they Worked, and in neighbouring barracks while they .slept, these pitiful martyrs were as abso- lutely abandoned by their-kind as though they had been adult convicts on the way to Botany Bay, or negro slaves on the !middle passage." A long series of Factory Ads was necessary before the conditions of women and children were sensibly improved. . Mr. Perris, we think, is just in his judgments on ' the men responsible for many of the evils of industrial life in the early part. of last century.' They were men who sin- cerely believed in the doctrine of "laisser- faire," and to whom the idea of State regula- tion of industry was tiB absqr'd as the idea of interference with the solar system. They be- lieved that each individual pursued-his own in- terest, that liberty to this, end was an essen- 'Hal condition of progress; and that given liberty a harmony would result in which each person would obtain his just share in the general production of wealth. How grievous was their mistake is shown in Mr. Perris's account of the long and bitter struggles for factory inspec- tion and for the right of labour to combine. Every step taken since these' early days in in- dustriaFand social betterment has been opposed to the position taken up• by these " laisser. faire " manufacturers and economists. The liberty . they claimed was the liberty. of power. But the masses of the workpeople had, no liberty, precisely because, relatively to the employers, they were powerless. The whole story, which Mr. Perris recounts so sympathetically and in such detail, is the story of the attempts made during the last century to equalise the liberty of the' employer and employe. The Immense progress that has been made in social and political theory may well be gauged by the• profound develop- Ment in the conception of liberty since this period.. Mr. Perris justly appreciates the parts played by the great novelists, poets and agita- tors in the movements which he describes.

Mr. Perris's researches in the past he not made him pessimistic in regard to the future. "Industrial unrest' he says, "is the text of alarmed and pessimistic preachers. Could any result have been more confidently predicted of a generation of elemen- tary schooling? ,Does England repent her awakening? .Does she repent having established some equality Of start and opportunity? Is it not good to watch the approach of the day when the • romance which learning and research already hold for the privileged scholar will; reach the mechanic at his bench, when a library will be a /part of every home in the land, and none will speak of setting up a ladder ' for a few athletes to climb from the board school to the university, because the uni- versity will have been brought down from its Olympian heights and made accessible to every youth of decent abilities? The desire, without which this could not be, is growing, and it is good. Old England is burgeoning with young vigour. In particular directions the national genius may have reached higher in the past, but -at no time has there been such a volume and variety of talent, earnest work, and fruit- ful speculation. The results are already prodi- gious. Nor should even the rarer of these re- sults, in physical science and literature, be attributed to a Mere; academic movement; they are fruits of a vast growth of thought and energy which is fermenting throughout the whole body of the nation, It is impossible to do anything like justice to a book of this description in the space at our disposal. Mr. Perris covers so large a canvas with such an enormous mass of detail.that all we can do is to urge the reader to apply himself direct to Mr. Perris's work. He will find in it a mine of information relating to industrial de- velopment presented in a most entertaining fashion, and we venture to think he will' feel repaid for his trouble if he will give the book a careful study. Its usefulness is greatly en- hanced by a number of valuable appendices and p. very full bibliography.

STRUGGLE FOR SCUTARI.

It is an appalling picture, or series of pictures, that Miss M. E. Durham presents in a ‘strik- ing book in whigh she tells of her experiences In Albania and Montenegro during the Balkan War. There have been many volumes on that war written from various points of view, but none that for• graphic intensity, for deep im- pressiveness, will compare with Miss Durham's deeply-painful volume. The story of the inter- necine struggles in the Balkans has for so many years been one of charges and countercharges of " atrocities ".that many people at a distance must have felt that there was little hope of the diverse nationalities taking part in such a con- flict as the recent one witho'ut there being a recrudescence of such barbarisms as " civilised warfare" does not permit. But even so, it may be said that there has been a general feeling of sympathy with "gallant little Montenegro," and a firm confidence that the people of the Black Mountain would not disgrace themselves, Row that confiderfce was misplaced this book shows in all too painful fashion. " There are people, I believe, who still imagine that war 'brings forth fine qualities. To me it had appeared only as a sort of X-ray, which showed up pitilessly all that is most base, most foul, and most bestial‘in human nature. . . In the sacred names of Liberty, Civilisa- tion, and Christianity, the Montenegrin people, blood-drunk, lust-drunk, loot-drunk, had re- verted to primitive savagery—and in so doing had lost the very small idea of discipline they had acquired. Judging by their talk 'they pro- posed to live in future as a marauding army. Never fond of work, they declared that .they had conquered enough people to do the work for them, and looked forward to a life of something like slave-driving. A marked result of the war was the complete manner in which the Royal princes had lost their prestige." Montenegro and the Montenegrins, indeed, come very badly out of Miss Durham's narrative of the happenings in Scutari, in other parts of Albania, and along the Montenegrin frontier, and she pours scorn upon the claim that Scutari should fall as part of the spoils to King Nicholas's country, saying that the pretens ion was ridiculous. •The Turks took it from the Venetians in 1478, and "as the Petrovitch dynasty did not come into being as rulers till two centuries later, King Nicholas's assertion that the bones of his ancestors rest in Scutari seems—poetic !"

Madame If archesi's answer was, "There is only right and wrong." The forcing of registers should be studiously avoided, and proper classification should always be regarded most seriously. MUNICIPAL HELP.

How Dependent We Are on

Communal Services.

COUNCILLOR OSBORNE'S ADULT SCHOOL

LECTURE.

Councillor Osborne, speaking at the current events class of the Sankey Bridges Adult School dealt with the -question of " our municipal ser- vices." _ . .

• Some of Ili, he said, scarcely realise the extent to which we are indebted to our municipal ser- vides.. Mr. B. R. Suthers, in "Mind your own Business," put this matter in a very interest- ing way. "Think, for instance," he wrote, "of the citizen of Glasgow, who is generally con- sidered to be the richest in municipal institu- tions in the kingdom. A citizen of Glasgow may live in a municipal house. He may walk along the municipal Street, or ride on the muni- cipal tramcar,' and ,watch the municipal dust- cart collecting the refuse which is used to fer- tilise the municipal farm. Then he may thrn into the municipal market, buy a steak from an animal killed in the municipal slaughter-house, and cook it by municipal gas on a municipal gas stove. For his recreation he has the choice of municipal libraries, municipal art galleries, and municipal music in the municipal parks. Should, he fall ill lie can ring up his doctor through the'municipai telephone, or he may be taken to the municipal hospital on the muni- cipal ambulance by a municipal policeman. Should he be so unfortunate as to get on fire, he will be put out by a municipal fireman using municipal water, after which he will perhaps forego the enjoyment of a municipal bath, though he may find it necessary to buy a new suit in the municipal old clothes market." In the midst of all this municipal happiness he would probably fall down dead with aston- ishment when he learnt that Glasgow had no cemetery. If lie had died in Warrington, we, at least, could have provided him with a muni- cipal resting-place. _ Object of Municipal Undertakings. . . . .

In the non-trading departments of corporation life come the provision of police, the mainten- ance of high roads, the lighting of public streets, free education of children, health and recrea- tion, all those departments which are provided for entirely by contributions from the rates or by taxation. _ . . .

_ Under the heading of trading are included those economic functions which are performed primarily for the benefit of individual members of the comMunity, and net for the community as a whole, as, for example, the provision of gas and electricity, tramways, water, baths, mar- kets, where the products or services are paid for entirely or partially by the consumers or users. _ _

Municipal undertakings should provide the best Possible facilities at the lowest possible price consistent with a due regard for the con- venience and the comfort and the health of the community, and the Money needed for the ad- ministration of the toWn's affairs should be raised openly and frankly by a proper system of rating. _ _ .

The Trading Surplus. The expediency of relieving the rates with any surplus from' profitable undertakings, after making careful provision for depreciation, sink- ing fund and interest, is questionable. It simply meant that one section of the community was over-charged in order to relieve another of its responsibilities. Further, it is a short- sighted policy, so far as the Gas Committee is concerned, at any rate, for it has been proved over and over again that a reduction in price has meant a larger consumption and a bigger profit. By far the most logical and equitable arrangement is to give the benefit of any real surplus to those from whom it is directly de- prived—the users. ' There was a temptation to councillors to incur expenditure, in the light of prospective profits from these undertakings, which would be more carefully considered if direct taxation was in- sisted upon to Maintain it. Before undertaking the expenditure of large sums of money, ways and meanewould be more carefully considered, and the cost would be of some moment. Large 'sums of money have been spent in the past On the vurelinse of properties for street improve- ments, and to-day the fallacy of such procedure is very noticeable. He was not opposed to necessary improvements—one had to look ahead —but the mistake in the past' had been that there had been no completed scheme to work to, and many mistakes had been made, natur- ally. His contention was that so long as respon- sibility is relieved by large sums of money being handed over year after year, from these under- takings there was likely to be no appreciable difference in the outlook.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
RIGHTEOUSNESS OF Tiff FIRST BLOW:

Mr. Sherburn Speaks of Bernhardi at Stockton Heath.

"The Teaching of Bernhardi " was the sub- ject of a lecturette by Mr. Sherburn at the Stockton Heath Adult School. The speaker showed how in later years Krupp and the Kaiser had come to dominate the Ger- man Empire. Bernhardi had been treating war as a gospel. War, in his view, was not only necessary, but healthy and morally obligatory. He thought of Christianity as a religion for in- dividual relationships, but quite unsuitable for international relationships. . - .

It followed that if war were good in itself, there was righteousness in getting in the first whack. But it was curious that though bun- 1 hardi preached aggression as a national duty, he wanted his country to avoid being judged the.l akgressor by civilisation. It therefore became 1 a secondary duty to compel other nations to seem to take the initiative in war. "World: power or downfall" was a phrase which en- shrined the teaching of the General founded on the philosophy of the Prussian scholar, Treitschke.

One corollary of this new gospel of might was that a free Press was a danger to the State. Another was that treaties might be torn np by any nation, which found them clashing with self-interest.

In the discussion, armaments, commercialism and Parliamentary responsibility came under review. Of course, it was recognised that thou- sands in Germany had not bowed the knee to the newest Baal. After the Morocco crisis Bernhardi issued his book for the express pur- pose of waking his countrymen to what he be- liend to be their destiny, if only they would accept his doctrine that might is the only right. In the Bible lesson, which followed, it was intereking to consider the Imperial background of the life of Paul, and to note both his pride of Jewish race and Boman citizenship, not to speak of his other pride of earning his bread by handicraft.

The Stockton Heath School made a collection to help support three families \of Belgian refugees now being, entertained at the Adult School guest house at Hayes Farm, near Clitheroe. The school is devoting a proportion of profits on whist drives this winter •to local cases of distress.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPORTANCE OF BREATH CONTROL

The Stockton Heath Women's Adult School were favoured with a visit from Mr. Levi Richardson, who delivertd a lectiirette on voice production. Breath control, he said, was the true basis in voice production. Breathing should be deep, full and well established, with care, under the guidance of a conscientious teacher, until it gradually becomes volitional'. Entire command of the breath was essential for artistic and effective performance, leaving the singer (or speaker) free to colour the tone, to articulate the words, and give thereby the full- est expression. It was a good habit not to sing with the full voice; only rarely did circumstances demand the extreme power of a voice. The student should be sufficiently alert to select the best method. It was said that all voice teachers claimed to have "the best."

GERMANY WHEN THE JWASK IS OFF.

Some Striking Articles Translated from German Newspapers.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BID FOR WORLD POWER.

Tuesday's newspapers relieved the slowness of things by reporting an interview with the Crown Prince of Germany, Written by an American, who had access not only to his Headquarters, but to the inmost chambers of his guileless heart. As-an effort to gratify •an inquisitive world and to prove that rumour is a lying jade, the result leaves nothing to ba desired. " He broke all precedents," says the innocent journalist, "not only in granting me an inter- view, but in receiving me at his Headquarters in the field." Most manifestly His Royal High- ness had something upon his mind. It came in a -rain of adjectives. "This is a stupid, sense- less, unnecessary war—a war not wanted by Ger- many, I can assure you, but forced upon us." Again, "There is no war party' in Germany —nor has there ever been. . . As to mili- tarism, that is merely a phrase with which Eng- land is endeavouring to scare end arouse the world against us." And with a note of sadness and regret, he expressed his disappointment that America should fail to reveal her usual love of fair play. The charges levelled against him and against German conduct were too painful to be discussed. Looting? Perish the thought! The royal head moved slowly from side to side as the words were uttered.; "The English Press says I steal. Really, der people believe such things of me? Do they think me capable of stealing, or of permitting my soldiers to loot French homes?"

There are sentiments which' are prepared for export, and it will be charitable to place these under that category. Very much more inter- esting is the information which is served up in Germany for consumption at home. Here, at least, the mask is off, if indeed it has ever been on, and writers write and readers read without any thought that perfidious Britons may lay hold upon stray copies of the newspapers. Here, for instance, is an article by Major- General von Disfurth, contributed to the "Ham- burger Nachrichten." It can be quoted without comment.

: acknowledge no such jurisdiction. Our might shall create a new law in Europe. It is Ger- many that strikes. When, she has conquered new domains for her genius, then the priest- hoods of all the gods will praise the good war. We stand at the beginning of a struggle whose development and duration no one can foretell, and in which as yet no combatant has been overwhelmed.'

" It is the statesman's 'duty to point out the goal for the sword, and to prepare quietly, skill- fully, and strongly for the conclusion of peace; and that duty can be fulfilled only by one who has not, by short-sighted errors, lost the confi- dence of his countrymen. "Germany is not making this war to punish sinners, or to free oppressed peoples, and then to rust in the consciousness of disinterested magnanimity. She sets out from the immov- able conviction that her achievements entitle her to demand more elbow room on earth, and wider outlets for. activity., . _ .

" The Powers still live from whom she wrested her advancement, and some of them have re- covered from their debilitation. Spain and the Netherinds, France and England have siezed. possessed and colonised great stretches of the most fertile soil in the world. Now Germany's hour has struck, and she must take her place as the leading Power. " Any peace which did not secure her the first 'position would be no reward for her efforts. However many shining milliards it brought into her treasury, the fate of Europe would still de- pend on the will of the United States. She is fighting for herself alone, and yet she is sure that all men who desire the good of the world will rejoice in the result. For there must be an end of the policy which has frightened off al free-born people from fellowship with the strong- est of Continental Powers.

"What Germany needs is free access to the ocean, and unquestioned validity for her spirit and speech, for her goods and her bills of ex- change." The purpose of Germany, says Herr Thirden, is so beneficent that it must be taken for granted that all the processes by which it is gained are beneficent. Thus Belgium must regard herself as a favoured nation, for a war waged with such high aims must confer great benefits upon the conquered ones._ _

We have heard this before. It reveals the spot in the Prussian eyeball and nothing more. The fact that the plucky journalist cannot see the woes of Belgium does not make him less inter- esting when he tells us what he does see. Here in a few sentences is Germany's programme.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
BARBARIANS-AND WE KNOW IT.

"No object whatever is served by taking any notice of the accusations of barbarity levelled against Germany by their foreign critics. Frankly, we are, and must be, barbarians, if by this word we understand those who wage war relentlessly to the uttermost degree. There is nothing for us to justify, and nothing for us to explain away. Every act, of whatever nature, ammitted by our troops for the purpose of dis- couraging, defeating, and destroying our enemies is a brave act, a good deed, and is fully justi- fied. There is no reason whatever why we should trouble onrselves about the notions con- cerning, us in other countries. Certainly .we should not worry about the opinions and feelings held in neutral countries. Germany stands supreme, the arbiter of her own-methods, which must in time of war be dictated to the world. "It is of no consequence whatever if all the monuments ever created, all the pictures ever Pain,ted, all the buildings ever erected by The great architects of the world, be destroyed, if, by their destruction, we proinote Germany's vic- tory over the enemies who have vowed her com- plete annihilation. "War is war, and must be waged with severity. The commonest, ugliest stone placed to mark theplace of burial of a German Grena- dier is a more glorious and venerable monument than all the cathedrals of Europe pht together. They call us barbarians. What of it? We scorn them and their abuse. Let them cease to talk of the 'Cathedral of Rheims and of all the churches and all the castles in France which have shared its fate. These things do not inter- est us: Our troops must achievo victory. What else twitters?" HERR HARDEN'S,ADMISSIONS. There are few journalists in Germany mere influential than Herr" Harden. There are few more jaonest. His paper, "Die Zukunft," has told the truth about- the Kaiser in a way that has made him very familiar with the Civil Courts and libel proceedings. With a courage that does him credit, he scorns the hypocrisy that pretends that Germany did not want the war. The whining of pious Royalty gets on his nerves. Why not, he says in effect, tell the world that it is our programme, that we engi- neered it, and that we are going to see it vic- toriously through? Here are his own words; 'Let us drop our miserable attempts to excuse Germany's action. Let us have done with paltry abuse of the enemy. Not against our will, and as a nation taken by surpriSe, (lid we hurl our- selves into this gigantic venture. • "We willed it; we had to will it. We do not stand before the judgment seat of Europe; we / ,

ROME OF THE RENAISSANCE.

Why Art Was Called to the Church's

Service.

Continuing the series of popular lectures which, under the auspices of the Warrington Museum Committee, he is delivering upon "Rome, the Eternal City," Mr. J. E. Phythian, in the Co-operative Hall on Saturday evening, gave an interesting and instructive discourse dealing with‘" Rome of the Renaissance." There was an excellent attendance.

, RISEN FROM A WILDERNESS. The Lecturer, having alluded lo the internal strife which, in the Middle Ages, had been ex- perienced in Rome, said that, just before the beginning of the fifteenth century, it had been practically a wilderness: dogs had fought with wolves quite close to St. Peter's. In 1420, how- ever, Pope Martin V.—a strong man—began to restore- the city to something like its old con- ; and Pope Nicholas V., who followed, almost rebuilt it. The Pope believed that, to impress the people and to obtain authority over them, there must be magnificence and splen- dour. • Art was therefore brought into the ser- vice of the Church, that it might literally stand well in the eyes of the people—a sound policy, for the purposes of a world-wide Church., In the sixteenth century came the revival of learning—the Renaissance. Martin Luther went to Rome, and what he saw there turned him to Protestantism. This was followed by the Catholic reaction—purification from the inside.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
A CIVILISING POWER.

Rome was the Eternal City, and even those who were outside its pale knew its value as a civilising power: something that was world- wide, they might be sure, was something of 'value. lie wished to spend the time that night , with the Roman art, for its own sake. Of this, a great example was the Church of St. Peter, the dome of which had been designed by Michael Angelo—who also did much of the inside paint- ing. Many architects designed the church— which was begun, in'the fifteenth century, upon the spot where St. Peter was supposed to have I been crucified—and it, took a century and a half to complete, the design being changed from time to time. It was an enormous building; and, frankly, he did not like it—though that was a daring thing to say—because it was too gorgeous, and suggested a Striving for effect. I Still, one felt more confidence in putting one's money in a large, rather than in a small hank; and sty it was with the Catholics and St. Peter's, , although it was too gorgeous for them to learn ' to love. .

Passing on to the Vatican, the Lecturer said that it was built in the sixteenth century, and was surrounded by colonades. The roof of the Sistine Chapel, painted by Michael Angelo, gave the story of Christianity from the Creation to the Flood; and Raphael, Angelo's great con- temporary (who was only 37 when he died), painted other parts of the building. SOLIRCE OF ROMAN ART.

Many private -p-alite—es-;iiid Museums also con- tained much of the Roman painting and sculp- ture. Yet most of the Roman art was not done by the Romans-themselves, but came from out- /side the city. Rome herself produced hardly any artistic wealth. /. .

_ As usual, the lecture was illustrated with a number of excellent lantern slides, photographs having been. taken in all parts of the buildings described.

GERMANY'S DREAMS.

" What territory could Germany take from France or Russia that would be of any particu- lar use to the German people? No; what Ger- many is fighting for is not French, Polish, Rutherniais, Esthopian, Lithuanian provinces— nor is it milliards of indemnities. The object is to hoist -the storm flag of the Empire on the nar- row channel that opens and closes the way to the Atlantic."

With Calais conquered, Germany will make her bow to Europe, and to the world and along with it a moving speech. Herr Harden gives us the precise words: " You now see what Germany's power and genius can do, and will in future think twice before you attack us. Germany demands noth- ing more of you—not even the repayment of her war_ expenses. _

" For these she will find her recompense in rthe wholesome terror her victories have in- spired. If you want anything more from us, you may be sure that we will never refuse a challenge to fight. We will remain in the Bel- gian Netherlands, to which we will add the nar- row strip of coast as far as Calais. This done, we will voluntarily close the war, from which we have nothing more to gain, after having vin- dicated our honour. .

"'We will return to the joys of work, and only take up the sword again if yon try to force from our grasp what dm- blood has won for us. We demand no formal conclusion of peace, no parchment and seals. Our prisoners we set free. You can keep your fortresses if you think them of any value and bold it worth while to rebuild them. To-morrow life resumes its ordinary course.'" . A. M.

WOMAN'S WORLD.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

made by them for Britain and France. It is feared that the Flemish lace industry will suf- fer greatly from the war, in devastated Bel- gium. For centuries the beautiful filmy lace called Malines has been made in that city, and the neighbouring villages, all now lying in ruins, and their former inhabitants scattered far and wide. It is doubtful if the art and industry will over be restored, and specimens of 3.llalines lace should be treasured by their owners, for they will be valuable possessions in the future. The flax for Flemish laces is grown in Brabant, and the thread is steeped in the River Lys, whose waters give a better result than those of any other stream in Bel- gium.

War and the Price of Furs. For muffs and stoles, fur substitutes are not only popular, but very successful. When trimmed with frills of pleated black chiffon, or heavy corded silk, these sets are not only com- fortable, but attractive and elegant. When civet cat and leopard skin first became fashion- able, they appeared barbaric to unaccustomed eyes, but familiarity with their peculiar char- acteristics has led to an appreciation of their beauties, for both are very handsome pelts. Imitations are marvellous, and the sets are so very moderate in price, and, at the same time, so attractive, that their popularity is not sur- prising. As a trimming, civet cat, real, or in cloth, is at its best. But since the war began there has been a marked reduction in the price of all peltry in demand, and if real fur can be afforded the present opportunity should be seized, for it is probable that such furs as red fox, wolf, natural musquash, and squirrel will not be procurable next year at this season s prices.

fur and velvet, is very like a Dutch bonnet; it should be worn low on the forehead, and is more suited to a youthful fate than one of elderly appearance. Seasonable Dressing Gowns. • • •

With the approach of the wintry season warm dressing gowns and jackets are a necessity, and great impovements b Ivo been made in the 3e garments during the last two years. The quilted dressing gown now built is far superior in every way to its predecessors. A much better quality of silk is used, And the stitchery is much more ornamental and elaborate than formerly, Louis Seize designs and floral trails being often seen. Zenana lambs' wool gowns have It pretty cape like collar, and the daintiest hoods in net and lace. The latter are rarely dead white in tint, for that is a trying shade to the generality of complexions, a deep Paris tone proving much more becoming. Some of these cosy gowns are trimmed with swansdown, othcrs with teather trimming, and yet others with marabout. Fink Zenana lambs' wool, combined with white marabout, is charming. Rest gowns are equally alluring, black or coloured satin, with wool back, is an ideal material for such a purpose, whilst chiffon, velvet and velveteen are also largely employed.

Fashions for the Young. Children's dress invariably follows the lead of their elders, consequently the tunic sash, belt at hips, and even the flowing cape, all find a place in the schemes designed specially for the little people. Should short skirts be v orn by grown-ups, the children wear the shortest of dresses; whilst their millinery always re- flects the styles of adults. rims the velvet hat predominates in the children s tiihlinry department; and is charming in its sbnplizity and almost entire lack of ornamentation. Gold is a favourite trimming, and the little gold tassels on the three-cornered hats for girls in their early teens are very novel and smart. The military influence is noticeable hete also, and both boys and girls wear the Guards' coat in khaki cloth, with a military collar and double- breasted front. But the long coat has a rival in a very pretty short jacket in plain colour, generally worn over a check or a plaid pleated skirt. Little black velvet coats of a waistcoat type, therefore sleeveless, are often worn in- doors, with ninon or net long sleeves. With those, coloured skirts are the most effective, and frequently they are edged with fur.

By MARJORIL

Queen Alexandra's Birthday. Queen Alexandra's seventieth birthday falls ' under sad conditions, so many of her relatives being at daggers drawn, and engaged in the war. The Tsar of Russia is the son of her favourite sister, and in the opposing Army is the Duke of Brunswick, the son of another sister. The Kaiser is her nephew by marriage, although one for whom she never had a liking, and the late Prince Maurice of Battenberg was similarly related to her. Then, whilst her nephew, Prince Albert of Schleswig-Holstein, son of our Princess Christian, is fighting with the Germans, her grandson, -the Prince of Wales, is with the British Army at the front. His Royal Highness is now with General French, although he would have preferred the work of an officer in his regiment, the Grenadier Guards. The Prince, when at Oxford, joined the University Officers' Training Corps, and took part in drills, narches, and field-days.

A New Peeress In Her Own Right. Lady Roberts is a new addition to British peeresses in their own right. This lady will now have a place in the House of Lords, and a seat in Westminster Abbey, and may occupy the latter on the occasion of a Coronation, or any national celebration. It is curious that so few of our great soldiers and sailors have left sons to succeed to the titles they have won. The Duke of Wellington, Lord Nelson, Lord Wolseley, Lord Roberts—whose son perished in the Boer War—died without heirs male; whilst Sir John French and Admiral Tellico° have daughters only.

The Flemish Lace Industry. Since medireval times the Flemings have been famous for their skill in lace-making. Coloured Furs.

The coloured furs of last year are usnd by milliners; monkey and rabbit are dyed in he palest tints, and combined with black or col- oured velvet. A very smart toque was of black monkey fur, with deep violet velvet, and a large bunch of violets of the same hue. Monkey fur is naturally straggling, but since its intro- duction it has been very much improved. It is now so cleverly manipulated that it looks quite soft and fluffy. HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

U

Brass Polish. If you put a tablespoonful of powdered chalk in a cup and mix it to a cream with turpentine, then add a teaspoonful of liquid ammonia, and put it in a tin, it makes an exesllent I.rass polish. It will also make the Ills cf your saucepans shine like silvor.

etiLl

To Whiten Ivory. Irory knife handles which have yellowed may be whitened by soaking them in alum w'ater which has been boiled. The water must he allowed to cool and the ivory should be left soaking for an hour. Then brusli them with a small brush and dry thoroagii!y with a soft towel.

' Getting Back. , "What men think," remarked the knowing 1 , woman, "causes fully one-third of all the • trouble in the world." "Yes," rejoined the mere man, "and what r .' women say causes the other two-thirds."

Quite True Eve: "Adam, I want you to remember one thing." Adam: "What's that?" Eve: "That when I am looking the eyes of half the world are upon you."

" Washing " Hands. If a little vinegar is added to the rinsing water when washing clothes, the hands will not become red and rough. Another plan is, if the hands and fingers appear " spongy " after being long in the water, to take a spoonful of granu- lated sugar and rub well into the hands. The sugar draws the water out of the hands, and if rubbed in for a minute or so, and the hands washed to take away the stickiness, you will be able to do any needlework or other work, which is often impossible on washing day unless this plan is adopted.

Lunch Loaf (Without Eggs). Two pounds of flour, half a pound of butter, one pound of sugar, one pound of sultanas, a quarter of a pound of candied peel, a seasoning of nutmeg, mixed spice and salt. Rub the butter into the Sour, add the sugar and fruit and the seasonings, and mix with half a pint of milk, adding a teaspoonful of carbonate of soda dissolved in two tablespoonfuls of milk at the last moment.

.. -, Apple Sponge. ~ Five medium-sized apples,' Peeled, cored , stewed whole in If pints of water and atwm-,1) '. tablespoonfuls of sugar. Melt five sheets of , - gelatine in a little water. When apples are I cooked, place with five penny sponge cakes in ' a wetted mould. Strain the gelatine into the ' apple juice, pour on to the apples and sponge cake, set overnight. Turn out on glass dish , and serve with whipped cream.

Cheap Nourishing Soups. Take one pound of lentils and wash them thoroughly in three or four waters till they are perfectly clean; soak them all night in cold water; then put them into a pot, with the water.in which they have been soaked, and add it as much more water as makes ten breakfast- , cupfuls. When this boils add about half a '• pound of carrot or turnip and two large onions —all cut up roughly—and a small pinch of car- tonate of soda about the size of a pea. Allow it to boil 2i hours, then press through a colan- *tr. Return it to the pot to warm. Season

with pepper and salt. This soup is of a pleas- ant taste and requires no meat of any kind or bones, and is very nourishing. Half a pound neck of mutton, 4 potatoes, 2 good-sized onions, 1 large red carrot, lb. rice, pepper and salt. Put the mutton, well washed, into a pot with ten breakfast-cupfuls of water; when it boils add the onions chopped up finely, the potatoes peeled and cut into very small pieces, and the carrot grated; add a teaspoon- ful of salt and let the whole boil two hours. Then wash a tea-cupful of rice (which is one quarter of a pound) and add it, and allow the whole to boil half an hour longer. Season with enough pepper and salt, and the B°4 is ready. This soup has a good flavour and is very cheap.

Temperance Wine. A very nice temperance wine can be made cheaply at home from Ma-son's Milo Essences, and we specially recommend the Ginger flavour. A 6d. bottle of this makes, with the addition of sugar, a gallon of delightful non-alcoholic Wine suitable for Christmas and New Year parties. We .recommend our readers to try a sample. We notice Messrs. Newball and Mason, Notting- ham, are giving with each sample an attrative money box.

Christmas Pudding. Take three-quarters of a pound of flour, two heaped-up teaspoonfuls of Borwick's Baking Powder, two ounces of bread-crumbs, one and a half pounds of suet, two pounds of raisins, one pound of currants, ten ounces of sugar, two ounces of almonds, one pound of mixed candied peel, salt and spice to taste. Mix the ingred- ients well together, and add six eggs, well beaten, and three-quarters of a pint of milk; divide in two, and boil eight hours.

HERE AND THERE.

The man who can be on time to breakfast comes as near being a good husband as there is.

Some men are so attentive to their wives that you might think they weren't married.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information in Popular Language.

Effects of a Complete Meat Diet. In its famous march to Moscow, Napoleon's army suffered from the effects of a meat diet, as shown by the following quotation from Four- flier's "Napoleon the First." "With meat as their sole food, for no bread and vegetables were to be had, the troops became so sickly that they broke down on the march. Finally dysentery broke out, and carried of thousands."

Vegetable Diet in Colitis. A person suffering from colitis should use an anti-toxic diet. Let it consist very largely of fruits and vegetables. Lettuce is especially good. Even bran and such coarse articles as turnips, cabbage and beets will act as a broom to sweep away the foul matter which is irri- tating the bowels. It is the rotting, putrefying ftecal matter adhering to the wall of the intes- tine which occasions the irritation, and by means of a rather coarse vegetable diet these materials may be scraped off, svept on and car- ried away, and the habitual rhythm restored, and the intestines given a chance ro heal. This is no new idea. When in Vienna, some two years ago, at Professor von Noorden's clinic, I inquired, "What does Professor von Noorden do for colitis?" "Well," said Doctor Yalta, his assistant, "we believe in a vegetable diet for colitis. The patient must use a great deal of fresh vegetables." "What," I said, "coarse vegetables?" "Of course; certainly, coarse vegetables." "Lettuce?" "Yes, indeed." "Cabbage?" "Certainly, and brown bread."

A HEARTY INVITATION TO join this Corner is extended to all children who read it regu- larly.
