ALL ABOUT OUR MEMBERSHIP. Mr DEAR CHILDREN,— This week I received a,nice letter from a little nephew of mine, in which he told me quite a lot of news about himself. This pleased me very much, for I am always delighted to hear from any of my young friends. But I mention this• letter particularly, because it finished like this: " What a lot of nieces and nephews you must have by now. Have you ever counted them F"

Well, I must confess that up to then I had not: and that letter made me think it was a shocking thing for your old uncle not to know exactly how many children he had in his family. So I waited until nobody was looking; and then I unlocked the drawer of my desk, in which I keep the Big Book. This I took out, put on my spectacles, took just one more look round to make sure that nobody was-about— and then counted up the names. How many do you think there are? Well, I will not tell you just yet; but there are a lot, as my nephew guessed; and, better still, there are almost as many boys as there are girls, so that it is evenly matched. . _

• You would be surprised to see some of the addresses, too. Not only have we members down in the South of England, as well as up in the North; but we even have one who lives right away in Ontario, Canada. I should like to hear from him very much. Our membership is growing. This week I have the pleasure of enrolling: IDA BARLOW, 50, Lancaster-street. AMY BARLOIT, 50, Lancaster-street, EVA BENNETT, 36, Lancaster-street. ELSIE OAKLEY, 38, Lancaster-street.

MILLIE -JENKS, 40, Lancaster-street. This, of course, is a splendid increase. Let us hope it will be kept up. Your loving UNCLE NED.

Fruit for Breakfast.

There are people who believe in the "no breakfast" habit. So long as it suits their condition and work one has nothing to say against it. But for the majority of English peopie, particularly those of robust habit and active disposition, a good breakfast is generally a good beginning for a good day's work. Fruit should certainly be included. If the individual wisely omits altogether the flesh of the swine or other creatures, clean or unclean, he—and also she—should give the chief position to fruits and nuts. They are wholesome, healthful, pleasant to the palate, and a perfectly natural food. For- tunately such foods can be obtained in abund- ance. A good start is a plate of oatmeal por- ridge, eaten in the English fashion with fresh milk and a little sugar or honey. Honey is an excellent thing on the breakfast table. A touch of it destroys any tartness u iiich an apple not quite of the best may possess. Doine- made wheatmeal bread, with fresh dairy butter, is delightful in itself; but eaten with ripe pears or apples, and a sprinkling of Brazil nuts or walnuts, the lima' to the healthy novitiate(' appetite becomes a feast. Very little beverage is required at a breakfast of this character, but cocoa, coffee or tea may be partaken cf—the preference for these being in the order above- named. An active man, after a breakfast on these lines, can work well for five or six hours before partaking of his next meal. One feature attaching to meals on such simple, natural lines is that they are really enjoyable.—,T. Nayler, in "Farm and Home."

Lighten your work for Xmas. when preparing your Puddings and Mincemeat. Buy Shredded ATORA Beef Suet, it is guaran- teed absolutely pure, goes further and keeps for months. Your Grocer sells it. Insist upon ATORA, refuse substituted brands.

THE BLUE JACKAL.

In India, three thousand years ago, the story was told of a jackal, who, prowling round a town one night, fell into an indigo-tank, and came out dyed blue. Then the thought came to him: "No one will know me now that I am this splendid colour; so I will pretend that I am king of all the beasts." He began by ruling over the jackals, and then the lions and the tigers submitted to him. This made him proud and insolent, and he no longer took any notice of his old jackal friends. They therefore determined to expose him to the other beasts, and put an end to his reign. One might, they gathered round the self-made king and began to bowl; and as soon as the blue jackal beard all the others yelling, his natural instinct led him to do the same. Then at once all the other creatures in the jungle knew him to be nothing better than a jackal; and he lost for ever his crown and his authority. Silence is sometimes golden.

"WHEN DISJOINTED."

A foreigner, who did not understand the/Eng- lish language, opened a furniture shop in Lon- don. Amongst the stock was a collection of articles which, when unscrewed and re-arranged, could be used in many ways. One of the sec- tions be labelled: " Fireside easy-chair for a gentleman when disjointed !" Perhaps he meant that it was a haven of rest for a man dreadfully cut up, eh? Anyhow, it reminds me of a Venetian print- seller, who had a large frame filled with col- oured prints; and who, feeling that they had more advantages than could be seen from a careless glance, affixed a large label, which said : "These oil-colour printed images or pictures possess • the following advantages—that the colour never comes off, that they never accept or receive vermins or insects, that they may be washed or cleansed."

This, again, calls to mind the Copenhagener— a furrier—who exhibited "a lady-collar of greenlands-birds," and also what, for want of a "h," he described as an "earth-rug." Next week I will tell you some more about "English as she is wrote."

THE PASSING OF AUTUMN. (BY JESSIE Simi.) Deep on the ground the leaves are lying; Low o'er the land the breeze is sighing: Slowly brave Autumn now is dying. Chirps now yon bird no longer eheerly— " Stay yet awhile; we love thee dearly !" But it is gone. Life lingers drearly.

Moon! thou art lost in mists of sadness: Shed thy soft rays in summer gladness,: Spin yet round heaven in merry madness!"

But from our view its way is wended. 'Gainst Winter harsh, Earth is defended. Autumn's frail life has gently ended.

C.L.B. AND THE WAR. Over 7,000 Past and Present Members With the Colours.

It is estimated that not less than 7,000 of the Church Lads' Brigade boys and old comrades have responded to the appeal of their Governor and Commandant, Field Marshal Lord Grenfeil, P.C., G.C.8., G.C.M.G., and the authorities have successfully formed a complete battalion in the new army of C.L.B. old comrades, and it is pleasing to find that the Liverpool Regiment of Cadets, of which the Warrington companies form the 2nd Battalion, have nobly contributed their quota to the colours, for many are serv- ing in all branches of the naval and military forces, the effectiveness of their cadet training being speedily recognised and appreciated.

VACANCIES IN THE ST. PAUL'S COMPANY The very strong and successful Company of St. Paul's Church have their weekly parades, and meet at the Kendrick-street Schools at eight o'clock every Thursday night. The Chaplain and the officers of the company in attendance are always pleased to welcome any boys desirous of enrolment in the company. New squads are being formed and boys now joining will receive the full course of physical drill and training. The Bugle Band of this company is the band of the Warrington Battalion, and their services in the absence of other military bands have been given to the Territorials and the Training Corps, accompanying them on their 'marches. .Consequent upon so many of the old band boys having joined the colours, there are vacancies in the band at the ptesent time, and names will be accepted of boys willing to undertake a course of musical instruction. OF INTEREST TO PARENTS AND GVARDIANS. Parents and guardians might note that in connection with this company there is a good social club and savings bank, controlled and governed by the boys themselves; also that the aims of the Church Lads' Brigade are not only to teach the members the arts of physical cul- ture and drill, but to endeavour to follow out the object of the founders, viz., the advance- ment of Christ's kingdom among lads of all classes, and the promotion of charity, reverence, patriotism, discipline and self-respect, and all that tends towards true Christian manliness. Three of the officers of the company—the Chap- lain (Rev. W. Braceeamp), Capt. L. S. lien- shall and Lieut. L. Henshall—who are at pres- ent serving with their regiment, the 4th South Lancashires, have expressed their wish that all the members of the company should rally round and by every means help and encourage their present acting officers, Lieut. IPCEMBER 5, 1914.

POTATO AND ROOT CROPS. Heavier All-round Yields. _ _ _

The preliminary report of the Board of Agri- culture sliowing the estimated total produce and yield per acre of the potato and root crops in England and Wales for the present year has been issued this week. The total production of potatoes ginounts to 2,955,299 tons, or some 60,000 tons mom, than hist, year; while the yield per acre, although slightly less than in 1913, is about ton above the aver- age of the last ten years. Turnips and swedes have given a yield considerably better than in 1913, but rather below the ten-year average. The yield of mangels, on the other hand, is only slightly above that of 1913, and a ton below the average. Both kinds of roots are, speaking gen- erally, above the average in most of the western counties, and below the mean, sometimes con- siderably so, in the eastern half of England. The total production of turnips, swedes and mangels in England and Wales is some 960,000 tons greater than last year. The average yields in the divisiOna in which Lancashire and Cheshire are classified are esti- mated as follows : Potatoes : 1914, 7.43 tons per acre; 1913, 7.52; ten years, 7.23. • Turnips and swedes: 1914, 16.25 tons to the acre; 1913, 18.34; ten years, 17.49. Mangels : 1914, 22.52 tons to the acre; 1913, 20.37; ten years, 21.52. Thus potatoes were slightly worse than last year, but slightly better than the ten-year aver- age; turnips and swedes were decidedly worse than both last year and Cie-MT.-year average; while mangels were substantially better than last year or the ten-year average.

THE DEMAND FOR CHEESE. Further evidence of the effect which the rapid rise in the price of Cheshire cheese has had upon the depletion of the stocks on the farms was forthcoming at the annual show of the Nantwich and District Dairy Farmers' Association. The total entry of cheese and butter was 232, as com- pared with 399 at last year's exhibition, while there was a decrease in the pitch of nearly 40 tons. The fact that the prize-money bad been considerably reduced doubtless furnished a reason why some very well-known makers abstained from exhibiting, but the main cause of the smaller display was that farmers have to a very great extent disposed of the summer-made cheese, which under more normal circumstances they would have retained for exhibition pur- poses.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
SHEEP ON GRASS.

There has been a lot of controversy of late as to why sheep are steadily diminishing in num- bers of late years. There is no doubt that to a certain extent it is due to the steady decrease of arable land, for sheep and arable go together, and also to some extent to the practice which obtains on .a good many mixed farms of extend- ing the dairy at the expense of the sheep. Fif- teen to twenty years ago farmers were quite jus- tified in doing so, for then it was only by substi- tuting something more profitable than sheep and corn that many farmers were able to go on nt all. Times have changed, and, in my opinion, at the present time, when a consider- able portion of the farm has been retained as arable, and that arable of a medium or light soil, a flock of sheep, looking at it all round, would be more profitable than working it all as dairy farming. The touchstone of successful farming in this country is to maintain your land in the highest possible state of fertility, and it is not possible to maintain any considerable quantity of arable land—say a third or half of a farm—without sheep. Theorists tell us that the most profitable way to run a dairy farm is to keep a considerable portion under the plough, and then one can keep a larger head of stock. This may be so in expert hands, but in the general run of this sort of farming the plough land absorbs all the farmyard manure, and there i is not half enough at that. The grass land s starved, and the arable gets so full of weeds that it will grow nothing else, and the end of it is that this sort of farm generally carries less stock than any. In fact, if you want to impoverish ii farm in the quickest possible way, work it as a ' mixed holding, devote it all to dairying, and sell the milk. In running sheep on an all-grass farm it is well to get about it carefully. To stock a grass farm heavily and entirely with sheep is to court disaster; yet a good many farmers might profitably run a lot more sheep than they do on grass land, for nothing main- tains the fertility of the land like sheep.— "Somerset Farmer" in "Farm and Home."

LATE APPLES.

It is many years since such an abundant crop of apples has been produced in this country, but I fear that many of the late varieties will be wasted, as there is very little attention given to storing these, the alleged reason being that it does not pay. WHY WARRINGTON WON!

The Warrington ,forwards treated the on- lookers to a smart display, which was all the more creditable by reason of the fact that—in the persons of FairhurSt and Harmer—they had two fresh men among their number. Dainteth and Young were most reliable at half-back, and Taberner, Tranter and Brookes played soundly on the thr4-quarter line. Jolley was his old self, and if he did occasionally make a trifling slip,, he soon wiped out the memory of it by contributing something tof exceeding brilliance. The St. Helens forwards were foemen worthy of the steel of the home front rank, but their threequarters were somewhat lacking, and ought to be thankful that they had such a sound man behind them as Molyneux, especially in the later stages.

MANCHESTER AND DISTRICT

LEAGUE.

RYLANDS' RECS. v. BRADFORD AND CLAYTON.

This match, in the First Division of the Man- chester and District League, was played at Bradford on Saturday, before a good crowd.' Rylands' won the toss and elected to play against the wind. Smith kicked off for Bradford, Brand- wood returning. Give-and-take play was the order, both full-backs having plenty of work, which they did well. Good play by Turner took the game to the visitors' goal, but they kept their lines intact. Rylands' at last took the offensive and stormed the defenders, Povey and Horrocks both being tackled on the line. One of the homesters, being tackled, attempted to pass, and Potter, intercepting, scored, Warren failing at goal. Half-time arrived with the score: Rylands', 1 try (3 points); Bradford and Clayton, nil. Whittaker commenced the second half for Rylands', who at once made matters warm for the home team. Playing well, their forwards gave Warren and Gallop good opportunities to feed their backs, and presently Povey scored a second try for Rylands', the goal kick failing. Later on Povey passed to Griffiths, and that player, going inside, set the whole of the team in motion, the outcome being a glorious try by Whittaker, which Povey failed to convert. The home team tried hard to lessen the score against them, but they could not break down the de- fence, and the final score read: Rylands', 3 tries (9 points); Bradford and Clayton, nil. Rylands', on the whole, played a grand game, Horrocks, Povey and 'Griffiths being most in the limelight. Of the home' team, Flanagan, Turner and Smith were. the pick.

TEAMS FOR TO-DAY.

WARRINGTON v. SWINTON.

Swinton will be the visitors at Wilderspool to-day, when all the auguries point to an inter- esting game. This will be the first meeting be- tween the teams this season. Last year the clubs divided the honours, Swinton beating Warrington early on and Warrington retaliat- ing towards the close of the season by adminis- tering a nine-point defeat. To-day's teams will line out as follows : Warrington. 1 Jollety. 4 Taberner, 3 Brookes, 2 '['ranter, 5 Bradshaw. 6 Dainteth, 7 Young. 8 Cox, 9 Clare, 10 Cunliffe, 12 Critchley, 13 Fairhurst, 11 Algther.

A DATE RE-ARRANGED.

At their meeting in Manchester on Tuesday night, the Northern Union Committee fixed March 29th as the date of the match with Wid- nes, that should have been played on the 21st ult., but had to be postponed on account of the fog. The match will be played away.

WAR CONDITIONS.

WARRINGTON PLAYERS' SPLENDID LEAD.

Agree to Voluntary Reduction of Wages.

PERFECT HARMONY AT WILDERSPOOL. We are able to announce that, as arranged at the commencement of the season, the com- mittee and players met after the match with St. Helens on Saturday to consider the posi- tion of the club, with a view to a revision of the playing terms. The financial aspect and the stand the com- mittee had taken in opposing a compulsory re- duction were explained, and the players were asked to assist the committee.

At the meeting on Wednesday, the Cap- tain of the Ist team (E. Brookes) announced that the players recognised the position of the club and the efforts made by the committee, and that they had unanimously agreed to a voluntary reduction in their playing terms of 15 per cent. The action is a commendable one, and shows the mutual agreement c‘xisting between the com- mittee and the players, and the sacrifice made is an example to other players in the Northern Union.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
£l,OOO FOR_N_ECESSITOUS CLUBS.

At a meeting of the Northern Union Commit- tee held in Manchester on Tuesday night it was decided to grant £l,OOO to the League Relief Fund for necessitous clubs.

The committee regretted that the League clubs had decided to make the fees and expenses of referees and touch-judges and the railway fares of visiting clubs a charge upon the relief funds, and desired the League Committee to ask the clubs to meet these expenses themselves, as the charge will amount to a larger aggregate sum than they provided by their voluntary tax upon gates.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
AN APPEAL TO PLAYERS TO ENLIST.

In -vie; of the great need of recruits for the Army, it was decided, at a meeting of the Nor- thern Union Committee, held in Manchester on Tuesday night, to ask the League Clubs Com- mittee to make an appeal to their players to volunteer for service. The committee consid- ered that the clubs would earn greater honour by the enlistment of a large proportion of their players than in the winning of the football championship during the period of the country's need. "THE OLD FLAG."

"Old Journalist" 'writes:— The Mayor's reference to this, in replying to the Town Council's vote of thanks, sent me afresh to a congenial subject on which I wrote briefly not long ago. I find that our Union Jack took its present form so comparatively recently as 1801. Why " Jack "? It has been suggested that the derivation is from King James (Jacques), under whose auspices it sprang into existence, or alternatively from the jacque or surcoat, with the St. George red cross, anciently worn by English soldiers (hence our modern jacket). However, an authority upon whom I thing reliance may preferably be placed points out that in 1606, three years after the Union of the Crowns of England and Scotland, the King's ships had then, as now, for their principal flag the ensign, but they also had, as now, a minia- ture Union flag as a jack—i.e., a flag displayed from a staff at the jack-staff end of the bowsprit. This is probably how the name has come to be applied to the larger as well as the small flag. The first time the term "Union Jack" was offi- ciallf used was in a proclamation issued by Queen Anne on July 28, 1707, which required that merchant vessels should fly a red flag " with a Union Jack described in a canton at the upper corner thereof, next the staff," while the Union flag, as before, was reserved for the Royal Navy.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
League Leaders Strengthen Their Position.

===== 0002601_19141205_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITE STAR AGAIN BEAT itoLura GREEN.

Owing to the compulsory postponement of the games in the local League, consequent upon the fog which enwrapped the district on the 21st ult., the players on Saturday were eager for the fray, and in several instances some very keen games were witnessed, the most note- worthy being that at Orford-road between the leaders of the League:—Fairfield United and Paddington, the latter of whom were the visit- ors. It is well known that very keen rivalry exists between these clubs, and, owing to toe tlepleted strength of the United, the Padding- ton lads fancied their chances very much, and the way they played during the greater por- tion of the time occasioned much excitement among spectators and players alike. But dur- ing the whole game the ccfence proved superior to the attack. The United gave the more pol- ished exhibition, and by a piece of good com- bination and a grand centre by Cobb, Millward managed to score the only goal of the match just before the interval. Early in the second half the visitors had their best chance of equal- ising, being awarded a penalty kick, but, although the kick was well taken by Derby- shire, it lacked fire, and Potter saved at the expense of a corner. The rest of the game was vigorously contested, and the visitors were more robust in their methods, but Woodhouse and Bebbington defended very stubbornly, and kept their citadel intact. Thus the United made their position more secure at the head of the chart.

The White Star had Hollins Green as visit- ors, and although they won by two clear goals, they had not much advantage over their oppo- nents in the run of the game. Ellams, the visitors' goalkeeper, brought off many smart saves, and was ably supported by Clarke and Sowerbutts. The strength of the home team rested with their half-backs; who worked very hard the whole of the time. Maddock proved himself the most dangerous forward, and it was chiefly owing to his splendid display that the visitors' defence was so severely tested. The White Star team have now extracted four points from the Greenites.

Owing to various causes—enlistment in the ranks of Kitchener's Army being the chief—the Fairfield A.F.C. had some trouble to raise a team against the Central; and, under the cir- cumstances, they put up a strong defence. When a team with five reserve players only loses by four goals to two it may be considered a creditable performance. The Central players gave a very even display, Williams and Hands being most prominent among them, whilst Law- less and Kinnister were the pick of the visitors.

The game between St. Barnabas' and Long- ford United, at Lovely-lane, was very keenly contested, there being a very strong rivalry be- tween the clubs. On the run of the game the United did not deserve to he defeated, as they wer• a shade the better team, but dallying in front of goal instead of shooting lost them points. Bennett, Smart and Sherratt were very prominent for them, Smart shining specially during the latter stages of the game, when he appeared to hold the opposing forwards in submission by himself. Barratt, Darling- ton and Long showed up best for the Saints.

The Latchford Baptists disappointed again, this time on their own ground. Only six of their team put in an appearance. The Recs. are to be sympathised with upon being unable to fulfil the fixture after making the journey.

The match on the Penlceth Recreation Ground between Great Sankey and Calder Mersey was a very keenly contested one. The visitors, who at present head the League table, came with a good record, and there was a large crowd to receive them, but as the game proceeded and it was evident to the visitors that they were going to suffer defeat from their more experienced opponents, they resorted to a style of play such as would not be creditable to any team. If they continue to give such exhibitions the Alli- ance Committee will have to deal strongly with them!

The match at Altrincham on Saturday be- tween the Stockton Heath and Altrincham St. George's Clubs came to a very unsatisfactory conclusion, from the Heathens' point of view. Owing to the late start the game could not be played for the same length of time in the second portion as in the first, and a dispute arose as to whether a shot that was sent in scored or not. At the time the score was 2-1 against the Heathens, and owing •to darkness the referee could not tell definitely whether the ball had gone between the uprights or not, and when the visitors claimed .a goal the referee stopped the game and would not give a decision. The visit- ors admit that the game ought to have been stopped fully ten minutes earlier owing to darkness, but as the claim for a goal had been made the referee should have given a decision either one way or the other.

ford, Denny and Caldwell; Maddock, Meachin, Stubbs, Dunbobbin and Hankinson.

The referee was Mr. B. Powell. The visitors won the toss and Stubbs kicked off for the home team. Following the opening exchanges Meachin, with a neat pass, set Mad- dock going on the right, and from his centre, Stubbs came within an ace of opening the score. The visitors then retaliated with vigour, and with grand combination, Mellor, Billington and Perrin outwitted the home backs, but Miller made a grand clearance. Denny, receiving the ball, transferred to Maddock, who raced away on the wing and centred splendidly, but the chance was lost owing to Stubbs fouling Ellams, the visitors' goalkeeper. The free kick brought temporary relief to the visitors, but the home half-backs were playing a strong game and plied their forwards with well-timed passes. Sowerbutts and Clarke were defending stub- bornly, and Maddock on the home right was playing a very fine game. Sowerbutts had the misfortune to head into his own goal, the ball glancing through off the upright. It was by this goal that the White Star led at half-time. On the resumption the homesters set to work in vigorous fashion and attacked the visitors' citadel very strongly. Cleworth cleared from amongst a crowd of players, but the visitors' goal was soon placed in danger again, as the re- sult of good play on the part of Tlfinkinson -and Dunbobbin.. Ellams, however, proved equal to his task, and saved on several occasions. Fol- lowing several determined efforts by the visit- ors to get on equal terms, Caldwell, from a free kick, sent the leather towards the visitors' goal, and Stubbs, fastening on to it, completely beat Ellams, whilst a little later Maddock, with a fine effort, hit the upright. When time was called the score was: Orford White Star, 2 goals; Hollins Green, 0. FAIRFIELD UNITED v. PAMINGTON. The above teams met on Saturday on the Orford-road ground, in the presence of a good crowd of supporters. The following players turned out:—

Fairfield United: Potter; Woodhouse and Bebbington; Gleave, Lewis and Watkin; S. Woodhouse, Hunter, Millward, Scott and Cole. Paddington: Harrison; E. Holmes and Cham- berlain; W. Holmes, J. Taylor and Spencer; Garnett, S. Taylor, Woodhead, Derbyshire and Smith. The referee was Mr. J. Smith.

The home team, who started the game with only ten players, on the toss and defended the railway end. It was soon evident that the game was to be keenly contested, and the visit- ors, quickly getting into their stride, wero soon making raids into the home territory, only to be repulsed by good defensive play on the part of Woodhouse and Bebbington. A free kick against the visitors relieved the home defence, but their forwards did not make much use of the opportunities afforded them, and were easily overcome by Holmes and Chamberlain. A free kick, given against Lewis, placed the home goal in danger, but Bebbington and Woodhouse re- lieved in turn. For a while the game was strenuously contested in mid-field, and then Cole, on the extreme left, received a well- judged pass, and, making tracks for the visitors' end centred well in front of goal. Millward seized the opportunity and placed the ball well out of Harrison's reach, thus scoring the only goal of the match. Immediately afterwards half- time was called, the homesters leading by one goal to nil.

The teams having crossed over, the game con- tinued to be as keenly fought as in the first half, and the backs at both ends had to bestir themselves to clear. In the visitors' ranks, W. Spencer and Chamberlain changed places, and an improvement in their attack was quickly noticeable. The home backs were hard pressed, when Bebbington was penalised for a foul on Garnett. Derbyshire was entrusted wifli the kick, which was well placed, but lacked speed, and Potter brought off a good save at the ex- pense of a corner. As time was drawing near the visitors made poor use of several good open- ings, whilst the homesters were sent back for infringing the offside rule. Result: Fairfield United, 1 goal; Paddington, 0.

WARRINGTON CENTRAL V. FAIRFIELD ATHLETIC. These teams opposed each other in Victoria Park on Saturday, the following players turn- ing out : Central: Williams; Glover and Ashcroft; Emson, Brown and Goulden; Mason, Hands. Farrell, Williams and Longshaw. Fairfield Athletic: Bolton; Johnson and Roberts; Howard, Challinor and A. Roberts; ifigham, Lawless, Kinnister, Short and Bates. The. referee was Mr. W. Pet:nett. Owing to various causes the visitors had some difficulty in raising a team, and at the time for kicking off they could only muster six of the selected players. However, their team was made up by calling upon five of their sup- porters. The Central won the toss and Kin- nister set the ball rolling. The game opened at a fast pace, and was very interesting to' watch. The visitors' •scratch team not only '•held their own for the greater portion of the initial half, but opened the scoring after seven minutes' play, Lawless doing the needful, and the point being gained whilst Ashcroft was off the field receiving attention to an injury. A little later Williams equalised for the home- sters. Half-time arrived with the score 1-1. On resuming, the Central at once became ag- gressive, and a stinging shot was sent in from the left. Bolton managed to touch the ball, but failed to intercept it, and the Central thus gained the lead. The Fairfield lads tried hard to equalise, and for a while their forwards kept up a strong attack on the home goal, Chal- linor, at centre-half, rendering good service. The home defenders, however, managed to keep their goal intact, although it had several nar- row escapes. Gradually the home players forced the visitors back, and Hands scored a third goal for them. This was immediately fol- lowed by a brilliant individual effort by Mason, who registered a fourth point. The visitors pulled themselves together for another effort, and Kinnister reduced ;Ale ,ead. During the concluding stages the liairlieid goal vas sub- jected to a fierce attack, the homesters gaining a couple of corners in quick succeggiln, but failing to improve upon them. Re:ult : Cen- tral, 4 goals; Fairfield Athletic, 2 goals.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

ST. BARNABAS' v. LONGFORD UNITED.

The above teams met on Saturday on the Lovely-lane ground, which, owing to the recent heavy rain, was in a very heavy condition. The following players turned out under the control of Referee M. J. Kite:— St. Barnabas' : Davenport; Jefferson and Long; O'Neil, Challinor and Lawton; Milling- ton, Darlington, Barratt, Sherrington and Ray. Longford: Sturgess; Stevens and Mason; 4.1.•'.1x, Smart and Fearnley ; Richards, Hands, Ben- nett, Sherratt and Hughes. _ „

The home captain won the toss, and Bennett 'kicked off for the visitors, and the United at once made tracks for the home goal, Sherratt being smartly pulled up by Long. who returned the leather with a hefty kick. Darlington and Millington transferred play to the visitors' end, the latter centring, and Barratt missing by inches with a good attempt. Davenport made a smart save from a good shot from Sher- ratt, after which end-tend play followed, both teams being well balanced and playing a strenuous game. Both also gained several corners, but failed to improve upon them. Bennett, the visitors' centre-forward, tried hard to break through on his own, hut was smartly robbed of the ball by Jefferson. A good dis- play of combination by the home forwards ended in Barratt scoring the first goal. The interval arrived with the score 1-0 in favour of the Saints. On resuming, the visitors were seen to better advantage, but they failed to utilise several good openings when in front of goal. Consider- ing the heavy state of the ground, some good ! play was shown by both teams. From the _centre kick Bennett, Sherratt and Hughes made ! desperate efforts to equalise, but, their shots either went just wide of the posts or straight into Davenport's hands. From a goal kick, Challinor intercepted the ball and passed out to Ray. From the latter's centre, Barratt sent in a dropping shot at thirty yards' range, which beat Sturgess, the hall passing between his hands and the cross-bar. Following this the visitors did all the pressing. Smart at centre- half being a tower of strength to them, holding up and intercepting the home forwards in mas- terly fashion. Bennett scored with a splendid shot, Davenport being completely beaten. The final score read: St. Barnabas', 2 goals; Long- , ' ford United, 1 goal.

WIDNES AND DISTRICT ALLIANCE.

GREAT SANKEY v. CALDER AND MERSEY These teams met on Saturday on the Penketh Recreation Ground, there 'being a good Crowd .present when the teams turned out under the control of Mr. S. C. Essenhigh. The visitors won the toss, and Hawthorn kicked off for the home team. Ditehfield, by good play, got past the visitors' backs, but his final shot was wide. Following a fruitless corner, the same player was only inches out with a fine shot. The homesters continued to have the best of the game, and after some good passing movements, Hawthorn scored a capital goal for them. Nettled by this reverse, the visitors rushed the leather towards the home citadel, and Brindle was conspicuous for some splendid saves between the uprights. After several free kicks, Poulter gained possession and sent in a long shot, which the visitors' goal- keeper mulled, the homesters' lead being thus increased. The visitors made a desperate attack on the home goal, but the defence proved to be very sound. A free kick for hands afforded the home defence relief, and the interval arrived with play in mid-field and the score 2-0 in favour of Great Sankey. _

Announcement has already been made to tulite, public with regard to the extended use Admiralty purpose to make of homing pigeer; for purposes of communication. The public 6t, particularly appealed to to refrain from abeo,ls ing or otherwise interfering with the bir" . whilst on passage. As it is possible, however' that birds may be shot, or injured, or ateB3(i; and be found by private persons, anyone lag a pigeon under such circumstances shoal" at once communicate with the police, liand,ilt to them any message found and also the be number of the bird. The pigeons used will .1, conveyed to various points in baskets by and it is hoped all will assist in seeing Ole.' are not interfered with in any way.

WOUNDED SOLDIER'S SOUVENIRS. One of the wounded Britishers, who is Irnscf convalescent after having spent the best Part: a month in the Warrington Infirmary, and w",° has now left the town to spend a short furlong!9l. at his home, prior to returning to the frant,4 the possessor of two souvenirs, with which, -al. declares, he would not part for "love ° money," One of the souvenirs is of a ratber grim description; it is the nickel bullet which sur. was extracted from his head by an ArwlY geon at the front. The other is an extraordiai arily pretty watch-chain of filigree silver wa,rd and enamel, which was given him by aWO he Prussian Guard, who fell into the hands of British after the fighting in the neighbourbou"e of Ypres. It is "Tommy's" intention to bac.s the bullet mounted as a pendant to wear the chain.

14,694 MEMBERS. Warrington Co-operative Society's Quarterly Meeting. The 216th quarterly meeting of the Warring- ton E.I. Co-operative Society, Ltd., s% as held in the Co-operative Hall on Monday. Mr. J. Upson presided, and was supported by the whole of the committee, whilst about 200 members were also present. Sales and Membership Increased. The report, which was submitted, showed that the sales far the thirteen weeks ended October 20th had reached £85,898, an increase of .68,003 ' over the corresponding petiod. The member- ship had increased by 612 in the three months, and now stood at 14,694. Prices As Usual for the Present. In moving the adoption of the report, the President drew attention to the action the society had taken in keeping down pries, with special reference to the fact that they had not advanced tea, and did not intend doing so whilst their stocks lasted. Owing to buying in ad- vance, they were also still able to retail bread at the same Rrice as before the war, and hoped to do so for some little time to come. Attention was also drawn by the President to the millinery and dressmaking departments, which have been placed under new manage- ment, and the support of the members was asked for these. Elections. Scrutineers and returning officer were ap- pointed for dealing with ,the voting papers for the election of committee. As there was only one nomination for Presi- dent, Mr. 'Upson was declared re-elected for another twelve months. The voting for the committee resulted in the return of Messrs. Allen, Sinclair and Rushton, the figures being as follows:—Allen, 129; Sin-

ALIENS' BRITISH-BORN WIVES. Relief by Guardians Whilst Husbands are Interned. At the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday a letter was read from the Local Government Board, in. which they ordered the relief of British-born 'wives of interned aliens, with instructions that -it was not to be rogarded as Poor Law relief. The Local Government Board had a fund at its disposal from which the money .would be re- funded. The Clerk explained that in the case of tlip wife of an interned German he had conferred with the Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley), and he had paid the relief from his 'office in Bewsey- street. In answer to questions, the Chairman said that they wanted to differentiate between the cases of aliens' wives and ordinary relief cases. The cases would be reported to the Guardians just as the Relieving Officers reported cases where they had given temporary relief. Mr. Flanagan, who mentioned a case in his district of a woman with four children, who had spent nearly all the £2O her husband had in the bank, was asked to report the full parti- culars to the Clerk.

LEATHER IS DEARER! Owing to the enormous quantity of Boots required for the Armies of the Allies, practically all of which have to be made in England, there is no possible doubt that Boots are certain to be MUCII DEARER. The Manufacturers demand an increase of 10 per cent. to 20 per cent., and, in certain kinds of Boots, up to even 30 per cent. Since the outbreak of War the price of LEATHER has risen by nearly one-half. It is evident that the Public will have to pay largely-increased prices, or accept an inferior article—one or the other. WE ARE, HOWEVER, IN TIIE FORTUNATE POSITION TO BE ABLE TO OFFER LARGE STOCKS OF BOOTS, SIIOES AND SLIPPERS AT PRICES RULING BEFORE THE WAR. We strongly advise our friends to take advantage of these Stocks, which represent the best possible value. R. ROBERTS & SON 19, BRIDGE STREET.

FARM 7-ND GARDEN. fortunately,one other reason that keeps down the prices is the fact that many apples, whose proper season is Christmas and the early months of the New Year are being now sent into the market. Were these late apples to be stored carefully and held over until the proper season, then better prices would be realised, and we should not hear of the glut of apples in the market and the poor returns made to growers for their pro- duce. GLADIOLI. Last spring, noticing that many of the corms of G. brenchleyensis that had been accidentally left in the ground were quite plump and good and flowered well during the past summer, we this year as an experiment are leaving several lots in the ground merely covering thenr during the coming winter with some well rotted manure. We find that many of the corms when lifted, dried and stored, are ()lore or less shriv- elled in the following spring, and Start away very badly, many of them refusing to grow at all. Of course, those who grow the corms for sale must lift them, but those who grow them for their effect in the gaiaert when in flower might well try the experiment of leaving the corms in the ground and note the result. FRUIT TREES.—.. The roots of these when received from a dis- tance, however carefully packed, are almost sure to be more or less dry. They should immedi- ately on arrival be stood in a water tank for an hour or two so as to thoroughly moisten the roots. As soon as they have partially dried, after having been removed from the water, all the roots should be gone carefully over, remov- ing with a sharp knife all rough and jagged ends. at the same time paring any bruises about the branches. PRUNING CLEMATIS JACKMANNI. All too often as one comes across this clematis it will be found that the blooms are all in a mass on the top of the plant, the lower part being quite flowerless. This is, entirely due to the way in which the pruning is done. This clematis belongs to the set which flowers on the young wood made during the current season, hence it should be cut down yearly during the present month to within a foot of the ground, giving at the same time a good mulch of rotten manure. In this way fresh young wood with fine foliage from the bottom will result, and when flowering time comes round the blooms will be much finer in every way than those on the neglected plants referred to.—" Wick," in " Farm and Home." 4 GERMAN VANDALS. An Officer's Confession to His Diary. In a diary found on the dead body of a Ger- man officer the following entries occurred : September 3.—Still at Rethel, on guard over prisoners. The houses are charming inside. The middle class in France has magnificent furni- ture. We found stylish pieces everywhere and beautiful silk, but in what a state! Good God!' Every bit of furniture broken, mirrors smashed. The Vandals themselves could not have done more damage. This place is a disgrace to our army. The inhabitanta who fled could not have expected, of course, that all their goods would have been left in full after so many troops had passed; but the column commanders are re- sponsible for the greater part of the damage, as ' they could have prevented the looting and de- I struction. The damage amounts to millions of marks; even the safes have been attacked. In a solicitor's house, in which, as luck would have it, everything was in excellent taste, including a collection of old lace and Eastern works of art, everything was smashed to bits. I couldn't resist taking a little memento 'my- self here and there. . . • One house was par- ticularly elegant, everything in the best taste. The hall was of light oak; near the staircase I found a splendid aquascuturn and a camera for Felix. A Grave in France. September 22.—Troops of the 18th Corps passed all day. Apparently they are to come up on the right wing. Their infantry has lost as heavily as ours. God knows what the idea of this rein- forcement is. lam convinced that this country will give us all a grave. September 24.—1 took leave of my hosts rind went back to the trenches at Ville aux Bois. September 25.—A furious artillery duel. Pass Was Verified. The smart Cockney tourist thought lie would have a joke with the gendarme and handed him the restaurant menu instead of his passport, while his friends stood by to laugh at the puzzled " Froggie." But the Frenchman took the document with grave politeness, glancing keenly at the Cockney as ff to verify the description. Then he read and translated slowly from the menu : " Calf's head," " pigs feet," "pass on, m'sieu—quite correct."

FAIRFIELD UNITED STILL ON TOP.

was evenly contested. A free kick 'against Roberts enabled the visitors to get to the home area. There they were met by a stubborn de- fence, and were gradually driven back. At this juncture Ditchfield had the misfortune to receive a bad kick, and had to leave the field. The visitors began to exhibit a display of bad temper, and were penalised several times, whilst several of their players received cautions from the referee. • The homesters worked the ball down to the visitors' goal, and from a mis-kick by one of the latter's backs, Poulter had not muck difficulty in registering a third goal. From the centre kick the visitors again made an at- tack on the home citadel, but Brindle would not yield. Off-side spoiled several other attempts. Result: Great Sankey, 3 goals; Calder and Mer- sey, 0. • ITEMS. The White Star have gained four points at the expense of Hollins Green. Ellams, the Greenites' goalkeeper, was in good form on Saturday. Sowerbutts and Clarke played good defensive football. Maddock was the most dangerous forward on the field, and he gave his best display of the season. Caldwell, Denny and Gifford are a very strong half-back line. Paddington failed to lower the colours of the United. Millward got his usual goal, and it proved sufficient to win a couple of points for the United. Derbyshire's penalty kick lacked speed, and Potter fell full length in making the clearance. Woodhouse was the best back on the field. Bebbington and Woodhouse were a better pair than Holmes and Chamberlain. Mason scored with one of the best efforts ever seen in the Park. Challinor played a great game at centre-half for Fairfield. Fairfield were handicapped by having to play five reserves. Longford came a cropper on the Lovely-lane ground. Bennett scored for the United with the best shot of the game. Smart was very smart hdeed at centre-half, and Sherratt was a hard worker. The Saints were very lucky to capture both points. Long was strong in defence. Jefferson proved a good substitute for Glover, and Darlington did some smart things amongst the forwards. Barratt scored both goals. Sturgess certainly ought to have saved the second. Latchford Baptists could not raise a team on their own ground. It was a disappointment fez--; the Recs. The Earlestown and Newton clubs had a rest last week. Great Sankey defeated the Alliance leaders by 3-0 in a game which was marred by an acci- dent to Ditchfield. Berresford, of Newton Cons., is the first War- rington League player to be "black-listed." He got six weeks' suspension Tor gllsconduct during the match with the Recs. Rawlinson was let off, and Fairhurll escaped with a caution. Chesters missed another penalty kick for the Heathens last week. LATCHFORD BAPTISTS AGAIN DEFAULT. Latchford Baptists and Bank Quay Recs. should have met on the ground of the former on Saturday, but the match had to be put off owing to the home club being unable to raise a team. Only six of the players turned up at the dressing-room. It is time this state of affairs was put an end to by the Baptists' Com- mittee, as this is the third consecutive fixture they have failed to fulfil. Probably the League Committee will have something to say at their next meeting! STARKEY CUP COMPETITION. To Be Carried On If Enough Clubs Enter. At the monthly meeting of the Warrington and District League, held this week, the matter of the Starkey Cup Competition was discussed. It was decided to carry it on again, provided sufficient clubs express their desire to enter. The Secretary (Mr. R. G. Brandreth) will supply all information in response to inquiries, and clubs intending to enter should do so on or before the last day of the present year. LATCHFORD BAPTISTS CENSURED AND FINED. The monthly meeting of the Warrington and District League was held on Wednesday night at the Lion Hotel, Mr. P. Booth presiding over a good attendance of club representatives. It was decided that the Latchford Baptists' Club be censured for not fulfilling fixtures, and that they be fined in three instances. Several other clubs were also fined for not complying with League rules. Mr. Bate, of Hollins Green, was nominated for a seat on the sub-committee, in place of Mr. Clarke. MATCHES FOR TO-DAY. at once threatened to fire, he surrendered, and the whole of his men were made prisoners with- out a shot being fired. A fleet of motor cars were some miles away, unable to penetrate the part of the country where. the capture was actually effected. When Colonel Jordaan and his men reached the cars the rebels were imme- diately hurried to the nearest town. BOTHA FREE TO ATTEND TO GERMANS. Maritz, the first traitor, went over to the German enemy during the first weeks of the war, and late in October De Wet, Beyers, Kemp, and others followed his example by tak- ing up arms. From the first the rising had no chance of success. General Botha attacked Beyers promptly and routed Ile commando; next he beat Be Wet soundly. Since then the troops have been busy receiving surrenders and taking prisoners. The most serious feature of the little rebel- lion has been that it has delayed the departure of General Botha to meet our real enemies, the Germans. Before Be Wet was captured Botha had set out for German South-west Africa, to take command of the invading Union forces. He would have joined the army in the German colony a month ago had not the De Wet-Beyers rebellion kept him busy within the Union. That he has found it safe for him to leave the Trans- vaal and the Orange Free State shows the Boer danger to be over. Some scattered rebels re- main under arms, but they have lost their power for mischief. TURKEY. OUR PREPARATIONS IN EGYPT. The Turks are emulating the Germans by making the reports of victories take the place of victories. It is, however, stated that the com- mand of the Turkish forces destined to operate against Egypt has been given to Djemal Pasha, Minister of Marine. The "Times" correspondent at Cairo describes the impression made in Egypt by the passage of British and Indian troops through the Suez Canal under warship escort. The be- haviour of the troops has been exemplary, and the Moslem population has no desire to go back hon the security and prosperity of the British occt pation to the turbulence and tyranny that prevailed when the Turk was master in Egypt. AUSTRALASIANS ARRIVE. The Press Bureau announced on Thursday that the Australian and New Zealand contin- gents have disembarked in Egypt to assist in the defence of that country and to complete their training there. When the training is completed they will go direct to the front to , fight with other British troops in Europe. ' While they are in Egypt the Australasian troops may have the chance of practising mili- tary exercises against the Turks. Constanti- nople and Berlin have often announced the seizure of the Suez Canal, and Enver Pasha announces at intervals that an immediate con- quest is inevitable. In view of such circum- stances, says the "Manchester Guardian," it would be impolite to disbelieve altogether in the projected attack on Egypt, but Turkey is certainly strangely slow in showing that she can invade the country, or even seriously intends to make an attempt. ENTERIC. Enteric has broken out in the Belgian Army and patients are constantly arriving in France. Thanks to the prompt gift of .tlO,OOO by the British Red Cross Society, the responsible authorities have been able to take all desirable steps to fight the epidemic. WAR ITEMS. LATEST JOKE. Why do the Germans spell Culture with a K? Because the Allies have possession of all the C's. ASSISTANT PAYMASTER NIELD, Mr. Maurice C. Nield, son of the late Coun- cillor Nield, a purser on the Lusitania, has ob- tained a commission in the Navy and joined H.M.S. Vivid as assistant paymaster. DR. H. NIELD BETTER. Dr. H. Nield, another son of the late Coun- cillor Nield, who joined the R.A.M.C., has been seriously ill at the front, suffering from enteric fever. The latest news received in Warrington, we are glad to say, was to the effect that the crisis had passed, and there were now good hopes of recovery. REPORTED TO BE A PRISONER. Captain Colville, who a few years ago was stationed at Orford Barracks, and resided' at 'Latchford, has been unofficially reported to be a prisoner of war, after having, a short time ago, .been reported. as wounded and missing. NOT QUITE WHAT WAS MEANT. A message accompanying a Christmas pudding sent by a thoughtful soul to the collection which 'is to be forwarde,d from Orford to the " Tommies " in the trenches read as follows "God bless the soldier who eats this pudding, and bring him safely home again !" 1 PLUM PUDDINGS FOR "TOMMY." The response to the appeal made in Orford parish for Christmas puddings for British soldiers at the front has, we learn, met with a , gratifying response, 136 toothsome specimens of the Yuletide dainty having been contributed and forwarded to the proper quarter by Captain Crane, who kindly undertook the packing, &e. IKindly and cheery messages either in. verse or :prose accompanied many of the puddings. WAR CAUSES STAFF CHANGES AT THE INFIRMARY. ' Dr. Law, senior house surgeon at the Infirm. ary, having joined the Reserve Territorial Bat- talion ofi the South Lancashire Regiment, Dr. Forster has been appointed in his place tempo- rarily. Dr. Maurice S. Bryce has taken the place of Dr. Carson, junior house surgeon, who has accepted a commission in the Army. . MENTIONED IN DISPATCHES. Writing to a friend, Driver Thomas Potter, of the A.S.C., whose home address is 14, Clare- street, Warrington, says: "I am very pleased to see in the honours list the name of an old sergeant of mine—Sergeant-Major Roberts, of the South Lancashire Regiment—who has been mentioned in dispatches for bravery on the battlefield, and also that of Major Grech, the medical officer at Orford Barracks." ANOTHER COTTAGE HOMES' "OLD BOY" DOING WELL IN THE ARMY. Corporal John Molyneaux, formerly of the Padgate Cottage Homes and now of the 6th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, writes from Tidworth thanking the Warrington Board of Guardians for their good wishes, and stating that he is well and has every comfort, although things are rough and ready. He has been re- commended for promotion to sergeant. He is now the Quartermaster's clerk. WORKING MEN'S COMMITTEE RECRUITS. At a meeting of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee, held on Wednesday night, Mr. George Walker presiding, the following resolu- tion was passed on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. W. E. Stout : "That' the committee desire to express their appreciation of the patriotism of the members of the committee who have joined the Armed Forces of the country, and trust that they may return safely to the town on the completion of their duties." TRIBUTE TO LIEUTENANT-COLONEL GREEN'S MEMORY. A graceful tribute to the memory of Lieut.- Colonel Malcolm C. A. Green—who formerly held the command at Orford Barracks, and who, as was recorded in our last issue, was killed in action after only being three weeks at the front —is contained in the Christ Church Parish
